P·L ENARRATIONES IN CANTICA CANTICORUM 115

Patrologia Latina · Vol. 115 ·

ENARRATIONES IN CANTICA CANTICORUM

PL 115, coll. 551–628  ·  Latin from the Migne printing  ·  First English translation  ·  column numbers follow the original plates, not the Garnier reprint

Angelomus of Luxeuil

Angelomus Luxovensis

fl. c. 830s – 850s

Benedictine monk of Luxeuil Abbey in Burgundy under the Carolingian emperor Lothair I, to whom this commentary and his commentaries on Genesis and the Books of Kings are all dedicated. Little is known of his life outside these works; the dedication here recounts that Lothair himself, and after him Angelomus's own abbot Drogo (an illegitimate son of Charlemagne, and so Lothair's uncle), pressed him to undertake it.

PRAEFATIO. AD LOTHARIUM IMPERATOREM.

551BGloriosissimo praestantissimoque imperatori LOTHARIO semper Augusto, ANGELOMUS, ultimus Monachorum, exorans exorando, exorat nancisci perennem gloriam.

Nuper excubantem me in vestro sacro palatio, sub obtentu, inquam, traditionum liberalium artium, enucleationumque divinatum scilicet Scripturarum, dignata est vestra sagax prudentia, sicuti non est immemor, accersire vestrae solerti praesentiae, mecumque habere oraculum. Sed post spiritualia coelestis vitae colloquia, et suffragia orationum placuit imperando, imo intentando [ minitando ] imperare, ut styli officio juxta sensum antiquorum patrum, et frugalitatem ingenioli mei opusculum in Cantica canticorum Salomonis, ubi Christi et Ecclesiae conjunctio, 551Callegoriarumque mysteria prae caeteris Veteris Testamenti τοῖς βιβλοῖς contineri probantur, digererem et ut ita dicam exponere non abnuerem, atque praesentiae culminis vestri ostendere non omitterem, quo haberet crebro post dispositionem reipublicae frequentata, auribusque lectione relata solertia vestra, in contemplatione se extendens a tumultu causarum imperii atque potissimum de amissione sanctissimae conjugis Deo annuente, ut credimus, collegio sanctarum allectae, alboque beatitudinis inscriptae, consolationis gratia legendo theorica, et velut turtur singularis cujus natura est, ut physici ferunt, si semel conjugem casu perdiderit, alium non requirere, sed castimoniam servans vidua quodammodo permanere, solitarius ingemiscens, atque poenitens: de quo in 551Deodem libro legitur: « Vox, inquit, turturis audita est in terra nostra (Cant. II), » id est Christi dicentis: « Poenitentiam agite, appropinquabit enim regnum coelorum (Matth. III). » Et pulchrae sunt genae tuae ut turturis, imbre lacrymarum genarum rimulis infusis, preces pro vestris excessibus et absolutione piissimae imperatricis domino in abscondito fundere quivissetis. Quo audito, pondus tantae rei expavescens, attonitus, imo obstupefactus admirari coepi, cur clementia vestra tantum opus viris etiam eruditissimis formidandum, mihi indocto, omnesque liberalium artium disciplinas non nacto atque oraculis 552Beulogiisque divinarum Scripturarum imbecilli injungere libuisset, cum plures haberet in monarchia doctores qui honeste et catholice tractare potuissent. Sed censuram imperii vestri considerans, tacitoque pertractans illud apostolicum quo dicitur (I Petr. II): « Estote subjecti omni humanae creaturae propter Dominum, sive regi tanquam praecellenti, sive ducibus tanquam ab eo missis » , sed et potius illud Dominicum, ubi pollicetur: « Amen, inquit, dico vobis, quidquid orantes petitis, credite quia accipietis, et fiet vobis (Marc. II). » Animadvertens quoque interna trutinatione mentis, quod sine nutu divino non foret quod a tanta majestate imperaretur, temerario ore refragare nullo apologetico anfractuque excusationis praesumpsi, sed magis ut 552Cquiverim divinis fretus suffragiis, imperiis imperialibus, quanquam pavidus, obsecundare tentassem. Sed non flocci pendens quod sine magnifici domini, et abbatis mei scilicet Drogonis patrui vestri assertionibus, pariterque jussionibus ne regularem excedere viderer disciplinam, et stylum appellere ad exarandum debuissem, non solum quod Caroli Magni imperatoris filius, atque frater sanctissimi et piissimi genitoris vestri Ludovici Caesaris, sed quia summus erat pontifex atque abbas meus egregius. Cumque sacris ejus auribus suggerendo intulissem, et ordinem rei indicassem, quo auribus, hausto non solum renuit, verum ut est mitissimus et affabilitate suadibilis, benignitate praestantissimus, et ad omne bonum ferventissimus, 552Dmore suae modestiae non tantum ingenue libera auctoritate imperavit, sed blanda tranquillitate et pio affatu, ut angustale opus, et spirituale decus quomodo quivissem aggredi non abnuissem. Quod audiens, sic jam tandem ejus exhortationibus animatus, vestris, almificis, affaminibus, sanctisque jussionibus adactus potissimumque divinis auxiliis fretus sanctorumque doctorum orationibus provocatus, quod, o doctissime princeps, in doctorum penes nos sanctorum sparsim invenire potero, praesertim in beatissimi Papae Gregorii Romanae decoris eloquentiae dictis, quia a nullo solum per omnia reperio 553Acompactum atque ex traditione magistri ingenii mei conjectura stylo currente, commatico potius compendiosoque sermone, licet impolito aggredi tentabo, et in libelli modum finire conabor, ut habeat vestra celsitudo velut enchiridion, hoc est manualem quodammodo libellum excerptum, quem congruis horis lectitare Deo favente post studia imperii, post sollicitudines Ecclesiae ad consolationem divinarum ruminando complaceat. Et quia campos divinarum scilicet Scripturarum non valet percurrere latissimos, breviter saltem stromata nostra non pigeat scrutari quietus. Sed sciendum vero, quia ut moris est quorumdam scriptorum non in pagella e regione singulorum doctorum viritim litteris insignitis assignaverimus nomina omnia, sed ex eorum dictis profecto 553Bexpositorum nonnulla compaginare ex multimodis, breviter recidendo videlicet, demptis superfluis multimoda, nonnulla vero ex prolixioribus sensum eorum sequentes conjungere decerpendo, aliqua nostra interpolando, augmentare censuimus longiora. Sic nimirum sic diversis allusionibus dramatum percurrentibus brevia syrmata, quae vicissim nectendo sententiarum explere libellum sancivimus, non pertimescentes eos qui nasum veluti sapientes contorquent, nec eos qui promptiores ad reprehendendum quam ad imitandum, quique quod reprehendere subsannando moliuntur, adimplere nequeunt. Scimus namque scimus quod concentu organi fistulae aeris, aliae longiores sunt, aliae breviores, sed uno flatu follibus administrato, et docta manu imperante, 553Cdigitisque attrectando unam, cum sint diversae fistulae, harmoniam velut salpinx, tubarum voces atque concentum dulci cantilena melos afflando, attonitis auribus suaviter reddunt. Sic profecto cum sint aliae sententiae longiores recisis superfluis, aliaeque breviores additis necessariis, flante Spiritu sancto per corpus unum imperante manu digitisque stylum tangentibus, vicissim conglutinatis sententiis dictationes enchiridionis explere ausi erimus, adminiculante illo qui in hoc libro de Ecclesiae donis permistis ait prophetice: Cyprus cum nardo, nardus cum croco, fistula et cynamomum cum universis lignis Libani, myrrha et aloe, cum omnibus primis unguentis. Sed qualiter istae virtutes Ecclesiae alternando sibi permistim concinnant, in suo posterum 553Ddicemus loco. Unde jam tandem supplex exoro decus augustale aeque et eos quibus fortassis legere contigerit, ut quod ego more medicorum ac pigmentariorum, qui ut diversa unguenta et antidota temperatim possint componere, bilanciis staterae aeque ponderare satagunt, ut una alteram non superabundet, nec de aliis minus utilia derelinquant, sed aequa lance sua componant, studui aequali lance mentis trutinare sententias, et mella quodammodo et antidota animarum componere, non horrescat nec squalescat, sed diligenti exagio lustrando perlegat, atque medicamina animae gratanter investiget. Sed antequam prolegomena ad calcem perveniat, notandum est quod in hoc libro nil carnale nilque historiale requirere 554Adebeat, sed allegoriarum mysteria contineri non dubitet. Nominantur enim in hoc libro oscula, nominantur ubera, nominantur genae, nominantur femora. In quibus verbis non irridenda est sacra descriptio, sed major Dei misericordia consideranda est, quia dum membra corporis nominat, sic ad amorem suum vocat. Unde considerandum est, quam mirabiliter nobiscum et misericorditer operatur, qui ut cor nostrum ad instigationem sacri amoris accenderet, usque ad turpis amoris, ut papa Gregorius exponit, verba descendet. Sed unde se loquendo humiliat, inde nos ad intellectum exaltat, quia ex sermonibus hujus amoris discimus qua virtute in divinitatis amore ferveamus. Hoc autem nobis solerter intuendum est, ne cum verba exterioris 554Bamoris audivimus, ad exteriora sentienda remaneamus. Sic est enim Scriptura haec in vobis, sicut pictura in coloribus et rebus. Et nimis stultus est qui sic coloribus picturae inhaereat, ut res ignoret. Nos enim si verba quae exterius dicuntur amplectimur et sensus ignoramus, quasi ignorantes res quae depictae sunt solos colores tenemus. Scimus enim quia postquam genus humanum a paradisi gaudiis expulsum est, et in istam peregrinationem vitae praesentis veniens, caecum cor ab spirituali intellectu habet. Cui caeco cordi si diceretur voce Dominica: sequere Deum vel dilige Deum, per torporem insensibilitatis frigidum non caperet quod audiret. Propterea per quaedam aenigmata sermo divinus animae torpenti et frigidae loquitur, et de rebus quas novit: 554Clatenter insinuat amorem quem non novit, et animae longe a Deo infra positae, ut praefatus tractator ait, quasi quamdam machinam fecit, ut per illam levetur ad Deum. Interpositis quippe aenigmatibus, dum quiddam in verbis cognoscit quod suum est, in sensu verborum intelligit quod non est suum, et per terrena verba sperantur aeterna. Idcirco, o invictissime Caesar, oramus ut nihil in hoc volumine historialiter requiras, sed flores allegoriarum cum morali intellectu investiges, quem quibusdam in locis ob tuam consolationem subnectere decrevimus, ne solummodo allegoriarum allusionibus pressus, violenter vestra celsitudo theorica legendo fastidiat. Audiat etiam quod per eumdem Salomonem dicitur: « Gloria Dei celare verbum et gloria regum investigare 554Dsermonem (Prov. V). » Menti enim Deum quaerenti, tanto Deus gloriosius apparet, quanto subtilius investigatur ut appareat. Debemus itaque quod Deus mysteriis celat requirere. Ideoque sequitur: et gloria regum investigare sermonem. Qui enim non solummodo regnat terrarum, sed corpora sua, vel motus carnis regere et investigare noverunt, reges sunt. Quibus et per Psalmistam dicitur: « Et nunc, reges, intelligite, erudimini, qui judicatis terram (Psal. II), » et reliqua. Regum ergo gloria est investigare sermonem, quia bene viventium laus est perscrutari secreta mandatorum Dei. Scrutare ergo gloriosissime imperator scripturas, quatenus mysteria divina penetrando et intelligendo desideria et 555Amotus carnis investigare et regere vestra sapientia et solertia caeterosque imperio vestro subjectos docere, et actus prudentia consultuque honestissime gubernare valeat. His itaque praelibatis, imo efflagitatis, 556Atandem ad ea quae imperastis stylum flectere conabimur. Valeat semper in Christo prosperis successibus pollendo gloria vestra, decusque Francorum oramus.

PREFACE. TO THE EMPEROR LOTHAIR.

551BTo the most glorious and most excellent emperor LOTHAIR, ever Augustus: ANGELOMUS, the least of monks, entreating by entreating, entreats to obtain everlasting glory.

Lately, when I was keeping watch in your sacred palace — under the pretext, I say, of the handing down of the liberal arts and of the unfolding, namely, of Holy Scripture — your sagacious prudence deigned, as it is not unmindful, to summon me to your attentive presence, and to hold converse with me. But after the spiritual conversations of the heavenly life, and the supports of prayers, it pleased you by commanding — nay rather, by threatening [ minitando ] — to command that, by the office of the pen, according to the sense of the ancient fathers and the frugality of my slender wit, I should set in order a little work upon the Song of Songs of Solomon, where the conjunction of Christ and the Church, 551Cand the mysteries of the allegories, are proved to be contained beyond the rest of the τοῖς βιβλοῖς of the Old Testament; and that, so to speak, I should not refuse to expound it, and should not omit to display it to the presence of your eminence — whereby your diligence might have it, often frequented after the ordering of the commonwealth and brought back to your ears by reading, stretching itself out in contemplation away from the tumult of the affairs of the empire, and above all from the loss of your most holy consort, who by God's granting, as we believe, has been enrolled into the fellowship of the holy women and inscribed on the roll of blessedness — by reading contemplative things for the sake of consolation, and like the solitary turtledove, whose nature it is, as the natural philosophers report, that if once she has by mischance lost her mate, she does not seek another, but keeping chastity remains in a manner a widow, groaning alone and doing penance: of which we read in 551Dthe same book: « The voice, he says, of the turtledove is heard in our land (Cant. II), » that is, of Christ saying: « Do penance, for the kingdom of heaven will draw near (Matth. III). » [Vulg. appropinquavit, "hath drawn near"] And fair are thy cheeks as the turtledove's, the little channels of the cheeks being flooded with a shower of tears, so that you might have been able to pour out in secret to the Lord prayers for your own excesses and for the absolution of the most devoted empress. Which having heard, dreading the weight of so great a matter, astonished — nay, stupefied — I began to wonder why it had pleased your clemency to enjoin so great a work, to be dreaded even by the most erudite men, upon me, unlearned, and one who has not attained all the disciplines of the liberal arts, and feeble in the oracles 552Band blessings of Holy Scripture, when you had in your monarchy many doctors who could have handled it honorably and catholicly. But considering the censure of your rule, and silently turning over that apostolic word by which it is said (I Petr. II): « Be subject to every human creature for the Lord's sake, whether to the king as excelling, or to leaders as sent by him » [Vulg. propter Deum, "for God's sake"] , but rather that word of the Lord's, where he promises: « Amen, he says, I say to you, whatsoever you ask praying, believe that you shall receive, and it shall be done for you (Marc. II). » [Vulg. Marc. XI, 24 Propterea dico vobis, omnia quaecumque orantes petitis … et evenient vobis] Perceiving also, by the inward weighing of my mind, that what was commanded by so great a majesty would not be without the divine assent, with rash mouth I presumed to gainsay by no apologetic and no winding of excuse, but rather, as far as 552CI could, relying on divine supports, I attempted, though fearful, to comply with the imperial commands. But not counting it worth a straw that, without the assurances and likewise the orders of my magnificent lord and abbot, namely Drogo your uncle, lest I should seem to exceed the discipline of the Rule, I should have had to set my pen to the writing — not only because he was the son of the emperor Charlemagne, and brother of your most holy and most devoted father Louis Caesar, but because he was a chief bishop and my distinguished abbot. And when by suggestion I had brought it in to his sacred ears, and had set forth the order of the matter, which having been drunk in by his ears, he not only refused, but — as he is most gentle, and persuadable in affability, most excellent in kindness, and most fervent toward every good — 552Dafter the manner of his modesty he not only commanded frankly by free authority, but with soothing tranquillity and devout address, that I should not refuse to undertake, as best I might, the angustale work and spiritual ornament. Hearing which, so now at last emboldened by his exhortations, driven on by your nurturing utterances and holy commands, and above all relying on divine helps and stirred up by the prayers of the holy doctors: whatever, O most learned prince, I shall be able to find scattered among the holy doctors in our keeping — especially in the sayings of the most blessed Pope Gregory, of the ornament of Roman eloquence, since I find it compacted throughout by none alone 553A— I shall attempt to take in hand out of the tradition of my master, by the conjecture of my own wit, with the pen running, in a manner of speech rather clipped and compendious, though unpolished; and I shall endeavor to finish it in the form of a little book, so that your highness may have as it were an enchiridion, that is, a little book excerpted and in a manner handy, which, God favoring, it may please you to read often at fitting hours after the labors of empire, after the cares of the Church, ruminating upon divine things unto consolation. And because he cannot run through the most broad fields, namely of Holy Scripture, let it at least not irk him, at rest, to search out our stromata briefly. But it must indeed be known that, as is the custom of certain writers, we have not assigned all the names of the several doctors man by man on the little page in the margin, marked with letters; but rather, out of their sayings, we have judged it right to fit together some things of the 553Bexpositors from among many kinds — by briefly cutting short, that is, the manifold matters, the superfluities being removed — and, following their sense, to join together some things from the more prolix by excerpting, and, by interpolating some things of our own, to enlarge the longer ones. Thus assuredly, thus, with the various allusions of the dramas running through the short trains, which by knitting together in turn we have ordained to fill out the little book of sentences, not greatly fearing those who twist the nose as though they were wise, nor those who are readier to reprehend than to imitate, and who cannot fulfil what they contrive to reprehend by sneering. For we know, we know, that in the concord of the organ the pipes of bronze are some longer, some shorter; but when one blast has been administered by the bellows, and a trained hand commands, 553Cand the fingers handle one — though the pipes are diverse — they sweetly render to astonished ears a harmony as a salpinx, the voices of trumpets and a concord, breathing forth melody in sweet song. So indeed, since some sentences are longer, the superfluities being cut away, and others shorter, the necessary things being added, we shall have dared — the Holy Spirit breathing through one body, the hand commanding and the fingers touching the pen — with the sentences glued together in turn, to fill out the dictations of the enchiridion, with the help of him who in this book says prophetically concerning the mingled gifts of the Church: Cypress with spikenard, spikenard with saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon with all the trees of Lebanon, myrrh and aloes with all the chief ointments. [Vulg. Cant. IV, 14 nardus et crocus, "spikenard and saffron"] But in what manner these virtues of the Church, alternating, are fitted together among themselves in mingled fashion, we shall say afterward in its own 553Dplace. Whence now at last I suppliantly entreat the imperial ornament, and equally those to whom it may perchance fall to read it, that what I have striven to do — after the manner of physicians and perfumers, who, that they may be able to compound various ointments and antidotes in due measure, take pains to weigh them evenly in the scales of the balance, so that the one may not overbalance the other, nor may they leave behind the less useful of the others, but may compound their own with an even scale — namely, to weigh sentences in the equal scale of the mind, and in a manner to compound honeys and antidotes for souls: that he neither shudder at it nor let it grow squalid, but read it through, surveying it with diligent assay, and gratefully search out the medicines of the soul. But before the prolegomena come to their end, it must be noted that in this book one ought to seek nothing carnal and nothing historical, 554Abut ought not to doubt that the mysteries of the allegories are contained in it. For in this book kisses are named, breasts are named, cheeks are named, thighs are named. In which words the sacred description is not to be derided, but the greater mercy of God is to be considered, because while he names the members of the body, he thus calls us to his own love. Whence it is to be considered how wonderfully and how mercifully he works with us, who, that he might kindle our heart to the incitement of sacred love, will descend even to the words of base love, as Pope Gregory expounds. But whence he humbles himself in speaking, thence he exalts us to understanding, because from the discourses of this love we learn with what power we are to be fervent in the love of the Godhead. But this must be diligently observed by us, lest, when we have heard the words of outward 554Blove, we remain at the perceiving of outward things. For this Scripture is in you just as a picture is in colors and in things. And he is exceedingly foolish who so clings to the colors of the picture that he is ignorant of the things. For if we embrace the words that are spoken outwardly and are ignorant of the senses, then, as though ignorant of the things that are depicted, we hold the colors alone. For we know that after the human race was driven out from the joys of paradise, and coming into this pilgrimage of the present life, it has a heart blind to spiritual understanding. To which blind heart, if it were said with the Lord's voice, "Follow God" or "Love God," it would not, through the torpor of insensibility, take in cold what it heard. Therefore by certain riddles the divine speech speaks to the torpid and cold soul, and out of things that it knows 554Cit secretly insinuates a love that it does not know; and for the soul set far beneath, away from God, as the aforesaid treatise-writer says, he has made as it were a kind of engine, that by it she may be lifted up to God. For, riddles being interposed, while she recognizes in the words something that is her own, in the sense of the words she understands what is not her own, and through earthly words eternal things are hoped for. Therefore, O most unconquered Caesar, we pray that you seek nothing in this volume after the historical manner, but that you search out the flowers of the allegories together with the moral understanding, which in certain places we have determined to append for your consolation, lest, pressed down by the allusions of the allegories alone, your highness should violently grow weary in reading contemplative things. Let him also hear what is said through the same Solomon: « The glory of God is to conceal a word, and the glory of kings to search out 554Da speech (Prov. V). » For to a mind seeking God, God appears the more gloriously the more subtly he is searched out that he may appear. We ought therefore to seek what God conceals in mysteries. And so there follows: "and the glory of kings to search out a speech." For they who not only reign over lands, but have known how to rule and to search out their own bodies, or the motions of the flesh, are kings. To whom it is also said through the Psalmist: « And now, O kings, understand; be instructed, you that judge the earth (Psal. II), » and the rest. The glory of kings, therefore, is to search out a speech, because it is the praise of those who live well to scrutinize the secrets of the commandments of God. Search therefore the Scriptures, most glorious emperor, so that, by penetrating and understanding the divine mysteries, your wisdom and diligence may be able to search out and to rule the desires and 555Amotions of the flesh, and to teach the rest who are subject to your rule, and most honorably to govern their acts by prudence and by counsel. These things therefore having been tasted beforehand — nay rather, urgently demanded — 556Awe shall at last endeavor to bend the pen to those things which you have commanded. We pray that your glory, and the ornament of the Franks, may ever fare well in Christ, prevailing with prosperous successes.

INCIPIT LIBER

  • 555

Priusquam augustale decus ad fistulas harmoniae spiritualis, videlicet allegoriarum concentus manum ad scribendum, flante Spiritu sancto, exeram, et abyssos theoriae vela pandendo historiae, styli officio tangam, veluti clavi abdita domus reseram, quo facilius interiora mysteria revelentur, in argumentatione 555Bfidei ostendere decrevi, quare iste liber tertius in opusculis Salomonis ponitur, et quid significationis geratur, cujusve vitae typum habere asseratur, simul etiam quibus nominibus sancta Ecclesia in hoc eodem libro inscribitur, cur quoque idem volumen non simpliciter Canticum, sed Canticum canticorum appellatur: ad extremum quibusque Salomon in Scripturis sanctis nominibus insignitur, ut cuncta prius praelibando dum fuerint praescita, in posterum facilius atque avidius capiantur exposita. Tribus namque nominibus vocatum fuisse Salomonem Scripturae manifestissime docent. Salomonem, id est pacificum, eo quod in pace regnum obtinuerit, et per parabolas populum erudierit, in significationem 555CSalvatoris qui discipulis suis ait: « Pacem relinquo vobis, pacem meam do vobis (Joan. XIV). » Et ipse per parabolas turbas docuit, de quo etiam scriptum est: « Multiplicabitur ejus imperium, et pacis non erit finis (Isa. IX) » et reliqua. Et Idida, id est dilectus Domini, videlicet quia prius a Domino dilectus fuerit, similiter in typum Domini qui a Patre dilectus est, sicut ipse ait: « Hic est Filius meus dilectus, in quo mihi complacui (Matth. III). » Et Coheleth, id est Ecclesiasten. Ecclesiastes autem Graeco sermone appellatur, eoque coetum, id est Ecclesiam congreget, quem nos nuncupare possumus concionatorem, quod loquatur ad populum, et sermo ejus non specialiter ad unum, sed ad universos generaliter dirigatur, qui velut extensa manu omnium 555Dtumultus sedat, eosque ad unam sententiam revocat. Sic idem Salomon in hoc libro fecisse ostenditur, cum in libri termino ait: « Finem loquendi omnes pariter audiamus: Deum time, et mandata ejus observa, hoc est enim omnis homo (Eccli. XIII), » illum praefigurans qui non solum pacem intulit, et dilectus Deo Patri exstitit, sed qui omnibus ait: « Poenitentiam agite: appropinquabit enim regnum coelorum (Matth. III) » qui « medio pariete destructo, » et inimicitias in carne sua evacuans fecit utraque unum, quique Deum timere, et mandata ejus observare jubet. Denique sicut Salomon tribus praefatis nominibus appellatus est, sic juxta numerum vocabulorum tria volumina edidit, Proverbia videlicet quae nos Parabolas nominamus, quas Hebraei Masloth 556Aappellant. Ecclesiasten, quem et illi, ut diximus, Coheleth nominant. Et Cantica canticorum quem Schir aschirim praenotant et hos rationabili ordine distinxit. In Proverbiis namque parvulum docens, et quasi de officiis per sententias erudiens, unde et ad filium ejus sermo crebro reperitur. In Ecclesiaste 556Bvero maturae aetatis virum instruens, ut nequaquam mundi rebus credat aliquid esse perpetuum, sed caduca et brevia universa quae cernimus. Ad extremum jam consummatum virum, et calcato saeculo praeparatum in Cantico canticorum amplexibus jungit. Nisi enim prius reliquimus vitia, et pompis saeculi renuntiantes, expeditos nos ad adventum Christi paraverimus, non poterimus cantare Canticum canticorum, et dicere: « Osculetur me osculo oris sui. » Sive ita volumina edidit, in quibus trium generum disciplinas composuit, quibus ad rerum scientiam pervenitur. In primo namque moralem, in secundo vero naturalem, ad ultimum quippe contemplativam, quas Graeci ita ethnicam, physicam, et theoriam nominaverunt. Unde non abhorret ab intellectu, quia 556Cliber iste in opusculis Salomonis tertio loco ponitur. In proverbiis itaque moralis vita exprimitur, ubi dicitur: « Audi, fili mi, sapientiam meam, et prudentiae meae inclina aurem tuam (Prov. I). » In Ecclesiaste vero naturalis. Ibi quippe quod omnia ad fidem tendant, considerantur, dum dicitur: « Vanitas vanitatum, et omnia vanitas (Eccli. I). » In Canticis autem canticorum contemplativa vita exprimitur, dum in eis ipsius Domini adventus et aspectus desideratur, cum sponsae voce dicitur: « Osculetur me osculo oris sui. » Hos etiam ordines trium patriarcharum vitae significant Abraham, Isaac, videlicet et Jacob. Moralitatem quippe Abraham obediendo tenuit quando de terra sua exiit, et filium 556Dimmolare non distulit. Isaac vero naturalem vitam puteos fodiendo figuravit, imo enim puteos fodere est, per considerationem naturalem omnia quae infra sunt perscrutando rimari. Jacob vero contemplativam vitam tenuit, qui ascendentes et descendentes angelos vidit. Sed quia naturalis consideratio ad perfectionem non ducitur, nisi prius moralitas teneatur, recte post Proverbia Ecclesiastes ponitur. Et quia superna contemplatio non conspicitur, nisi prius haec infra labentia despiciantur, recte post Ecclesiasten Canticum canticorum ponitur. Prius quippe est mores componere, postmodum omnia quae adsunt, tanquam non adsint considerare. Tertio vero loco, munda cordis acie, superna et interna conspicere. His itaque ut summa moralium 557Atractator papa Gregorius depromit librorum gradibus quamdam ad contemplationem Dei scalam et machinam fecit, ut dum primum in saeculo bene geruntur honesta, postmodum etiam honesta, saeculi despiciantur et ad extremum etiam Dei intima et secreta contemplentur. Unde non immerito liber iste, non Canticum, sed Canticum canticorum nominatur, ubi coelestis verbi Dei et animae conjunctio demonstratur. Quod canticum ita intelligitur omnibus praecellere canticis, sicut Dominus noster assumptus homo, apostolis, patriarchis, vel prophetis, aut quaecunque illae sunt coelestium potestates: ut sicut rex regum, et Dominus dominantium est Christus, ita et Canticum canticorum super omnia cantica quae a prophetis cantata sunt titulatur. Et sicut in Veteri Testamento 557Balia sunt sancta, et alia Sancta sanctorum, et alia sabbata, et alia sabbata sabbatorum, sic et alia sunt cantica, et alia Cantica canticorum. Sancta erant in tabernaculo, et quae exterius agebantur; sabbata erant quae ex singulis hebdomadibus celebrabantur, sed Sancta sanctorum secretiori quadam veneratione suscipiebantur: et sabbata sabbatorum, non nisi in suis festivitatibus celebrabantur. In Cantico canticorum secretum quoddam, et solemne interius est, quod secretum in occultis intelligentiis penetratur; nam si exterioribus verbis attenditur, secretum non est. Unde jam superius in praefatione disputavimus. Sciendum est etiam quia in Scriptura sacra alia sunt cantica victoriae, alia cantica exhortationis et contestationis, alia cantica exsultationis, alia cantica adjutorii, 557Calia cantica conjunctionis cum Deo. Canticum victoriae est quod Maria transito mari Rubro cecinit dicens: « Cantemus Domino, gloriose enim honorificatus est: equum et ascensorem projecit in mare (Exod. XV). » Canticum exhortationis et contestationis est quod Moyses Israelitis ad terram repromissionis propinquantibus dixit. « Attende coelum et loquar, et audiat terra verba ex ore meo (Isa. XXVIII). » Canticum exsultationis est quod Anna non solum pro Samuele, sed prospecta fecunditate Ecclesiae in semetipsa cecinit dicens: « Exsultavit cor meum in Domino (I Reg. II). » Ubi per semetipsam veritas fecunditatem prolis ecclesiasticae expressit, cum dicit: « Sterilis peperit plurimos, et quae multos habebat filios infirmata est (Ibid.) » Canticum adjutorii David post 557Dpraelium cecinit dicens: « Diligam te, Domine, virtus mea (Psal. XVII). » Canticum vero conjunctionis cum Deo, hoc est canticum quod in nuptiis velut epithalamium sponsi et sponsae canitur, id est Cantica canticorum, quod tanto est omnibus canticis solemnius, quanto et in nuptiis solemnitatis sublimioris offertur. Per illa cantica vitia devitantur, per ista vero unusquisque virtutibus locupletatur. Per illa cavetur hostis, per haec Dominus familiari amore complectitur. Cantata sunt et alia cantica a diversis prophetis, pro diversis personis vel causis: sicut et Habacuc pro ignorantibus praenotavit. Cantavit et Debora de victoria Sisarae. Cantaverunt et tres pueri in fornace in quo mirabilia omnipotentis 558ADei narrantur. Cantavit et Hieremias canticum lamentationis de conversione Hierusalem et sanctuarii, et totius populi sui, et multi alii. Sed ideo hoc Canticum canticorum appellatur, quia ad propriam Christi regis et Ecclesiae conjunctionem esse manifeste probatur, ubi orat ad patrem sponsi Ecclesia, qui est sermo Dei, ut jam non per internuntios qui sunt angeli et prophetae, qui a Dei Filio Ecclesiae futurae reprobationis nuntia deferebant, sed ipsum per incarnationis mysterium jam aliquando coram ostendat, jam ipsius formam speciosam prae filiis hominum amplecti mereatur eique dulcissima oscula suscipere, et gratissima ubera tractare. Ista namque quae sic desiderat ἐκκλησία Graeco vocabulo dicitur, Latine congregatio, vel melius convocatio, Hebraice 558BSynagoga interpretatur. Pro loco vel tempore diversis nominibus a verbo Dei vocata esse probatur. De cujus vocabulis superius polliciti sumus. Aliquando etenim Hierusalem nonnunquam Sion plerumque amica, saepe vero dilecta. Interdum soror, vel speciosa sponsa, columba, seu immaculata, vel perfecta in Christi persona ore prophetarum et in hoc libro appellata cognoscitur. Hierusalem siquidem dicitur, quia post iracundiam omnipotentis Dei pacem de coelo missam a patre suscepit, eo quod aliquam signatur habere pacem. Quam pacem et conjunctionem exorare dicitur cum eum deprecatur: « Osculetur me osculo oris sui. » Sion vero, quia de convalle lacrymarum idololatriae, ubi in corpore lapsa fuerat in Adam, nec corde ad montem paradisi reascendit, 558Cet pro munditia cordis speculum se praebendo individuae Trinitati, conspicua et excelsa efficitur. In qua Deus omnipotens quasi in fertili monte delectetur deambulare, et beatum doctrinae suae semen spargere, propter quod specula vel semen in ea interpretatur. Amica autem dicitur, quoniam repudiando diabolum, ad amicitias revocavit Deum. Dilecta vero appellatur, quoniam post incredulitatis amarissima odia credulitatis muneribus dilecta efficitur Dei. Soror videlicet nuncupata per assumptae carnis mysterium, cum ex eadem materia videndi nascitur terris, de qua et Ecclesia generatur. Speciosa, scilicet pronuntiatur, abstersa per baptismum ab omnibus peccatorum maculis, post multam Aethiopicam tenebrosamque consuetudinem criminum, 558Dpelle mundata, et ad genuinam pulchritudinem revocata. Sponsa vero effecta est, corpus et sanguinem suo corpori conjungendo. Per quod sacramentum spopondit, se praeter eum nullum alium amatorem in toto corde diligere, pretiosum ejus annulum fidei magno studio conservando. Formosa namque praedicatur, formam ejus imaginis in qua creata est in anima reformando. Columba vero nominatur: omni rapacitate quoniam aduncis manibus velut vulturinis consueverat perpetrare deposita, nunc consortio Spiritus sancti sociata, simplicitatis et largitatis candore columbarum refulget. Et quae consueverat in malis doctoribus aliis animabus aeternae vitae rapere cibos, nunc in bonis doctoribus de suo suavissimo 559Agutture audientibus reimpartiri, sicut natura est columbarum. Immaculata autem laudatur, deposita veteri antiquaque consuetudine criminum, et stola sacrosancti baptismatis semel in luta, immaculate vivendo, ultra se non maculavit. Et Domini sui Christi imitatrix effecta pro ejus nomine moriendo lima martyrii et mortificationis splendescit. Nullum aliud ferramentum fortius potuit reperire, per quod antiqua scoria animae, ut Apponius ait, limaretur, nisi martyrii limam, per quod in tormentorum fornace possit anima, quantalibet sordium peccatorum mole sit circumdata, in suo sanguine renovari, per quod perfecta sit columba, et in Christo immaculata. Unde et perfecta quippe nuncupatur, omne judicium a Patre videlicet suscipienda in 559BChristo. Haec ergo Ecclesia, in quam omnium credentium in Christo multitudo consistit quae retro ante videlicet sponsi adventum in suo sanguine ignominiose jacebat et commaculata, juxta prophetam, in curis magnis enutrita, et usque ad annos pubertatis provecta, et ejus doctrina quasi monilibus exornata, sed ejus conjunctione usque ad regnum coelorum sublimata. Sed quomodo ignominiose et conculcata jacebat per Prophetam increpatur. « Radix tua et generatio tua de terra Chanaan. Pater tuus Amorrhaeus, et mater tua Cethea in die qua nata es. In die ortus non est praecisus umbilicus tuus, non es lota aqua in salutem, nec sale salita, nec involuta pannis. Non pepercit super te oculus meus ut faceret tibi unum ex his, misertus tui, sed 559Cjacebas nuda plenaque ignominia conculcata in sanguine tuo. Ubera tua intumuerunt, et pilus tuus germinavit, et ecce tempus tuum amantium, et jacebas nuda, confusionis plena (Ezech. XVIII). » Et qualiter per adventum suae conjunctionis facere voluit unum ex his misertus sui subjunxit: « Et misi amictum meum, et operui ignominiam tuam. Lavi te aqua et mundavi sanguinem tuum ex te, et unxi te oleo, et vestivi te vestimento multicolorio. Simila et melle nutrivi te. Cinxi te bysso, et calciavi te hyacintho et posui coronam decoris in capite tuo, et circulos in auribus tuis, et juravi tibi et ingressus sum pactum tecum, et facta es mihi. ait Dominus Deus (Ibid.). » Quod quamvis plebs Judaeorum arguatur 559Dcontumax, alloquitur in ejus persona omnis anima quae per lavacrum regenerationis nascitur, per voluntatem ejus, quae eam prius genuerat per peccatum Amorrhaei, qui amarus interpretatur, id est diaboli, et per consuetudinem nefariam, quae Cethea praesenti loco dicitur, quae mentis excessus interpretatur, fuerit oblita improbari, probatur increpatione quam clemens Dominus noster Jesus Christus, per sanctam apparitionem suam, licet nescienter de praedictis sordibus collectam enutrivit, et pretiosis vestibus vel monilibus exornavit, et sibi consortem regni facere est dignatus per sacrosancti baptismatis undam, ubi gloriosa copula Christi Filii Dei et Ecclesiae celebratur. In quo sacro mysterio ablutionis aqua lavatur sapientiae sale suscepto, individuae 560Acoaeternaeque Trinitatis fide prostibuli traditione salitur, variarum virtutum charismate, vestimentis nudata operitur. Sanctus Spiritus pinguedine, charismatis unctione repletur. Ubi simila in Christi corpore, et melle in ejus eloquiis enutritur, ubi circulis divinae intelligentiae aures ornatur, martyrii gemmata in capite corona refulgens, bysso quae est puritatis indicium cingitur, id est amore virginitatis et pudicitiae, ab omni eam dissolutione continens, revocatamque constringit. Calciantur hyacintho, qui color praeparans Hebraice dicitur, ut praeparati sint pedes ad praecepta Evangelii pacis semper concurrere. His videlicet ornamentis decorari cupiebat Ecclesia, sed quia ornari non poterat nisi per ejus apparitionem, idcirco illius visitationem 560Bdesiderabat. Audierat enim tanquam sponsa per prophetam in persona sponsi, id est Verbi Dei Patris dicentis: « Desponsabo te mihi in sempiternum, desponsabo te mihi in justitia, desponsabo te mihi in judicio, desponsabo te mihi in misericordia, desponsabo te mihi in fide (Ose. II). » Et per Isaiam similiter vox ipsius Ecclesiae ait: « Posuit mihi mitram sicut sponsae et induit me vestimentis salutis, sicut sponsum decoratum corona, et sicut sponsam ornatam monilibus suis circumdedit me (Isa. LXI). » Et Zacharias hujuscemodi gloriam trepidanti Ecclesiae nuntiatam dicendo ait: « Noli timere, filia Sion, ecce Rex tuus venit tibi mitis et mansuetus, sedens super pullum asinae, Salvator et Redemptor ipse est (Zach. IX). » Hi ergo omnes patriarchae et prophetae, 560Cfuturam, ineffabilem Christi et Ecclesiae copulam cecinere. Apostolus autem jam celebratam exposuit dicens: « Viri, diligite uxores vestras, sicut Christus Ecclesiam dilexit, qui semetipsum tradidit pro ea, ut ipse sibi exhiberet immaculatam Ecclesiam, non habentem maculam aut rugam (Ephes. V). » Et iterum ad Corinthios: « Despondi enim vos uni viro virginem castam exhibere Christo (II Cor. XI). » Et alio loco ita exhortatur fideles: « Vos estis corpus Christi, et caro de carne ejus (I Cor. XII). » Huic videlicet tantis ac talibus testibus confirmandae, adhibenda sunt omnia officia nuptiarum. Ubi praesto sint etiam amici sponsi, sodales etiam sponsae, mores quoque cantantium, qui dulci modulamine sponsi potentiam canant vel sponsae pulchritudinem laudent, qua largitatem 560Dsponsi est consecuta, quomodo se excelsus humilitaverit de illa altitudine deitatis limum profundi, ut humilem, dejectamque carnis materiam ad coelorum culmen levatam suae majestati conjungeret. Et notandum quia aliquando Scriptura sacra Dominum vocat, aliquando patrem, aliquando sponsum. Quando enim vult se timeri, Dominum se nominat; quando vult honorari, patrem; quando vult amari, sponsum. Ipse per prophetam dicit: « Si Dominus sum, ubi est timor meus? Si Pater ego sum, ubi est honor meus? (Mal. I) » Et rursum dicit: « Desponsavi te mihi in justitia, et fide, vel certe recordatus sum diei desponsationis tuae in deserto (Ose. II). » Et quid est apud Deum, quando, et quando 561Anon est. Sed quia prius timeri se vult ut honoretur, et prius honorari, ut ad ejus amorem perveniatur, et Dominum se propter timorem nominat, et patrem propter honorem, et sponsum propter amorem, ut per timorem veniatur ad honorem, per honorem vero ejus perveniatur ad amorem. Quanto ergo dignius est honor quam timor, tanto plus gaudet Deus pater dici quam Dominus. Et quanto ergo charius est, amor quam honor, tanto plus gaudet Deus, sponsus quam pater. In hoc ergo libro Dominus et Ecclesia, non dominus et ancilla, sed sponsus nominatur et sponsa, ut non soli timori, non soli reverentiae, sed etiam amori deserviatur, et in verbis exterioribus incitetur affectus interior. Cum se Dominum nominat, indicat quod creati sumus. Cum se patrem nominat, indicat 561Bquod conjuncti. Plus est autem conjunctos esse Deo, quam creatos et adoptatos. Sic autem generaliter ex voce Ecclesiae adventus Domini in hoc opere praestolatur, ut etiam specialiter unaquaeque anima ingressum Dei ad cor suum, tanquam aditum sponsi in thalamum accipiat. Et sciendum quia in hoc libro quatuor personae loquentes introducuntur, sponsus videlicet et sponsa, adolescentulae vero cum sponsa, et greges sodalium cum sponso. Sponsa enim ipsa perfecta Ecclesia est, sponsus Dominus, sicut crebro dictum est. Adolescentulae vero cum sponsa sunt inchoantes animae, et per novum studium pubescentes. Sodales vero sponsi sunt sive angeli qui saepe hominibus ad ipsos venientes apparuerunt, seu certe perfecti quique viri in Ecclesia, qui veritate 561Chominibus nuntiare noverunt; sed qui singillatim adolescentulae vel sodales sunt, toti simul sponsa sunt, qui toti simul Ecclesia sunt, quamvis et juxta unumquemque fidelem haec ita nomina accipi possunt. Nam qui Dominum jam perfecte amat, sponsa est: qui sponsum praedicat, sodalis est, qui adhuc novellas viam bonorum sequitur, adolescentula est. Imitamur ergo ut simus sponsa. Si haec nondum valemus, simus sodales. Si neque hoc adepti sumus, saltem ad hunc thalamum velut adoloscentulae conveniamus. Sed sciendum antequam ad conjunctionis pacem veniamus, quod sapientissimus Salomon quidquid ab initio mundi usque in finem, in mysteriis egit acturusve erit Dei sermo erga Ecclesiam, in figura et aenigmatibus demonstravit. In quo cantico 561Domnia quae narrantur tecta mysteriis in Verbi incarnatione revelata et completa docentur, ubi allisa erigitur humana progenies, compedita absolvitur, corrupta ad virginitatis integritatem reformatur, expulsa a paradiso, redditur ex captiva libera, ex peregrina civis, ex ancilla domina, ex vilissima regina et sponsa Creatoris sui Verbi Dei Christi benignitate effecta ostenditur, quae digna sit ejus suscipere oscula, et una cum eo effecta spiritum in coelestibus sublimata regnare. Quae sublimitas humiliatione Filii Dei celebrata cognoscitur sicut magister gentium docet de Christo dicendo: « Humiliavit se, ut nos exaltaret (Phil. II). » Non enim injuria deputatur artifici pretiosum annulum lapsum in corporis foveam 562Apaulisper deposita stola ad quaerendum descendere, et inventum rursum suae dexterae reddere. Nam quidquid humile, quidquid vilissimum, quidquid despectum indignumque stulta impiaque gentilitas opinatur cujus incredulitatis caligo oculos cordis caecavit, hoc gloriosum, hoc mirabile, hoc, inquam, magnificum apud divinam clementiam et omnes virtutes coelestes esse probatur. Nam quae immensa potentia, quae bonitatis dulcedo, quae miserationis superlativa omnium operum flagrantia paternae virtutis, admirata ab angelis, omniumque sanctorum choris praecelsis vocibus perpetuis laudibus offertur, nisi Dominus ancillam vere pauperculam, ut externus mortalem per assumptionem carnis Dominus Jesus Christus Ecclesiam, id est Dei sermo 562Bsibi animam sub conjunctionis titulo copularet? Quia igitur saepius sponsum et sponsam, Dominum et Ecclesiam diximus, et rite, juxta poetam, viam sermone levamus: Spesque fidesque meum comitantur in ardua gressum, Blandius ad summam tandem pervenimus arcem.

Ut jam ipsum textum divini oraculi pandamus velut adolescentulae, velut sodales audiamus verba sponsi, audiamus verba sponsae, et in eorum sermonibus fervorem discamus amoris. Itaque sancta Ecclesia diu praestolans adventum Domini, diu sitiens fontem vitae, quomodo optet videre praesentiam sponsi sui, quomodo desideret et dicat.

HERE BEGINS THE BOOK

  • 555

Before, O imperial ornament, I stretch out my hand to write to the pipes of the spiritual harmony — that is, to the concord of the allegories — the Holy Spirit breathing; and before, by the office of the pen, I touch the depths of contemplation by spreading the sails of the history, and unbar as with a key the hidden things of the house, whereby the inner mysteries may more easily be revealed: I have determined to show, in the argumentation 555Bof the faith, why this book is placed third among the little works of Solomon, and what significance it bears, or of what life it is asserted to hold the type; and likewise by what names holy Church is inscribed in this same book; why too the same volume is called not simply the Song, but the Song of Songs; and finally by what names Solomon is marked out in Holy Scripture — so that, all things being tasted beforehand, when they have been known in advance, the exposition may afterward be grasped more easily and more eagerly. For the Scriptures teach most plainly that Solomon was called by three names. Solomon, that is, peaceable, because he obtained the kingdom in peace and instructed the people by parables, as a signification 555Cof the Savior, who says to his disciples: « Peace I leave with you, my peace I give unto you (Joan. XIV). » And he himself taught the crowds by parables, of whom also it is written: « His empire shall be multiplied, and of peace there shall be no end (Isa. IX) » and the rest. And Idida, that is, beloved of the Lord, namely because he was first beloved by the Lord, likewise as a type of the Lord who is beloved by the Father, as he himself says: « This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased (Matth. III). » And Coheleth, that is, Ecclesiastes. Now he is called Ecclesiastes in the Greek tongue, and by that, because he gathers the assembly, that is, the Church — whom we may name the preacher, because he speaks to the people, and his discourse is directed not specially to one, but generally to all; who as with hand outstretched quiets the tumults of all 555Dand calls them back to one judgment. So the same Solomon is shown to have done in this book, when at the end of the book he says: « Let us all alike hear the end of speaking: Fear God, and keep his commandments; for this is all man (Eccli. XIII), » prefiguring him who not only brought in peace and stood forth beloved of God the Father, but who says to all: « Do penance: for the kingdom of heaven will draw near (Matth. III) » [Vulg. appropinquavit, "hath drawn near"] — who, « the middle wall being destroyed, » and emptying out the enmities in his own flesh, made both one, and who commands us to fear God and to keep his commandments. Finally, as Solomon was called by the three aforesaid names, so according to the number of the appellations he published three volumes: namely Proverbs, which we name Parables, which the Hebrews call Masloth; 556AEcclesiastes, which they also, as we have said, name Coheleth; and the Song of Songs, which they entitle Schir aschirim. And these he distinguished in a reasonable order. For in Proverbs he teaches the little one, and instructs him as it were concerning duties by maxims; whence also his discourse is often found addressed to a son. But in Ecclesiastes 556Bhe instructs a man of ripe age, that he by no means believe anything among the things of the world to be perpetual, but that all things we behold are falling and brief. Lastly, in the Song of Songs he joins in embraces the man now made perfect and prepared, the world being trodden underfoot. For unless we have first left our vices, and, renouncing the pomps of the world, have made ourselves unencumbered for the coming of Christ, we shall not be able to sing the Song of Songs, and to say: « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » Or he published the volumes thus, in which he set forth the disciplines of three kinds, by which one arrives at the knowledge of things. For in the first the moral, in the second the natural, and in the last of all the contemplative — which the Greeks have named thus, ethnicam, physicam, and theoriam. Whence it is not repugnant to the understanding that 556Cthis book is placed in the third place among the little works of Solomon. So then in Proverbs the moral life is expressed, where it is said: « Hear, my son, my wisdom, and incline thine ear to my prudence (Prov. I). » [Vulg. Prov. V, 1 Fili mi, attende ad sapientiam meam, "My son, attend to my wisdom"] But in Ecclesiastes, the natural. For there it is considered that all things tend toward faith, when it is said: « Vanity of vanities, and all is vanity (Eccli. I). » But in the Song of Songs the contemplative life is expressed, while in it the coming and the sight of the Lord himself is desired, when it is said with the voice of the bride: « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » These orders also the lives of the three patriarchs signify, namely Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. For Abraham held morality by obeying, when he went out from his own land, and did not delay 556Dto sacrifice his son. But Isaac figured the natural life by digging wells; for indeed to dig wells is, by natural consideration, to search out by scrutiny all the things that are below. But Jacob held the contemplative life, who saw the angels ascending and descending. But because natural consideration is not brought to perfection unless morality be first held, rightly is Ecclesiastes placed after Proverbs. And because the heavenly contemplation is not beheld unless these slipping things below be first despised, rightly is the Song of Songs placed after Ecclesiastes. For first is to order one's morals; then, to consider all things that are present as though they were not present. But in the third place, with a clean keenness of heart, to behold the things above and the things within. By these degrees of the books, therefore, as the treater of the sum of moral 557Abooks, Pope Gregory, sets forth, he made a certain ladder and engine unto the contemplation of God: so that, while first in the world honorable things are well performed, afterward even the honorable things of the world are despised, and at the last even the inmost and secret things of God are contemplated. Whence not undeservedly is this book named not the Song, but the Song of Songs, where the conjunction of the heavenly Word of God and of the soul is shown. Which song is so understood to excel all songs, as our Lord, the man assumed, excels the apostles, the patriarchs, or the prophets, or whatsoever those heavenly powers are: so that, as Christ is King of kings and Lord of lords, so also the Song of Songs is entitled above all the songs that were sung by the prophets. And as in the Old Testament 557Bsome things are holy places and others the Holy of holies, and some are sabbaths and others sabbaths of sabbaths, so also some are songs and others the Song of Songs. The holy places were in the tabernacle, and the things that were done outwardly; the sabbaths were those which were celebrated out of each several week; but the Holy of holies was received with a certain more secret veneration, and the sabbaths of sabbaths were not celebrated except in their own festivals. In the Song of Songs there is a certain secret and an inward solemnity, which secret is penetrated in hidden understandings; for if it is attended to in outward words, it is not a secret. Whereof we have already disputed above in the preface. It is also to be known that in Holy Scripture some songs are songs of victory, others songs of exhortation and attestation, others songs of exultation, others songs of help, 557Cothers songs of conjunction with God. The song of victory is that which Miriam sang when the Red Sea had been crossed, saying: « Let us sing to the Lord, for he has been gloriously honored: horse and rider he has cast into the sea (Exod. XV). » [Vulg. Exod. XV, 1 gloriose enim magnificatus est … deiecit in mare, "gloriously magnified … cast down"] The song of exhortation and attestation is that which Moses spoke to the Israelites as they drew near to the land of promise. « Give heed, O heaven, and I will speak; and let the earth hear the words out of my mouth (Isa. XXVIII). » [Vulg. Deut. XXXII, 1 Audite, caeli, quae loquor; audiat terra verba oris mei] The song of exultation is that which Anna sang, not only for Samuel, but having foreseen in herself the fruitfulness of the Church, saying: « My heart hath exulted in the Lord (I Reg. II). » Where through her own self the truth expressed the fruitfulness of the ecclesiastical offspring, when she says: « The barren hath borne very many, and she that had many sons is made weak (Ibid.) » The song of help David sang after 557Dthe battle, saying: « I will love thee, O Lord, my might (Psal. XVII). » [Vulg. Psal. XVII, 2 fortitudo mea, "my strength"] But the song of conjunction with God is that song which is sung at nuptials as an epithalamium of bridegroom and bride, that is, the Song of Songs, which is by so much more solemn than all songs, as it is offered at nuptials of a more sublime solemnity. By those songs vices are shunned; but by these each one is enriched with virtues. By those the enemy is guarded against; by these the Lord is embraced with familiar love. Other songs too were sung by divers prophets, for divers persons or causes: as Habakkuk also noted his beforehand for the ignorant. Deborah too sang concerning the victory over Sisera. The three children also sang in the furnace, in which the marvels of Almighty 558AGod are recounted. Jeremiah too sang a song of lamentation concerning the conversion of Jerusalem and of the sanctuary and of his whole people; and many others. But this is called the Song of Songs for this reason: because it is plainly proved to belong to the proper conjunction of Christ the King and the Church, where the Church prays to the Father of the Bridegroom, who is the Word of God, that now not through go-betweens — who are the angels and the prophets, who bore from the Son of God to the Church tidings of the reprobation to come — but that he show him himself at last in person through the mystery of the incarnation; that now she may deserve to embrace his form, beautiful above the sons of men, and to receive from him most sweet kisses, and to handle his most welcome breasts. For she who thus desires is called by the Greek word ἐκκλησία; in Latin she is interpreted congregatio, or better convocatio; in Hebrew 558BSynagoga. According to place or time she is proved to have been called by divers names by the Word of God. Concerning whose appellations we made promise above. For sometimes Jerusalem, sometimes Sion, very often friend, but often beloved. Sometimes sister, or beautiful bride, dove, or spotless, or perfect — so is she known to be called in the person of Christ by the mouth of the prophets and in this book. She is called Jerusalem indeed, because after the wrath of Almighty God she received the peace sent from heaven by the Father, in that she is signified to have some peace. Which peace and conjunction she is said to entreat when she beseeches him: « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth. » But Sion, because out of the valley of the tears of idolatry, where she had fallen in Adam in body, and not in heart, she ascends again to the mount of paradise, 558Cand by offering herself as a mirror to the undivided Trinity for cleanness of heart, she is made conspicuous and lofty. In which Almighty God may delight to walk as upon a fertile mountain, and to scatter the blessed seed of his doctrine; on account of which watchtower or seed is interpreted in her. But she is called friend, because by repudiating the devil she called God back to friendship. But she is named beloved, because after the most bitter hatreds of unbelief she is made beloved of God by the gifts of belief. She is named sister, namely, through the mystery of the assumed flesh, when he is born to the lands out of the same matter of seeing out of which the Church too is begotten. She is pronounced beautiful, namely, being wiped clean through baptism from all the stains of sins, after much Ethiopian and shadowy custom of crimes, 558Dher skin being cleansed and she called back to her native beauty. But she was made bride by joining his body and blood to her own body. By which sacrament she pledged that besides him she loves no other lover with her whole heart, preserving with great zeal his precious ring of faith. For she is proclaimed shapely, by reforming in her soul the form of that image in which she was created. But she is named dove: since all the rapacity which she had been wont to perpetrate with hooked hands as of vultures has been laid aside, now, joined in fellowship with the Holy Spirit, she shines with the whiteness of the simplicity and bounty of doves. And she who in evil teachers had been wont to snatch away from other souls the foods of eternal life, now in good teachers imparts them again from her own most sweet 559Athroat to those who hear, as is the nature of doves. But she is praised as spotless: the old and ancient custom of crimes being laid aside, and the robe of most holy baptism having once been put on in luta, by living spotlessly she has not stained herself further. And having been made the imitator of her Lord Christ, by dying for his name she grows bright with the file of martyrdom and of mortification. She could find no other iron tool stronger, by which the ancient dross of the soul might be filed away, as Apponius says, save the file of martyrdom; by which, in the furnace of torments, the soul — however great the mass of the filth of sins by which it is surrounded — may be renewed in its own blood; by which she may be a perfect dove, and spotless in Christ. Whence indeed she is named perfect also, as one who is to receive all judgment from the Father, namely, in 559BChrist. This Church, therefore, in whom the multitude of all who believe in Christ consists — she who before, namely before the coming of the Bridegroom, lay ignominiously and defiled in her own blood, according to the prophet; nourished amid great cares, and advanced even to the years of puberty, and adorned with his doctrine as with necklaces, but by his conjunction exalted even to the kingdom of heaven. But how ignominiously and trodden down she lay is rebuked through the Prophet. « Thy root and thy generation is of the land of Chanaan. Thy father an Amorrhite, and thy mother a Cethite, in the day wherein thou wast born. In the day of thy birth thy navel was not cut; thou wast not washed with water unto health, nor salted with salt, nor swaddled in cloths. My eye did not spare over thee, to do one of these things for thee, out of pity for thee; but 559Cthou wast lying naked and full of ignominy, trodden down in thy blood. Thy breasts swelled, and thy hair grew, and behold, thy time of lovers; and thou wast lying naked, full of confusion (Ezech. XVIII). » [Vulg. Ezech. XVI, 5 Non pepercit super te oculus (without meus), and for the clause following, sed proiecta es super faciem terrae in abiectione animae tuae] And in what manner, having pity on her, he willed through the coming of his conjunction to do one of these things, he subjoined: « And I sent my mantle, and covered thy ignominy. I washed thee with water and cleansed thy blood from thee, and anointed thee with oil, and clothed thee with a many-colored garment. With fine flour and honey I nourished thee. I girded thee with fine linen, and shod thee with hyacinth, and set a crown of comeliness upon thy head, and rings in thy ears; and I swore to thee, and I entered into a covenant with thee, and thou becamest mine. saith the Lord God (Ibid.). » [Vulg. Ezech. XVI, 8–10 expandi amictum meum super te; vestivi te discoloribus; calceavi te ianthino] Which, although the people of the Jews is accused 559Dof stubbornness, he addresses in her person — every soul that is born through the laver of regeneration, by his will, which soul she had formerly begotten through the sin of the Amorrhite, who is interpreted bitter, that is, of the devil, and through the nefarious custom which in the present place is called Cethite, which is interpreted excess of mind, should have forgotten to be reproved — it is proved by the rebuke with which our merciful Lord Jesus Christ, through his holy appearing, nourished her, gathered though unknowingly out of the aforesaid filth, and adorned her with precious garments and necklaces, and deigned to make her the consort of his kingdom through the wave of most holy baptism, where the glorious coupling of Christ the Son of God and of the Church is celebrated. In which sacred mystery she is washed with the water of ablution; the salt of wisdom being received, she is salted with the faith of the undivided 560Aand coeternal Trinity by the handing over of the brothel; being stripped naked, she is covered with garments by the charism of various virtues. She is filled with the fatness of the Holy Spirit, with the unction of the charism. Where she is nourished with fine flour in the body of Christ, and with honey in his eloquences; where her ears are adorned with the rings of divine understanding, the jewelled crown of martyrdom shining upon her head; she is girded with fine linen, which is the token of purity, that is, with the love of virginity and chastity, which keeps her from all dissolution and, having called her back, binds her fast. They are shod with hyacinth, which in Hebrew is called preparing color, that the feet may be prepared to run together always to the precepts of the Gospel of peace. With these ornaments, namely, the Church desired to be adorned; but because she could not be adorned save through his appearing, therefore she 560Bdesired his visitation. For she had heard, as a bride, through the prophet, in the person of the Bridegroom, that is, of the Word of God the Father, saying: « I will espouse thee to me for ever, I will espouse thee to me in justice, I will espouse thee to me in judgment, I will espouse thee to me in mercy, I will espouse thee to me in faith (Ose. II). » [Vulg. Ose. II, 19–20 sponsabo three times, and includes et in miserationibus] And through Isaiah likewise the voice of the Church herself says: « He has set a mitre upon me as upon a bride, and has clothed me with the garments of salvation; as a bridegroom decked with a crown, and as a bride adorned with her jewels, he has encompassed me (Isa. LXI). » [Vulg. Isa. LXI, 10 lacks Posuit mihi mitram sicut sponsae and reads et indumento iustitiae circumdedit me] And Zacharias, in announcing glory of this kind to the trembling Church, says: « Fear not, daughter of Sion; behold, thy King cometh to thee, meek and gentle, sitting upon the colt of an ass; he himself is Savior and Redeemer (Zach. IX). » [Vulg. Zach. IX, 9 Exsulta satis, filia Sion … ecce rex tuus veniet tibi iustus et salvator; ipse pauper, et ascendens super asinam et super pullum filium asinae] All these patriarchs and prophets, therefore, 560Csang of the future, ineffable coupling of Christ and the Church. But the Apostle set it forth as already celebrated, saying: « Husbands, love your wives, as Christ loved the Church, who delivered himself up for her, that he might present to himself a spotless Church, not having spot or wrinkle (Ephes. V). » [Vulg. Ephes. V, 27 gloriosam Ecclesiam, "a glorious Church"] And again to the Corinthians: « For I have espoused you to one husband, that I may present you a chaste virgin to Christ (II Cor. XI). » And in another place he thus exhorts the faithful: « You are the body of Christ, and flesh of his flesh (I Cor. XII). » [Vulg. I Cor. XII, 27 Vos autem estis corpus Christi, et membra de membro, "and members of a member"] To her, namely, who is to be confirmed by so great and such witnesses, all the offices of the nuptials are to be applied. Where there are at hand also the friends of the Bridegroom, the companions too of the bride, and the manners as well of the singers, who with sweet modulation may sing the power of the Bridegroom or praise the beauty of the bride, by which she obtained the bounty 560Dof the Bridegroom: how the exalted one humbled himself from that height of deity to the mire of the deep, that he might join to his own majesty the humble and cast-down matter of flesh, lifted up to the summit of the heavens. And it must be noted that sometimes Holy Scripture calls him Lord, sometimes father, sometimes bridegroom. For when he wills to be feared, he names himself Lord; when he wills to be honored, father; when he wills to be loved, bridegroom. He himself says through the prophet: « If I am Lord, where is my fear? If I am Father, where is my honor? (Mal. I) » [Vulg. Mal. I, 6 Si ergo pater ego sum, ubi est honor meus? et si Dominus ego sum, ubi est timor meus? — the two clauses in the reverse order] And again he says: « I have espoused thee to me in justice and in faith; or assuredly, I have remembered the day of thy espousal in the desert (Ose. II). » [Vulg. Ose. II, 20 sponsabo te mihi in fide; the second clause answers rather to Jerem. II, 2 recordatus sum tui … et caritatem desponsationis tuae] And what is there with God of "when," and when 561Ait is not. But because he wills first to be feared, that he may be honored, and first to be honored, that one may come to his love, he both names himself Lord on account of fear, and father on account of honor, and bridegroom on account of love — that through fear one may come to honor, and through honor one may arrive at his love. By how much, then, honor is worthier than fear, by so much the more does God rejoice to be called father than Lord. And by how much, then, love is dearer than honor, by so much the more does God rejoice to be bridegroom than father. In this book, therefore, the Lord and the Church are named not lord and handmaid, but bridegroom and bride, so that service may be rendered not to fear alone, not to reverence alone, but also to love, and that in outward words the inward affection may be stirred up. When he names himself Lord, he indicates that we are created. When he names himself father, he indicates 561Bthat we are joined. But it is more to be joined to God than to be created and adopted. But thus generally, in the voice of the Church, the coming of the Lord is awaited in this work, so that also specially each single soul may receive the entrance of God into her heart as the approach of the bridegroom into the bridal chamber. And it must be known that in this book four speaking persons are introduced: namely the bridegroom and the bride, and the maidens with the bride, and the flocks of companions with the bridegroom. For the bride herself is the perfect Church, the bridegroom the Lord, as has often been said. But the maidens with the bride are the souls that are beginning, and coming to maturity through a new zeal. But the companions of the bridegroom are either the angels, who often appeared to men, coming to them, or assuredly all the perfect men in the Church, who have known how to announce the truth 561Cto men; but they who singly are maidens or companions are, all together, the bride, who all together are the Church — although these names can also be so taken with respect to each single believer. For he who already loves the Lord perfectly is the bride; he who preaches the bridegroom is a companion; he who as yet, being new, follows the way of the good is a maiden. Let us imitate, therefore, that we may be the bride. If we are not yet able for this, let us be companions. If neither have we attained this, let us at least come together to this bridal chamber as maidens. But it must be known, before we come to the peace of conjunction, that the most wise Solomon showed in figure and in riddles whatsoever, from the beginning of the world unto the end, the Word of God has done or shall do in mysteries toward the Church. In which song 561Dall the things that are narrated, covered over with mysteries, are taught to have been revealed and fulfilled in the incarnation of the Word — where the human race, dashed down, is raised up; fettered, it is loosed; corrupted, it is reformed to the integrity of virginity; driven out of paradise, it is restored: out of a captive, free; out of a stranger, a citizen; out of a handmaid, a mistress; out of the most vile, a queen — and it is shown to have been made, by the kindness of its Creator the Word of God Christ, a bride worthy to receive his kisses, and, made one spirit together with him, to reign exalted in the heavenly places. Which sublimity is known to have been celebrated by the humbling of the Son of God, as the teacher of the Gentiles teaches, saying of Christ: « He humbled himself, that he might exalt us (Phil. II). » [Vulg. Phil. II, 8 humiliavit semetipsum, factus oboediens usque ad mortem; ut nos exaltaret is not in the received text] For it is not reckoned an injury to a craftsman, when a precious ring has fallen into a pit of the body, 562Ato lay aside his robe for a little while and go down to seek it, and, having found it, to restore it again to his right hand. For whatsoever the foolish and impious heathendom — whose mist of unbelief has blinded the eyes of the heart — reckons humble, whatsoever most vile, whatsoever despised and unworthy: this is proved to be glorious, this marvellous, this, I say, magnificent, in the sight of the divine clemency and of all the heavenly powers. For what immense power, what sweetness of goodness, what surpassing fragrance of compassion out of all the works of the paternal excellence, admired by the angels and by the choirs of all the saints, is offered with lofty voices and perpetual praises, unless that the Lord should couple to himself a truly poor little handmaid — as an outsider, a mortal one — the Lord Jesus Christ, through the assumption of flesh, the Church, that is, the Word of God 562Bthe soul, under the title of conjunction? Since therefore we have rather often spoken of bridegroom and bride, of the Lord and the Church, and rightly, according to the poet, we lighten the way with speech: Both hope and faith accompany my step into steep places, More kindly at last we come through to the highest citadel.

That we may now open out the very text of the divine oracle: as maidens, as companions, let us hear the words of the bridegroom, let us hear the words of the bride, and in their discourses let us learn the fervor of love. And so holy Church, long awaiting the coming of the Lord, long thirsting for the fountain of life — how she wishes to see the presence of her bridegroom, how she desires and speaks.

CAPUT PRIMUM.

« Osculetur me osculo oris sui. » Angelos ad eamdem 562CDominus, patriarchas ad illam et prophetas miserat, spiritualia dona deferentes, sed ipsa vero munera non per servos sponsi, sed per ipsum jam sponsum percipere quaerebat. Ponamus ante oculos omne genus humanum ab exordio mundi usque ad finem mundi, totam videlicet Ecclesiam unam esse personam: quae arrhas spirituali dono perceperat, sed tamen sponsi sui praesentiam quaerebat, quae dicit. « Osculetur me osculo oris sui, » Suspirans enim sancta Ecclesia pro adventu Mediatoris Dei et hominum, pro adventu Redemptoris sui ad Patrem verba orationis facit, ut Filium dirigat, et sua illam praesentia illustret, ut eidem Ecclesiae non jam per ora prophetarum, sed suo ore locutionem faciat, ac si apertius dicat: Tangat me dulcedine praesentiae 562Dsuae, quam saepius a prophetis promissam audivi. Unde et de eodem sponso in Evangelio scriptum est (Matth. V), cum sederet in monte, et sublimium praeceptorum verba faceret: « Aperiens hic Jesus os suum dixit. » Ac si dicatur: Tunc os suum aperuit, qui prius ad exhortationem Ecclesiae aperuit ora prophetarum. Cerne libenter, Caesar Auguste, quomodo ab exordio mundi ab Adam scilicet, imo ab Abel justo, usque ad Joannem Baptistam semper Ecclesia Christum in eis qui altioris intellectus erant quaesivit, et per omnes patriarchas et prophetas ad Patrem omnipotentem lacrymabili voce clamavit, ejus sibi oscula propinari, quem et in Jordanis baptismo vox Patris de nube, et Joannes digito monstravit, dicendo: 563A« Ecce Agnus Dei qui tollit peccatum mundi (Joann. I). » Et Pater ipse de nube: « Hic est Filius meus, inquit, dilectus in quo mihi complacui (Matth. III). » Unum autem de multis amicis sponsi praeparantem sponsam Joannem intellige, cujus praesentia Ecclesia Christi oscula sancta suscepit, ipso testante de Christo. « Qui habet sponsam, sponsus est. Amicus autem sponsi gaudio gaudet ad vocem sponsi (Joan. III). » Miratur videlicet Joannes, cum vidit eum propter aquarum sanctificationem ad baptismum venientem, qui ait: « Ego a te debeo baptizari, et tu venis ad me? » (Matth. III.) Gaudio vero gaudet, audiens vocem ejus dicentis: « Sine modo, sic enim oportet nos implere omnem justitiam (Ibid.). » Ubi completae sunt preces Ecclesiae 563Bjam olim promissae ad Deum Patrem Domini nostri Jesu Christi dicentis: « Osculetur me osculo oris sui. » Sed dum ergo sancta Ecclesia incarnandum sponsum adhuc absentem desiderat, subito intuetur praesentem. Habet enim gratia Redemptoris nostri, ut cum de illo quaerentes loquimur, ejus praesentia perfruamur, sic de duobus Cleopha videlicet et alio, sicut in Evangelio scriptum est. Unde subjungit: « Quia meliora sunt ubera tua vino, » « et odor unguentorum tuorum super omnia aromata (Cant. III). » Vinum fuit scientia legis, scientia prophetarum, sed veniens Dominus quia sapientiam suam per carnem voluit praedicari, quasi fecit eam in carnis ubera lactescere. Et quam in divinitate sua capere minime poteramus, incarnatione ejus agnosceremus: 563Cunde non immerito ejus ubera laudantur, quia praedicationis ejus condescensio hoc egit in cordibus nostris, quod doctrina legis agere minime valuit. Plus enim nos nutrivit incarnationis praedicatio, quam legis doctrina. « Et odor unguentorum tuorum super omnia aromata. » Unguenta Domini virtutes sunt. Unguentum Domini, Spiritus sanctus fuit, de quo ei per Prophetam dicitur: « Unxit te Deus, Deus tuus, oleo laetitiae prae consortibus tuis (Ps. XLIV). » Hoc oleo tunc unctus est cum incarnatus est. Non enim prius homo exstitit et postmodum spiritum sanctum accepit, sed quia Spiritu sancto mediante incarnatus est, eodem hoc oleo tunc unctus est, cum homo creatus est. Odor ergo unguentorum ejus flagrantia Spiritus sancti est, qui ex illo procedens, in illo permansit. 563DOdor unguentorum ejus flagrantia virtutum quas operatus est. Habuit haec Ecclesia aromata quia habuit multa spiritus dona, quae in domo Dei, id est in congregatione sanctorum odorem bonae opinionis redoleret. Sed odor unguentorum tuorum super omnia aromata, quia flagrantia virtutum sponsi, quae per incarnationem ejus facta est, vicit praedicamenta legis, quae in arrhis ab sponso fuerant praerogata. Illa legis aromata per angelos ministrata sunt, istud unguentum per praesentiam sponsi datum est, Tanto quippe amplius ad intellectum credit Ecclesia, quanto et amplioris visionis gratia meruit illustrari. Hoc autem quod generaliter de cuncta Ecclesia diximus, nunc specialiter 564Ade unaquaque anima sentiamus. Ponamus ante oculos esse quamdam animam donorum studiis inhaerentem, intellectum ex aliena praedicatione percipientem, quae per divinam gratiam etiam ipsa illustrari desiderat, ut aliquando etiam per se intelligat, quae nihil se intelligere nisi per verba praedicatorum considerat, et dicat: « Osculetur me osculo oris sui. » Ac si dicat: Ipsa me tangat intus, ut cognoscam intelligentiam, et non jam praedicatorum vocibus, sed internae ejus gratiae tactu perfruar. Quasi osculo oris sui osculabatur Moysen Dominus, cum per fiduciam familiaris gratiae intellectum porrigeret. Unde scriptum est. « Si fuerit propheta in somnio loquor ad eum et non sicut famulo meo Moysi. Ore enim ad os loquor ei (Num. XII). » Os quippe 564Bad os loqui, quasi osculari est, et interna intelligentia mente tangere. Sequitur: « Meliora sunt ubera tua vino. » Ubera Dei sunt sicut prius diximus, humillima praedicatio incarnationis ejus; sapientia autem saeculi, quasi quoddam vinum est. Inebriat enim mentem, quia ab intellectu humilitatis alienam reddit. Quasi quodam vino inebriantur philosophi, dum per saecularem sapientiam vulgi morem transeunt. Quam sapientiam sancta Ecclesia despiciat, humillimam praedicationis Dominicae incarnationem appetat. Plus ei sapiat quod per infirmitatem carnis ejus nutritur, quam quod mundus hic per elationem falsae prudentiae extollitur, et dicat: « Quia meliora sunt ubera tua vino, » id est humillima incarnationis tuae praedicatio elatam 564Cmundi sapientiam superat. Unde scriptum est: « Quod infirmum est Dei, fortius est quam homines. Et quod stultum est Dei sapientius est quam homines (I Cor. I). » Sed quia ipsi hujus saeculi sapientes nonnunquam videntur quibusdam virtutibus studere, videmus enim plerosque habere charitatem servare mansuetudinem, honestatem exteriorem in omnibus exercere. Quas tamen virtutes, non ut Deo, sed ut hominibus placeant exhibent. Quae idcirco virtutes non sunt, quia Deo placere non appetunt. Olent tamen in humanis naribus dum humano judicio bonam opinionem reddunt. Sed comparentur haec vero odori Redemptoris nostri, comparentur veris virtutibus et dicatur: « Et odor unguentorum tuorum super omnia aromata, » id est flagrantia virtutum 564Dtuarum, omnem speciem virtutum sapientium mundi superat, quia videlicet fictas eorum imagines ex veritate transcendit. Quia secundo loco sentiri hoc quod dictum est de unaquaque anima diximus, adhuc eumdem sensum, si possumus, adjuvante Deo, subtilius exsequamur. Omnis anima quae timet Deum, jam sub jugo ejus est. Sed adhuc longe, quia timet. Nam tantum quisque ad Deum prope fit, quando poenam timoris amittit, et gratiam de illo charitatis percipit. Ponamus ante oculos animam electi cujuslibet, quae continuo desiderio in amorem visionis sponsi accenditur, quia quod in hac vita perfecte percipere non valet, contemplatur ejus celsitudinem et ex ipso amore compungitur.
