Finally, when she had said: « Let him kiss me with the kiss of his mouth, » and had subjoined: « For thy breasts are better than wine, » where the exceeding joy of love fulfilled is shown by the handling of the breasts, straightway she added, according to the Hebrew: fragrant with the best ointments — which things, although they have been set forth mellifluously above by the highest expositor in shining discourse, we can nevertheless repeat again according to the opinions of certain men. The wine, they say, was the word of the messengers, brought down by angels, who spoke to the prophets or in the prophets, as Zacharias the prophet says (Cap. II). And he said to the Angel « who spoke in me, » [Vulg. Zach. I, 9 dixit ad me angelus, "the angel said to me"] which gladdened the heart of the Church, 566Dhearing of the coming of him whom she desired. But when with mouth and hands she handled his body and blood, she knew breasts better — flowing with the milk of the apostolic doctrine and of the Gospels, where is all the divine perfection which is now ministered — than the wine of the Old Testament and of the law, which according to the Apostle brought no one through to perfection. For although perfect apostolic men, who are teachers to the Christian people appointed beforehand, are understood to be the breasts of Christ, whom Christ nourishes as little ones, yet it will not be unfitting: he understands properly the two breasts of Christ to be the Baptist and the Evangelist, the two Johns, who after the kisses of the aforesaid sacrament were proffered to the Church as fullest words, since the one shows him to the Church as alone taking away the sin 567Aof the world, and as the lamb, and the other as, in the beginning, the Word, God, abiding with God the Father — who through an unspotted life so clung in his love as breasts to the bosom, while the one shows to the Church, still little and suckling, the true man under the name of the unspotted lamb, and the other, from the effect of the creating of all things, shows him the maker, in the beginning the Word, God, through whom all things were created. No taste of sweetness of this kind, therefore, had until this time come to the Church, that in the meekness of the lamb, the king being terrible, the troops of the wolves, the demons, should be put to flight — whose presence strikes terror into all earthly and ethereal powers, who clothes others with the covering of the Holy Spirit and himself has need of none, who lead the Church, lifted up 567Bfrom earthly things, to the fellowship of the Father through the assumption of flesh, with which he was made one in mind and in matter, sitting at the right hand of the Father, with whom in the beginning was God the Word. By the milk, therefore, of his doctrine conferring eternal life — the aforesaid breasts of Christ our Lord flowing to the beloved Church, from whose milky bosom eternal life, which Adam had lost, was poured into the apostles through the whiteness of doctrine — which life, if the Church has received it through the breasts in believers, she holds out daily also to her own posterity, as butter made from milk, through the interpretation of the word. For first it contains a mystery through the nature of milk, that is, the liquid of what is chaste; and when the liquid has been worked by art it yields butter: so also the milk of the apostolic doctrine, when it has found a master, declares 567Cthat it contains within itself the undivided Trinity. Unless the soul shall have used these foods unceasingly, it will not be able to live for ever. Which food the Church received from the aforesaid breasts in the sacrament of baptism, where by the threefold confession of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit she knows the scent of that knowledge, since it has often been said to her that the milk of doctrine has conferred the threefold marrow of redemption: namely, the most white liquid of baptism, and the sweet juice of the body of Christ, and the richest butter, the oil of the sacred chrism, through which the Holy Spirit is poured in. Of which fatness the Church, in another Prophet, prays that the marrow of her soul be filled, saying: « Let my soul be filled as with marrow and fatness (Psal. LXII) » Whose scent raises up again souls wounded by sins; 567Dby the power of whose fatness the fragrance hidden in the aforesaid breasts, which after the kisses received she is taught to praise in the following little verse, saying: « Fragrant with the best ointments. » That ointment in the Old Testament, compounded with God for master, was indeed of good odor, that is, of merit; with it kings, prophets, and priests were anointed. But by as much as a living man differs from a picture smeared over with the dyes of colors, by so much is there a difference between what the Church receives by being born again in the Spirit and that whose figure the Synagogue was receiving in the body — whose things were so great that scarcely in one nation, Judea, and to a few, did it grant the primacy. But this ointment of the Church bears in itself so much power, of fragrance and of medicine at once, that it renders all believers most sound, 568Aappoints them kings and priests, and the scent of the knowledge of it has filled the whole world from the rising of the sun even to its setting. What other fragrance can it be understood to contain within itself, save the name of Christ? Of which it follows: « Ointment poured out is thy name. » For as men shut in amid a multitude of putrid corpses are endangered by disease, so it had befallen the inhabitants of this world through Adam, the devil bringing in a plural number of gods by the mouth of the serpent, saying: « You shall be as gods (Gen. II). » But when the name of the one true God had been brought in, the little vessel having been laid up in a body through the mystery of the incarnation — which vessel, being broken by the blows of the nails and of the lance, by the scent of the knowledge of him every stench of the devil's doctrine was wiped away from the whole world. And that great name which in the people of Israel alone was known 568Bthrough the anointing of the chrism — now, as when the vessel of ointment is poured out in a house the whole house is filled with the scent, so also from the time of the passion of our Lord Jesus Christ the whole world is proved to be filled with the knowledge of the name of the one true God, Christ, so that through all the nations of the Gentiles the fragrance of the virtues runs back daily like the scent of ointment, « while the dead rise at the invocation of his name, the blind see, the lame walk, the dumb speak, » demons are put to flight, every sickness falls away, languor is driven off, in the power of this name invoked. Behold with what odors the love and affection of Christ kindles the young maidens' souls — of whom, namely, he now says: « Therefore the young maidens have loved thee. » These things having been tasted beforehand, let us return to the sense above of the most sweet doctor. For it follows: « Therefore the young maidens 568Chave loved thee. » What do we take the young maidens to be in this place, save the souls of the elect renewed through baptism? For the life of the sinner belongs to the old man, the life of the just man to the new. Since therefore the ointment poured itself outward, it made young maidens burn in its love, because it showed souls renewed and aflame with desire for it. The age of boyhood is not yet suited to love. He is a boy who has not yet begun the zeal of an ardent life. He is an old man who had indeed begun, but has left off. Since therefore neither do these burn in the Lord who have not yet begun, nor these who have already begun the faith but have grown cold, the boyish and the aged life being set aside. The young maidens are said to run, that is, those souls which are in the very fervor of love. Yet this we can also understand otherwise. For adolescence can be referred to weakness. 568DFor the youthful ages are the orders of the angels, who have been conquered by no debility, overcome by no infirmity. Let it therefore be said: « Ointment poured out is thy name, therefore the young maidens have loved thee, » that is: through thy incarnation thou hast poured out the knowledge of thee outwardly; therefore weak souls have strength to love human nature. For those highest Powers, like the youthful ages, love thee even there where thou art not poured out, because there too they see thee, where thou containest thyself in the state of thy divinity. Thou therefore who by those highest orders, as by youthful ages, art seen even when not poured out, art poured out outwardly for the sake of men, that thou mayest be loved also by young maidens, that is, by weak minds. It follows.

569A« Draw me. » Everyone who is drawn is led forth unwilling, either as one not able or as one not willing. But he who says, draw me, has something which he is able to do, has something which he is not able to do. Human nature wills to follow the Lord, but, overcome by the habit of infirmity, it has not the strength to follow as it ought. It sees, therefore, that there is one thing in itself whither it strives, another thing in itself which it is not able to do, and rightly it says, draw me. Paul had seen himself as willing and yet not able, when he said: « With the mind I serve the law of God, but with the flesh the law of sin (Rom. VII); » and: « I see another law in my members, fighting against the law of my mind. (Ibid.) » Since therefore there is something which incites us, and another which weighs us down, let us say: « Draw me; we will run after thee in the odor of thy ointments. » [Vulg. in odorem, accusative, "to the odor of thy ointments"] We run in the odor of God's ointments when, 569Bbreathed upon by his spiritual gifts, we gape after the love of the vision of him. But it must be known that, in the fact that men follow God, sometimes they walk, sometimes they run, sometimes they run strongly, as it were after God. He walks who follows lukewarmly. He runs who follows fervently. He runs perfectly who follows perseveringly. For the heart was immovable for the following of God, and was unwilling to walk after him, when the coming of the Lord appeared in the world and moved human minds from their insensible standing. Whence it is written: « His feet stood, and the earth was moved (Psal. CVI). » But to these not motion but running is spoken of, because it is not enough that we follow, unless we also run by desiring. But because neither does it suffice to run, unless it be run perfectly, Paul says: « So run that 569Cyou may obtain (Rom. IX). » But some, while they run too much, slip away into indiscretion. For they are wise more than is needful, and they set behind them the judgments of him whom they were following. Whence well, when it was said we will run, there was set before it, after thee. For they run after God who consider his judgments, who prefer his will to their own, and who strive to come to him under worthy work with discretion. Hence the Prophet, considering the will of God and following it, says: « My soul hath cleaved after thee (Ps. LXXII). » Here it is said to Peter as he gives counsel: « Go back behind me, Satan. For thou savorest not the things that are of God, but the things that are of men (Matth. XVI). » [Vulg. Vade post me, Satana, "Go behind me, Satan"] Since therefore perfect souls behold the judgments of God with the utmost caution, and presume to run before them neither through torpor nor through indiscreet fervor, well 569Dis it said: « We will run after thee in the odor of thy ointments. » For then do we run after God, when we both follow by loving and, by fearing, anticipate the divine judgments. It follows: « The king hath brought me into his bedchamber; we will exult and rejoice in thee. » [Vulg. in cellaria sua, "into his storerooms"] The Church of God is like a certain house of the king, and that house has a gate, has an ascent, has dining-halls, has bedchambers. Everyone who within the Church has faith has also entered the gate of that house, because as the gate opens the rest of the house, so faith is the door of the remaining virtues. Everyone who within the Church has hope has already come to the ascent of the house. For hope lifts up the heart, that it may seek high things and forsake the lowest. Everyone set in that house 570Awho has charity walks about, as it were, in the dining-halls. For charity is broad, which is stretched out even to the love of enemies. Everyone set in the Church who now searches out the high secrets, who now considers the hidden judgments, will enter as it were into the bedchamber. Of the gate of that house a certain one said: « Open ye to me the gates of justice; having entered into them, I will confess to the Lord (Ps. CXVII). » Of the ascent of hope he said: « He hath disposed ascents in his heart (Ps. LXXXIII). » Of the broad dining-halls of that house it is said: « Thy commandment is exceeding broad (Ps. CXVIII). » In the broad commandment charity is specially designated. Of the bedchamber of the king he was speaking who said: « My secret to myself (Isa. XXIV). » And elsewhere: « I heard secret words which it is not lawful for a man to speak (II Cor. XII). » [Vulg. audivit, "he heard"] The first approach of that house, 570Btherefore, is the gate of faith; the second advance, the ascent of hope; the third, the breadth of charity; the fourth is now the perfection of charity unto the knowledge of the secrets of God. Since therefore holy Church, in her perfect members, in her holy teachers, in those who are now full and rooted in the mysteries of God, has come as it were to the high secrets, and, still set in this life, already penetrates those things, she says: « The king hath brought me into his bedchamber. » For through the prophets, through the apostles, through the teachers, who, set in this life, already penetrated the high secrets of that life, the Church had entered into the bedchamber of that king. And it is to be observed carefully that she does not say, into the bedchamber of the bridegroom, but into the bedchamber of the king. For by naming the king she wishes to show the reverence of the secrets, because the more open 570Cthe bedchamber is, the greater is the reverence to be shown to those things into which one enters: lest therefore, while each one knows the secrets of God, while he searches out the hidden judgments, while he is raised up to the heights of contemplation, he be lifted up and slip away into pride, it is said to be the king's bedchamber that he enters. To whom the greater reverence is to be shown, the more the soul is led to the knowing of his secrets, so that each one who advances, who now, exalted by grace, has come to the high secrets, may attend to himself and by that very advance be the more humbled. Whence Ezekiel also, as often as he is led to the contemplating of high things, is called Son of man. As if it were said to him: But attend to what thou art, and be not lifted up because of those things unto which thou art raised. But it belongs to few in the Church to search out and 570Dcomprehend these high and hidden judgments of God: nevertheless, while we see that strong men are able to come to so great a wisdom that they contemplate the secrets of God in their hearts, let us little ones also have confidence that at some time we shall come to pardon, at some time to his grace. Whence also, from the words of the young maidens, it is subjoined. « We will exult and rejoice in thee. » While the Church, in those who are perfect, enters the bedchamber of the king, the young maidens promise themselves the hope of exultation. Because while the strong come to the contemplating of high things, the weak take hope of pardon for their sins, therefore she says: « The king hath brought me into his bedchamber; we will exult and rejoice in thee. » -- « Mindful of thy breasts above wine. The upright love thee. » This bridegroom has 571Abreasts; he who is also called king for reverence' sake has breasts — holy men cleaving to him with the heart. The breasts are fixed in the narrow part of the bosom, and they draw nourishment for what is without, unto those whom he has nourished abroad. Holy men, therefore, are the breasts of the bridegroom, because they draw from the inmost things and nourish outwardly. His breasts are the apostles and all the preachers of the Church: the wine, as we also said above, was in the prophets, the wine was in the law. But because fuller commandments were given through the apostles than had been given through the prophets, she says: « Mindful of thy breasts above wine; » Because they who can fulfill those things which are commanded in the New Testament, they without doubt transcend the knowledge of the law. Yet this we can also understand otherwise: « Mindful of thy breasts above wine; » there are many who indeed have the wine 571Bof wisdom, but have not the knowledge of humility. This knowledge puffs up, because charity does not build up. [Vulg. scientia inflat, caritas vero aedificat, "knowledge puffeth up, but charity edifieth"] But there are many who so have the wine of knowledge that they know how to consider the gifts of doctrine. For the gifts of spiritual grace are like certain teats in the bosom, which subtly minister to spiritual men by hidden passages and nourish them. Therefore she says: « Mindful of thy breasts above wine. » Because these, who know how to remember the gifts of thy grace, so as not to ascribe to themselves what they are wise in, and are not lifted up, on account of the same wisdom which they have received, above those who are lifted up and puffed up on account of their own wisdom. For it is more to be wise humbly than to be wise proudly. For neither is it truly to be wise, not to be wise humbly; she says: « Mindful therefore of thy breasts above wine. » Because those who know how to consider 571Cthe gifts of spiritual grace transcend those who indeed have knowledge, but have not in memory the knowing of the gifts. Openly, therefore, is it to say: « Mindful therefore of thy breasts above wine, » because humility is stronger than knowledge. Wine is the knowledge which inebriates the memory of the breasts, which inebriates him whom it calls back to the knowing of the gifts. Let her say: « Mindful therefore of thy breasts above wine, » because humility conquers the abundance of knowledge. The upright love thee. No one, he says, loves thee save the upright. And no one is upright, save he who loves thee. For everyone who does good works through fear, even if he is perfect in the work, is not upright in desire. For he would wish that there were not that which he fears, so that he might not do good works. But he who does good works through love is upright both in work and in desire. 571DBut the sweetness of love is hidden from those who fear. Whence it is written: « How great is the multitude of thy sweetness, O Lord, which thou hast hidden for them that fear thee, and hast perfected them for them that hope in thee (Ps. XXX). » [Vulg. perfecisti eis qui sperant in te, "thou hast perfected [it] for them that hope in thee"] For sweetness is unknown to those who fear God; to those who love, it becomes known. He therefore who has striven through love to be upright, his love is perfect, so that he does not fear the judge when he comes, so that whatever he has heard of eternal punishments, he does not dread. Whence Paul also, while he awaited the coming of the judge, while he sought the rewards of eternal life, said: « Which God hath prepared not only for me, but for all who love his coming (II Tim. IV). » [Vulg. II Tim. IV, 8 non solum autem mihi, sed et his qui diligunt adventum eius, "and not only to me, but to them also that love his coming"; the opening Quae praeparavit Deus is not in that verse] For eternal rewards are prepared for those who love the judge, because everyone who knows that he does 572Aevil works fears the judge when he comes. But he who presumes upon his own works seeks the coming of the judge. Rewards therefore are prepared for those who await the coming of God and love his coming, because they do not love the coming of the judge save as presuming upon their own cause. But all certitude of uprightness is in love, and therefore it is rightly said: The upright love thee. It follows:

« I am black, but comely, O daughter of Jerusalem, as the tabernacles of Cedar, as the skins of Solomon. Do not consider me that, » etc. [Vulg. filiae Hierusalem, plural, "O ye daughters of Jerusalem"] We know that in the first beginnings of the Church, when the grace of our Redeemer had been preached, some out of Judea believed, others did not believe. But those who believed were despised by the unbelieving and suffered persecution, and were judged to have departed, as it were, into the way 572Bof the Gentiles. Whence the Church cries out in those same ones against those who were not converted. « I am black, but comely, O daughter of Jerusalem. » Black indeed by a true judgment, but comely through the illumination of grace. How black? « As the tabernacles of Cedar. » For Cedar was the second of the stock of Ishmael, and the tabernacles of Cedar were Esau's. How then black? As the tabernacles of Cedar. Because in your sight I have been judged after the likeness of the Gentiles, that is, after the likeness of sinners. How then comely? « As the skins of Solomon. » It is reported that Solomon, when he built the temple, covered all the vessels of the temple with skins. But doubtless the skins of Solomon could be comely, which were fitted to the tabernacle. Which, if they were in his tabernacle, could not but be comely 572Cfor the service of the king. But because Solomon is interpreted peaceable, we understand him to be the true Solomon himself, because all souls cleaving to God are the skins of Solomon, mortifying themselves and bringing themselves back into the service of the king of peace. But in your judgment I am as the tabernacles of Cedar, because I am judged to have departed, as it were, into the way of the Gentiles; but according to the truth I am as the skins of Solomon, because I cleave unto the service of the king. Do not consider me that I am brown, because the sun hath discolored me. That part which had not believed regarded as a sinner that part which had believed in Christ. But let this one say:

« Do not consider me that I am brown, because the sun hath discolored me. » The sun is the Lord himself; he himself, coming, discolored me; by his precepts he showed that I was not fair 572Din the precepts of the law. Whom the sun touches more closely, him it has discolored: so also the Lord, coming, discolored him whom through his grace he touched more strictly, because the more we draw near to grace, the more we know ourselves to be sinners. Let us see Paul, coming out of Judea, discolored in the sun. But if, wishing to be justified in Christ, we ourselves also have been found sinners. He who finds himself a sinner in Christ is found discolored in the sun. But behold, that part which believed out of Judea suffered persecution from the unbelieving Jews, afflicted with many tribulations and pressed. Whence it follows: « The sons of my mother have fought against me. » Because the sons of the synagogue who remained in unbelief waged a war of persecution against the faithful 573Aof the synagogue. But while that part which came out of Judea to the faith suffers persecution, it departed to the preaching of the Gentiles. Whence it follows: « They have set me keeper for the vineyards. My vineyard I have not kept. » Because, while those who are in Judea persecute me, they have made me a keeper in the churches. « My vineyard I have not kept, » because I forsook Judea. Whence Paul also says: « To you the word of God had been sent, but because you have judged yourselves unworthy, behold we turn to the Gentiles (Act. XIII). » [Vulg. Act. XIII, 46 vobis oportebat primum loqui verbum Dei; sed quoniam repellitis illud et indignos vos iudicastis aeternae vitae, "to you it behoved us first to speak the word of God: but because you reject it, and judge yourselves unworthy of eternal life"] As if he should say: We wished to keep our own vineyard, but because you yourselves have rejected us, you send us over to the keeping of other men's vineyards. These things which we have said of the Synagogue converted to the faith, let us now say of the Church called out of the Gentiles to the faith. « I am black, but comely, O daughter of Jerusalem. » 573BThe Church, coming out of the Gentiles, considers the souls of the faithful which she found, which she also calls daughters of Jerusalem. For Jerusalem is called the vision of peace. She considers what she was, considers what she has become, and confesses her past faults, that she be not proud. She confesses her present life, that she be not ungrateful, and says: « I am black, but comely. » Black through merit, comely through grace. Black through her past life, comely through the manner of life that follows. How black? As the tabernacles of Cedar. For the tabernacles of Cedar were the tabernacles of the Gentiles, they were the tabernacles of darkness. And to the Gentiles it was said: « You were heretofore darkness, but now light in the Lord (Eph. V). » How comely? As the skins of Solomon. We have been macerated through penance; and being mortified through penance, 573Cas a skin it is led into the service of the king. All who afflict themselves through penance make themselves members of Christ. The members of Christ, therefore, afflicted through penance, are the skins of Solomon, because they become mortified flesh. But behold, there were faithful in Judea who disdained to come to the faith of the Gentiles. Whence they rebuke Peter also, that he had received Cornelius. Whence here too the Church of the Gentiles subjoins: « Do not consider me that I am brown. » Do not despise the heathenism of my unbelief, do not despise my former sins, do not regard what I was. Why? « because the sun hath discolored me. » For the sun discolors him upon whom it presses itself more closely and more strictly. God, when he holds strict judgment, when he shows forth his strictness more fully, also discolors, while he shines 573Dmore fully, because while he exercises his strictness more subtly, he strictly adjudges. For the sun as it were holds back its rays when it mercifully desires our works. As it were strictly it shows forth its power when it strictly weighs our works. Let the Church therefore say: Thence am I brown, thence a sinner, because the sun has discolored me, because when my Creator forsook me, I fell through sorrow. But thou, O afflicted one so forsaken, what hast thou merited?

What hast thou obtained from the gift? The sons of my mother have fought against me. The sons of the mother are the apostles. For the mother of men is the Jerusalem above. They fought against the Church, when out of unbelief, in the faith of their preaching, they pierced her through as with certain lances. Whence Paul also, as a certain fighter, says 574A(II Thess. II). « Destroying the counsels of thoughts, and every height that exalteth itself against the knowledge of God. » [Vulg. II Cor. X, 5 consilia destruentes, without cogitationum] He who destroys height is assuredly a fighter; these fighters, therefore, these sons of the mother Jerusalem, warred down the Church from her error, that they might found her unto justice: « The sons of my mother have fought against me. » And what did they do in fighting? « They have set me keeper in the vineyards. » The vineyards of the Church are the virtues which bear fruit, because while the vices fight it out within me, they storm as it were my own evils within me. They gave me fruitfulness and the pursuits of the virtues; they made me keeper in the vineyards, that I might bring forth fruitfulness. After the storming, let her say specially: « My vineyard I have not kept. » The vineyard of the Church is the ancient habit of error. Which, while one is set as keeper unto the virtues, he forsakes — 574Bthat vineyard, that ancient habit of his error. We have spoken of the heathen world converted; let us now speak generally of the whole Church together, and specially of what is to be thought concerning each single soul. Depraved hearers are wont to consider their teachers not as what they are, but as what they were. But sound teachers both confess what they were and bring forward what they are, so that they neither hide that they were sinners, nor again, like ungrateful men, deny the gifts. Let the Church therefore say in these. « I am black, but comely. » Black through myself, comely through the gift. Black from the past; comely, from that I have been made so in things to come. How black? how comely? Black « as the tabernacles of Cedar, comely as the skins of Solomon. » And it is not just that anyone be weighed from his past life, and that there be not more regarded what he was, but what he is. Whence 574Cshe subjoins: « Do not consider me that I am brown, because the sun hath discolored me. » Sometimes in Holy Scripture the sun is put for the excessive heat of earthly desires. Whence then brown? Because the sun hath discolored me. Thus it is understood: Do not consider that I was black before through unbelief, because the sun, Christ — of whom it is written: « But unto you that fear my name the sun of justice shall arise (Matth. IV), » — has discolored me through faith. « The sons of my mother have fought against me. » In all creation two rational creatures were founded, the human and the angelic. The angel fell; he persuaded man. For the mother of every creature is the kindness and the power of God. We therefore and the angels, from the fact that we were founded rational, have as it were a certain fellowship of brotherhood, because the angels were founded by the same power by which we also 574Dwere. Yet because the falling angels daily wage war against us, she says: « The sons of my mother have fought against me. » Behold, while these spirits, reasons, fight — these spirits, the sons of the mother, while they fight against the soul, make it lean upon earthly things, be at leisure for the actions of the world, seek transitory things. Whence she subjoins: « They have set me keeper in the vineyards: the vineyard I have not kept. » For the vineyards are earthly actions. As if she should say: In earthly actions they have set me as keeper. And what? My vineyard, that is, my soul, my life, my mind, I have neglected to keep; because, while outwardly I was wrapped up in the action of earthly things, I fell away from the inward keeping. Very many consider themselves from that which is beside them, 575Anot from that which they are. Beside them are dignities, beside them are the inner ministries. And while they keep what they have beside them, they neglect to keep themselves. Let her say therefore: « They have set me keeper in the vineyards, my vineyard I have not kept, » that is: while by an outward keeping I serve in secular actions, I have lost the solicitude of the inward keeping. But behold, the soul, brought back to the grace of her Creator, now loves, now seeks where she may find her Redeemer. Whence it follows:

« Show me, O thou whom my soul loveth, where thou feedest, where thou liest in the midday. » At midday the sun is more fervent; everyone who is fervent in faith is fervent in love. This bridegroom, who below is called a fawn, feeds in their heart with the greenness of virtues; she says: « Show me, O thou whom my soul loveth, where thou feedest, where thou liest in the midday. » 575BWhy she thus asks where he feeds, where he lies, she has given the cause of her inquiry. « Lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions. » God's companions are his familiar friends, such as are all who live well. But many appear to be companions, and are not. For many teachers, while a perverse doctrine was persuading, seemed indeed companions, but proved enemies. While Arius, Sabellius, Montanus was a teacher, they seemed as it were companions; but when they were strictly examined, they appeared enemies. Let her say therefore: « Show me, O thou whom my soul loveth, where thou feedest, where thou liest in the midday, lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions. » As if she should say: Show me in whose hearts thou truly restest, lest I begin to wander after the flocks of those who seem to be thy companions, that is, who are believed to be thy familiars, and are not. All 575Cpriests, all teachers are companions as to appearance; but as to life, many are not companions, but are adversaries. For many little ones, faithful within the Church, desire to live well and wish to hold to a life of uprightness; they consider the life of the priests who are set over them, and while those priests themselves do not live well, those who follow slip away into error. Whence the Church, as it were in those same little ones and faithful, says: « Show me, O thou whom my soul loveth, where thou feedest, where thou liest in the midday, » that is: show me the life of those who truly serve thee, that I may know where thou feedest with the greenness of virtues, that I may know where thou liest in the midday, that is, restest in the fervor of charity, lest, while I look upon the flocks of thy companions, I myself begin to wander, not knowing whose words I ought to believe, 575Dwhose teaching I ought to use. Behold, the words of the bridegroom are returned to the bride.

« If thou know not thyself, O fairest among women, go forth, and go after the steps of thy flocks, and feed thy kids beside the tabernacles of the shepherds. » Every soul ought to care for nothing more than that it know itself. For he who knows himself has known that he was made to the image of God. If he was made to the image of God, he ought not to follow the likeness of beasts, nor to be rolled down either in luxury or in present appetite. Of which ignorance it is said elsewhere: « Man, when he was in honor, did not understand; he was compared to senseless beasts, and was made like to them (Psal. XLVIII). » The steps of the flocks are the actions of the peoples. 576AWhich, by how much they are many, by so much are they hindered, by so much perverse. Let it therefore be said to the Church: « If thou know not thyself, O fairest among women, go forth and go after the steps of the flocks and feed thy kids beside the tabernacles of the shepherds. » O thou who wast foul through ignorance, through faith thou art made fair among the souls of others. Which is evidently said to the Church of the elect. If thou know not thyself, that is, if thou art ignorant of this very thing, that thou wast made to my image, go forth, go outside. But if thou knowest not, thou hast been made something, go forth and go after the steps of the flocks. Follow not my examples, but the examples of the peoples. « And feed thy kids beside the tabernacles of the shepherds. » Our kids are carnal motions; the kids are our unlawful temptations. « Go after the steps of the flocks, » that is, depart after 576Bthe examples of the peoples. « And feed thy kids, » that is, nourish carnal motions — no longer spiritual senses, but carnal motions. Thou wouldst go beside the tabernacles of the shepherds, or to feed lambs, that is, in the doctrines of masters, in the doctrines of the apostles, in the doctrines of the prophets. Or if thou feedest kids, feed them beside the tabernacles of the shepherds, that thou mayest be called Christian by faith and not by works, because thou art seen to be within through faith, and not within through works. Behold, thou hast rebuked; behold, thou hast reproved. For what dost thou not say. What hast thou kindly wrought in her? Say it plainly. For it follows:

« To my cavalry among the chariots of Pharaoh have I likened thee, O my love. » All who serve luxury, who serve pride, who serve avarice, who serve envy, who serve deceit, are still under the chariot of Pharaoh, that is, 576Cunder the rule of the devil, who are as it were under the chariot of Pharaoh. But everyone who is fervent in humility, in chastity, in doctrine, in charity has already been made a horse of our Creator, is already set in the chariot of God, already has God for his rider. Whence to a certain one over whom the Lord presided it is said: « It is hard for thee to kick against the goad (Act. IX). » As if he should say: Thou art my horse; now thou canst not fling thy heels against me; now I preside over thee. Of these horses it is said elsewhere: « Thou hast sent thy horses into the sea, troubling many waters (Habac. III). » [Vulg. Habac. III, 15 viam fecisti in mari equis tuis, in luto aquarum multarum, "thou madest a way in the sea for thy horses, in the mud of many waters"] God therefore has a chariot, because he presides over holy souls and runs about everywhere through holy souls. Whence it is written: « The chariot of God is ten thousand (Psal. LXVII), » and the rest. Pharaoh nevertheless has chariots, which chariots nevertheless were sunk in 576Dthe Red Sea. The bridegroom therefore says: « To my cavalry among the chariots of Pharaoh have I likened thee. » While thou wast still serving devilish works, I likened thee to my cavalry, because I regarded what I had done in thee through predestination, and compared thee to my horses. Where the hidden judgments of God are to be considered: how many seem, through preaching, through wisdom, through chastity, through liberality, through long-suffering, to be horses of God, and yet by God's hidden judgment are likened to the horses of Pharaoh. And many seem, through avarice, through pride, through envy, through luxury, to be horses of Pharaoh, and yet by God's hidden judgment are likened to the horses of God, because he sees the former turned from good to evils, and sees the latter brought back 577Afrom evil to good. For as through predestination many who seem to be horses of God are horses of Pharaoh through the reprobate life which follows them, so also through piety many who seem to be horses of Pharaoh are, through the holy life which they will keep at their end, likened to the horses of God. Whence he speaks flatteringly and says: « To my cavalry among the chariots of Pharaoh have I likened thee, O my love, » that is: thou wast still serving the vices among the chariots of Pharaoh, thou wast running under viti s, but I regarded what I have done concerning thee through predestination. To my cavalry have I likened thee, that is, I regarded thee as like to my elect. Behold, at last thou perceivest, magnificent Caesar, what we said beforehand in the introduction: that nothing is found historically in this volume, but all things are redolent with the allusions of allegories, and are fragrant with the runnings-through of dramas 577Band with the sweetnesses of moral senses. Wherefore I greatly desire to persuade, nay rather to suggest, though rashly, to the summit of your excellence, that, since you have obtained a great wisdom of skill and have attained the defense and protection of holy Church, you should not only, set in readiness for wars by the aid of arms, fenced about with wedges of nobles and thronged with troops of soldiers, protect her from the incursions of the foreign nations of adversaries, but also persist devoted and diligent in sacred doctrine and in the understanding of the divine Scriptures, so that, fortified by the Gospels, girded with the swords of the divine Scriptures, adorned with the darts of sentences, you may be able to defend that same holy Church among your subjects from the shields, that is to say, of the inward malignant spirits: who always seek to extinguish not the bodies, 577Cbut the souls of the faithful. Whence Peter exhorts, saying: « Watch, because your adversary the devil goeth about as a roaring lion, seeking whom he may devour; whom resist ye, strong in faith (I Petr. V). » And that your nobility may learnedly be able both to beware of the snares of the ancient enemy and to preach them to others, be busy to study the expositions of this volume and of other books at fitting hours after the ordering of the empire, so that she may deservedly and with rejoicing be able to say what is read here: « The king brought me into the wine cellar, he set charity in order in me. » And that: « Show me where thou feedest, where thou liest in the midday, lest I begin to wander after the flocks of thy companions, » so that the bridegroom himself, namely the Lord who below is called a fawn, may deign, through the keeping of thyself and the vigilance of others 577Dand the understanding of the sacred Scriptures, in some manner to feed in you with the greenness of virtues, and to lie down in the fervor of charity. It follows:

« Fair are thy cheeks as the turtledove's. » After the holy kisses received, after all the gladness numbered out above, after the battle of the aforesaid sons of the mother, after the knowledge of herself, after the care of what sort she is and by whom she was created, after her power compared to the Lord's cavalry, the voice of Christ speaks to the Church. And now we are taught that the beginnings of her beauty are known, before the other members, from her cheeks. And these, for a great thing, are compared to the turtledove, that by its riddles he might show that she is to be drawn far away from the tumultuous disturbance of this world. This beauty, then, of the cheeks, which are adorned with the blush of chastity 578Aand the whiteness of modesty, and are in the face of the body: it is no wonder if the persons of those who adorn the face of the Church are compared to them. Whence in the cheeks of the Church are the ancient Fathers, who stand first in that very face of honor and are red with the comeliness of martyrdom. But the Church is compared to the turtledove on account of the variety of diverse goods, or the chastity of virtues. Which bird alone before other birds, withdrawn from the throng of men, loves to inhabit secret places, and there sets its nest and brings up its young, where its enemy either never or scarcely has access. So assuredly the holy Fathers, so the holy and faithful soul, which is called his love by the Word of God, by which soul indeed — by fleeing and by rejecting the fellowships of the wicked, whatever is seen to be precious in the present life — in that 578Bmanner she is known to make a heavenly offspring, and she sends to heaven the offspring begotten by the word of her doctrine and nourished by the example of her life. For the turtledove, as we tasted beforehand in the preface, is related by those who have described the natures of birds to be of such chastity that it knows no mate beyond one. Of which, if one shall by chance have failed, never now is another joined to the other, but in all its life it seeks by longing its own lost mate. To which, through the comeliness of chastity and of modesty, the Church is fittingly compared in the beauty of her cheeks. For he says: « Fair are thy cheeks as the turtledove's. » As if it were said by Christ: With so great a variety of virtues have I adorned thee, that thou shouldst corrupt by no seduction of depraved teachers the chastity of the faith promised to me. These things indeed have been said according to the order of history; but 578Caccording to the opinion of some, a loftier sense is said to lie hid in this comparison. For to certain men it seems that the cheeks of the Church's beauty are compared to that turtledove of which it is said in the Psalm: « For the sparrow hath found herself a house, and the turtledove a nest for herself where she may lay up her young (Psal. LXXXIII). » Where no wise man will be ignorant that the figure of the Holy Spirit is named in the sparrow, and that of the unspotted flesh assumed in the turtledove. Then, namely, is the Church praised as fair in her cheeks, which lie open to all, by the good and by the evil, by both, when through the greatest goods of chastity she shall have drawn ever so little into the likeness of the man assumed by Christ. Then is the whole body of the Church discerned to be most fair, when the modest look of the cheeks is admired by the impious for the glory of chastity. For then 578Dis chastity proved of great power by its own enemies, when those who assail it will not be able to conquer it. For as the members which are in the head, if they have been fair, excuse the foulness of the other members which are covered with garments, so also those which through priestly honor are known to be members of the head of the Church adorn by their own chastity the foulness of the people's negligence. And on the contrary there is nothing so deformed, so abject in the body of the Church, save when in the aforesaid members a most base life or reputation is discerned. And because holy Church in her prelates and teachers imitates by chastity and by variety of virtues the image of the turtledove, that is, of Christ, she is necessarily praised by him, saying: « How fair are thy cheeks as 579Athe turtledove's. » It follows: « Thy neck is as jewels. We will make thee golden murenulae, inlaid with silver. » In the neck there seem to be praised those who by the word of edification instruct the rest unto salvation, who transmit the spiritual food of the word, by interpreting it in whatever way, to the members of the body of Christ, whom belief alone supports; who through golden doctrine and through the jewel-like example of life are displayed upon the neck of the Church. For by the jewel the adornment of gems is discerned. But that their appearance may become fairer, they have need of the material of gold, by which, being joined together, they may be held in their own place. This too, the very natural talent of our nature, which is hewn out from a stony heart into the likeness of a gem, has need of the most shining help of the Holy Spirit, through whom the words of the divine law of the New and Old Testament, 579Bwhich declared or showed that the Saviour of the world had come — when, chained together as by one little chain of truth, the word is made, passed through the jewel-like minds of souls — adorns the neck of the Church through the concord of faith, through the sense of unanimity. And because the holy teachers, who are designated by the neck, adorned with virtues, minister the word of preaching to others, and what they teach in word they carry out in works. Which works are expressed by the jewels; and because they preach the Scripture of both Testaments, which is designated by the golden murenulae — namely, because it shines within with the gold of spiritual senses, and glitters without with the silver of heavenly eloquence — rightly is the Church praised in them when it is said: « Thy neck is as jewels. Murenulae » or little chains « of gold we will make thee, inlaid 579Cwith silver, » because the holy teachers cannot have the gifts aforesaid unless they receive them from him, in whom are all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge hidden. It follows:

« While the king was at his repose, my spikenard gave forth its odor. » Spikenard is an aromatic shrub, with a heavy, as they say, and thick root, but short and black and brittle, though it is rich, smelling in its place like cypress, of a rough taste, with a small and dense leaf, whose tips spread themselves out into ears; and therefore the perfumers celebrate the spikes and the leaves of spikenard for a twofold endowment. And this is what Mark says: « Of ointment of precious spikenard (Marc. XIV). » Namely, because that ointment which Mary brought to the Lord was compounded not only from the root of the spikenard, but also — 579Dthat it might be the more precious — by the addition of its spikes and leaves the grace of its odor and of its power had been heaped up. The naturalists also relate of spikenard that it is the chief ointment, whence deservedly the anointing of the head and feet of the Lord was offered. There are indeed many kinds of it, but all are herbs, except the Indian, which is more precious. But mystically this devotion of Mary ministering to the Lord designates the faith and the piety of holy Church, which speaks here in the song of love, saying: « When the king was at his repose, my spikenard gave forth its odor. » Which words she assuredly both fulfilled once according to the letter by the hands of Mary, and does not cease daily to fulfill spiritually in all her members. Which, spread through the whole 580Aworld, glory and say: But thanks be to God, who always maketh us to triumph in every place, « for we are the good odor of Christ unto God. » When she confesses with worthy reverence, praises and preaches the might of his divine power, which is his together with the Father, she assuredly pours precious ointment upon his head. But when she receives with equally worthy reverence the mysteries of the humanity assumed, she assuredly poured upon the feet of the Lord the ointment of pistic spikenard, that is, faithful and true: because that nature of his by which he deigned to touch the earth, that is, to dwell among men, she commends with pious preaching, and venerates with devoted services. Whence the repose is understood as the burial of Christ, although he had then reclined at table in the house of Simon the leper. But the spikenard is the women themselves who 580Bcame at first dawn to the tomb with spices, to anoint Christ and to spread abroad to believers the report of his resurrection. Whence it was said to them: « Go, tell my brethren that he goeth before you into Galilee; there you shall see him (Matth. XXVIII). » [Vulg. Matth. XXVIII, 10 ite nuntiate fratribus meis ut eant in Galilaeam, ibi me videbunt, "go, tell my brethren that they go into Galilee, there they shall see me"; the clause praecedit vos in Galilaeam, ibi eum videbitis stands at Matth. XXVIII, 7] Or the repose of Christ is the beatitude of the heavenly secret. For she says: « When the king was at his repose, my spikenard gave forth its odor: » because, namely, while Christ the king rests in the beatitude of the heavenly secret, the spikenard gives forth its odor, that is, the virtues of the saints in the Church minister to us the grace of great sweetness. Or also the repose of the king is understood as the holy Church of the elect. In her Christ found rest for himself, as he himself speaks through the Prophet: « This is my rest for ever and 580Cever (Psal. CXXXI). » The spikenard is said of the Church on account of the odor of the virtues. Whence the Apostle says: « We are the good odor of Christ unto God in every place (II Cor. II). » Which Church, when the devil has seen her enriched, adorned with innumerable jewels of graces, and, the blandishments of all the sweet things of the present life being despised, hastening to that one sweetness of eternal rest, he sends in innumerable inflictions of bitterness, both by himself and by his satellites, at every moment. But because God the Redeemer is always in the midst of her, she is not moved from the footstep of truth, nor is she disturbed on account of the assault of the impious rushing upon her, because Christ is both all things and in all. For if bitter things are brought upon her for the love of Christ, they grow sweet with the dew of future hope and of everlasting 580Dgladness. Therefore it follows:

« A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me; he shall abide between my breasts. » Holy Church, or the faithful soul, perseveres in the praise of her Creator, whom she ponders as having suffered in the bitterness of his passion. Whence, always having in the memory of recollection how near he is to the breasts of the bosom, and how he suffered for her unto the glory of her redemption, she says: « A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me; he shall abide between my breasts. » As if she had said: The death of my beloved, which he underwent for my salvation, shall always abide in my memory, for imitation and for the glory of praise. And because she rejoices that he suffered, she glories 581Aalso that he rose again from the dead; rightly she subjoined:

« A cluster of cypress is my beloved to me, in the vineyards of Engaddi. » Well does he say: « A cluster of cypress is my beloved to me, » as if he cried out openly: He who was a bundle of myrrh in the bitterness and sadness of the passion, the same is a cluster of cypress in the sweetness of the resurrection; myrrh signifies sadness, wine gladness: because, namely, Christ, begotten of the Jews according to the flesh, and hung by them upon the wood of the cross like a cluster, yet on the third day after the winepress of death showed the gladness of the resurrection to his disciples. And well « in the vineyards of Engaddi, » on account of the gifts of graces, which after the resurrection he lavished in baptism upon those who share in them. For Engaddi is called fount of the kid, or 581Beye of the kid, signifying, namely, baptism, where out of kids lambs are made. But in that it is interpreted eye haeai, it hints at Christ himself, who is the light of all sinners who come to him. Behold, for brevity's sake these things have been said concisely, that they may the more quickly be impressed upon my reader's mind. But because holy Church desires in her members to imitate the mystery of Christ's passion, that she may come with Christ to the glory of the resurrection, and may deserve to be set apart not with the kids on the left, but with the sheep on the right, let us speak more fully. Finally, when she had said, « a bundle of myrrh is my beloved, » she added to me: she understood that he had suffered for her salvation, and had risen from the dead for her glory. For she says: « A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me; he shall abide between my breasts. » 581CFor myrrh is interpreted bitter, which signifies in itself the saving discipline which the Church received in Christ; and as she, for his name, endures in her diverse members the diverse bitternesses of torments, Christ is made for her a bundle of myrrh. The tree of myrrh, therefore, from which is begotten a juice that will profit many infirmities of the body, is like the willow; and it is asserted by those who have described the natures of trees that it puts forth little branches with very fine and twisted twigs after the manner of little bundles. This aforesaid Church, therefore, or any holy soul whatever, when through the hearing of eternal life she receives the sweetness of taste at the coming of the bridegroom, does not feel the manifold and bitter sufferings brought upon her — that is, prisons, exiles, proscriptions, the stripping of present goods, hunger, 581Dthirst, chains, unjust slaughters, reproaches, perils, shipwrecks, showers of stones, the headlong casting into the deep, and the stings of various punishments, and many other things. When she has undergone these for the death of her Redeemer, with great gladness she says: « A bundle of myrrh is my beloved to me. » When therefore, for the healing of the many infirmities inflicted by the devil, she has patiently borne many chastisements from God, or has with joy sustained the diverse sufferings brought upon her for his name, as was said above, Christ is made her bundle of myrrh, for the driving out of all the diseases of the vices from her breast. Then, now clean and made ready with prayers, she is a divine lodging, so that now with such great confidence she invites Christ himself into the 582Arecess of her heart, as though within her breasts, saying in the Gospel: « I and the Father will come, and will make our abode with him (Joan. XIV.) » [Vulg. ad eum veniemus, "we will come to him" — the received text does not name the speakers here] Or also the Church rejoices that he abides between her breasts, among those men without doubt who, according to their age or according to their capacity, nourish little ones with the spiritual milk of their teaching. Of whom the Lord himself says: « If two or three shall have agreed upon earth, whatsoever they shall ask in my name shall be done for them. » [Vulg. Matt. XVIII, 19 si duo ex vobis consenserint super terram de omni re quamcumque petierint fiet illis a Patre meo, "if two of you shall consent upon earth, concerning anything whatsoever they shall ask, it shall be done to them by my Father"] And: « Where two or three shall have been gathered together in my name, I am in the midst of them (Matth. XVIII). » One of whom said out of his own breasts to his hearers: « I gave you milk to drink, not meat (I Cor. III). » There can also, if it please anyone, be understood as the two breasts of the Church Moses and Elias, in whose midst Jesus, transfigured on the mountain, the beloved 582Bof the Church, is proved to have abode unto the future glory of that same Church: of whom the one showed him promised, the other proclaimed that he had already come, in signification of the two Testaments. Whence it is said: In the midst of two living creatures thou shalt be known. [Vet. Lat. reading of Hab. III, 2; Vulg. in medio annorum vivifica illud, "in the midst of the years bring it to life"] Which two Testaments are the breasts of the Church. In whose midst Christ abides. And because Moses the lawgiver, and Elias the chief of the prophets, were the ones speaking of the departure of his passion, therefore there follows: « A cluster of Cyprus is my beloved to me, in the vineyards of Engaddi. » Before all lands, as it is reported, the island of Cyprus is attested to beget clusters of grapes of wondrous size. Of that size was the cluster which is read to have been carried by two young men out of the land of promise into the desert, and which in every way marked the figure of the assumed 582Cmanhood of Christ our Redeemer: which indeed, according to place, or time, or occasion, has been made the food and drink of the Church through the sacrament of his body and blood. But Cyprus is interpreted sadness or mourning. Engaddi, as has been said, fount of the kid. He is therefore a cluster of Cyprus to his friend the Church, while he sows in the minds of sinners the sadness, or the mourning, of penance, that he may lift them up to that blessedness of which he himself said in the Gospel: « Blessed are they that mourn now, for they shall be comforted (Matth. V). » [Vulg. Beati qui lugent, quoniam ipsi consolabuntur, without nunc, "now"] Who, like that cluster of which we spoke, was lifted up by two peoples — the Jew, namely, as persecutor, and the Roman soldier in command — by the phalanx onto the gibbet of the cross. Who to this day, through sadness and mourning, has begotten for the Church a life conferring eternity, as has been said before, in 582Dthose who, after countless heaps of crimes, are gathered into the bosom of the Church. But the aforesaid cluster daily supplies spiritual food and drink to those who believe in him, to those at least who are gathered in the vineyards of Engaddi, where is the fount of the kid, which is the fount of the most holy baptism, where the kids go down who were to be assigned to the left hand before the tribunal of the judge, to be handed over to eternal fire, and there ascend spotless lambs, to be gathered at the judge's right hand, to be granted the eternal kingdom. It is certain therefore that, just as the one Jewish nation is named the vineyard of the Lord through the knowledge of the divine law, so also the diverse nations brought to the faith of Christ through the husbandry of the apostles and the teaching of the doctors are called many vineyards. In the midst of which, as has been said, 583Ais the fount of the water of holy baptism, to which the kids come, that they may be made lambs. Which vineyards of Engaddi beget not wine, but balsams. Engaddi is a very great village in the land of Judaea, next to the Dead Sea. Which land holds the figure of the land of the living, where are the vineyards of balsam, which of old were only within the bounds of twenty iugera. After the Romans got possession of that same region, it was propagated also over the broadest hills — in stock like the vine, in leaves like rue, but whiter and always remaining. But the tree is balsam; the wood is called chirobalsamum, that is, its little branch; the fruit or seed, carpobalsamum; the juice, opobalsamum. Which is therefore signified with an addition, because the bark of the wood, struck with iron claws, distils through its caverns a drop of surpassing fragrance. For a cavern 583Bin the Greek tongue is called ὀρὴ. Its drop they adulterate by mixing in oil of cyprus or honey. But balsams, if they be pure, have such force that, if the sun grow hot, they cannot be held in the hand. From which vineyards many kings have transplanted little balsam trees into other places or lands — which the Romans too are read to have done — and they were utterly unable to take root or to live. Whence it came about, as we hinted above, that in that same place, or within those same bounds, great zeal was applied by kings in planting vineyards that beget balsams. And what the cause may be, that in no other soil do the vineyards come forth save in Engaddi alone, is worth the labor of understanding. I think, as a certain one of the doctors says, that this juice of these vineyards is to be understood to hold the type of the confession of the one faith, in which the hope 583Cof the faithful stands, in which each good medicinal fragrance of Christ, restoring life to dead souls, is proved to germinate. From which confession, whatsoever nation or soul shall have been plucked away or transplanted by evil husbandmen or by heretics, withers. Which is proved to have befallen many souls, which, torn away from the one most true aforesaid confession of faith and dried up, offered themselves as fodder for gehenna. For just as the body cannot subsist without food and drink, so it is declared by the voice of the Church that without the cluster which is Christ, the eternal life coming down from heaven, it cannot live; because in no other land of promise, in no other vineyard save in the vineyards of Engaddi is he found by those who hunger and thirst after justice — that is, in no congregation of heretics, in no sect of philosophers, in no 583Dflattery of schismatics, where, if anyone be transplanted, deceived by the report of his own perversity, he perishes withered. Understanding these things indeed, the Church says: « A cluster of Cyprus is my beloved to me, in the vineyards of Engaddi. » There follows:

« Behold thou art fair, my friend, behold thou art fair: thy eyes are of doves. » After the comeliness of the cheeks and of the neck, by rendering the bride her turn, the beauty of the eyes is praised in the simplicity of her doctors, and by a doubled proclamation kept hidden. Both the beauty of the whole body, and the eyes, are compared to the eyes of doves. And they are compared to that creature which, in gentleness, in fruitfulness, in keenness of sight, in swiftness of wings, is found more excellent 584Athan all winged living things. The eyes therefore are proved to be the guides of the whole body, without which either the body cannot advance, or it advances only scarcely, by stumbling. In them are understood the holy doctors, contemplating heavenly things with spiritual sight, who for their simplicity and innocence are compared to doves. There can also be understood, in right order, the priests, to whom the most holy mysteries have been committed, in whose faith the Church is adorned, in whose unspotted life she is exalted with frequent praises. For she is fair in exhibiting to God the purest knowledge; fair in showing to enemies and to those estranged from the faith of Christ a blameless manner of life. A third praise of beauty is also brought in, in the keenness of the eyes. For the nature of doves, beyond those things we have said, is proved to have this peculiar thing also, 584Bthat it watches for the coming of the enemy behind it from afar, before it in the water — the coming, namely, of the hawk — and considers lest he be able to snatch away her young. So without doubt the holy doctors, endowed with simplicity and innocence; so the priests of the Church, contemplate heavenly things with the keenness of their contemplation. Meditating, they sit upon the streams of the Scriptures, and they busy themselves to declare to their hearers, by the admonition of their words, the snares of the devil, who hastens to snatch away the souls of believers, that they may beware; and therefore in such the beauty of the Church is praised by the bridegroom. Whence he repeats "fair" and "fair," because he saw her praiseworthy in them both in preaching and in action. There follows:

« Behold thou art fair, my beloved, and comely; our little bed is flowering. » The voice of the Church to Christ. 584CAs if she said: Thou alone art fair; I am fair from thee. And because she understands that she was justified not by her own merits, and that she received the beauty of the virtues from him, being kindled in his love, she praises his beauty. No one can praise the comeliness of anyone whom he loves not at all, nor can he love him with whose ways he does not agree, as the Apostle teaches, saying: « No man, he says, speaking in the Spirit of God, saith anathema to Jesus. And no one saith the Lord Jesus, but in the Holy Spirit (I Cor. XII). » [Vulg. nemo potest dicere Dominus Iesus, "no man can say the Lord Jesus"] He therefore can both see the beauty and the form of the beloved Son of God, and extol it with praise, who by holy works has kept his Spirit in his own mind; and therefore in the present place, the Church, full of the Spirit of truth, as the bridegroom himself showed above, saying: « Behold thou art fair, 584Dmy friend, » — he who in the likeness of a dove came down from heaven upon the assumed man in the Jordan — and recognizing the beauty of his body without sin, and the comeliness of his true soul without lie and guile, utters in reciprocal and doubled praise, saying: « Behold thou art fair, my beloved, and comely; our little bed is flowering (Ephes. V). » Although, namely, a comely face or a costly garment may show the bride her costly bridegroom set at a distance, yet until the joining of the little bed she does not know the comeliness of the rest of the members. But when the lawful joining has been celebrated, and the two have been made one flesh according to the sentence of the first man — which sacrament master Paul asserted was carried through in Christ and in the Church — so also 585Athe aforesaid friend, up to the day of the burial, loved him indeed by desiring his coming, and praised him seen and kissed; but nevertheless all the love, and all the form of his comeliness, is proved to have reached, from the time of the resurrection up to the appointed term, as far as the shedding of blood for his name. For the flowering bed, that is, one strewn with flowers, where she recognized his beauty and comeliness in all his members, is understood by the Church, spoken in riddles, as the sepulchre of the Lord, where the body which the Word of the Father had assumed is proved to have lain. And the bed is with just reason proclaimed flowering, in which the spices, or the aloes, which are composed by the perfumer's art out of the juices or flowers of many kinds of herbs, were brought in with the body of Christ, and with which, when he rose again, the sepulchre 585Bwas besprinkled. In whose teaching the Church, that is, the throng of the faithful, found liberty; in death, life; in burial, silence; in hard labors, rest — which labors, driven by the scourges of the demons, she was daily discharging; where the true man is known to have dwelt, by the enclosing of his body, under the seal of the impious, and the true God to have gone forth by rising again from the sepulchre that had been marked. Where, under the name of the little bed, the joining of the Son of God and of the Church was celebrated; where with him the Church deserved to find the delightful sleep of the passion and the joy of the eternal waking, while the Apostle cries out to her members: « If you be dead with Christ, seek the things that are above, where Christ is at the right hand of God the Father (Colos. III). » [Vulg. Col. III, 1 si consurrexistis cum Christo, "if you be risen with Christ", and in dextera Dei sedens, "sitting at the right hand of God"] For his resurrection both the minds of men are daily prepared for the Holy Spirit to dwell in, and 585Cthe roofs of the congregations of believers; and even visibly, how innumerable houses of God and churches are enlarged without ceasing at every time, and of what things or from what materials they are built, or how durable they are, or how comely, he taught by saying:

« The beams of our houses are of cedar, the ceilings of cypress. » But it is to be known that certain of the doctors understand by the flowering bed the peace of holy Church, in which, secure from the tumult of the vices and from the dominion of the malignant spirits, she rests in Christ, and flowers with holy virtues, and is multiplied with spiritual flower. But let us, as we began, follow out the former sense. By the little bed we understand the sepulchre of the Lord, where his flesh, through the slumber of death, rested as though in a little bed, 585Dwhich truly flowered through the glory of the resurrection. For the divine word says: « The beams of our houses are of cedar, our ceilings of cypress. » The beams and the ceilings are, it may be, the doctors and the hearers, or the hearts of the elect, on account of their strength and comeliness; of cedar and of cypress he says, on account of their surpassing virtues, the fragrance of a good life and of incorruptions, in which Christ rests as under a roof. And, that it may lie open more plainly, let us speak by an example: The nature of the cedars is said always to grow, nor is it ever suffered to pass into old age, according to that saying of the Psalmist: « The just shall flourish like the palm tree, and shall be multiplied like the cedar of Libanus (Psal. XCI). » The juice of which trees, according to the opinions of the physicians, is of such virtue that it heals the diverse 586Amembers of bodies, kills the worms of ulcers, and, when bodies are anointed with it, defends them from the stings of flies. With the wood of which tree, together with living water and a bundle of hyssop bound with scarlet in an earthen vessel, the cleansing of sins by sprinkling is celebrated in the Old Testament. Which is now taught to be celebrated in truth through the priest, through baptism, and through the blood of Christ, who, defending the multitudes of the one faith, encompassed by that bundle, from the assault of the devil, is taught to be celebrated in the eternal and in the temporal remission of sins. The houses, namely, of the Son of God and of the Church — the little assemblies of the Christian people, where the aforesaid redemption of souls is celebrated — are known to be in the whole world. Which houses are proved to be built out of the doctors, nay rather by the doctors, that is, by the hewn iron tools of their teaching, in whose minds the Lord deigns to dwell. The very incorruptible cedar timbers 586Bof the aforesaid houses are understood to be those who do not perish from their strength of holiness by the most foolish suggestions of wicked thoughts, and, always forgetting the things that are past, grow daily without intermission, like cedars, toward the good works that are before them — that is, they are multiplied by the fruits of the saints unto the help of gains for holy souls; the juice of whose speech or of whose consolation defends souls from the stings of the demons, and heals the wounds of the habits of filthy misdeeds long since inflicted, and destroys the worms of the most wicked thoughts. In which, worthily, for the delight of those dwelling there, the cypress ceilings, fixed for the faithful, rightly hang down. For the wood of the cypress, planed by the craftsman's hand, affords a comely 586Cappearance, and gives back a most pleasant odor to him; it is proved to contain in itself the virtue of medicine for many sufferings of the body. Which things are understood to express chastity, modesty, obedience, humility, charity and mercy — the pattern of the hearers who dwell there, the honor of the priests who venerate — who hang unceasingly upon the word of the doctors, who, after the manner of a vault, by solacing out of their just labors the doctors of the present time, deck the aforesaid ceilings joined to the beams of the houses and cover their nakedness. For the glory of the doctors is the people, and likewise the glory of the people is the firm establishing of the doctors: while the one impart spiritual gifts to the people, and the people carnal offerings to them, glorious houses are prepared for Christ; and the flowers of heavenly wisdom, and lilies of integrity, through their 586Dpenance, the Lord himself bestows upon the aforesaid houses unto the great delightfulness of honor. Whence there follows:

CAPUT II.

« Ego flos campi, et lilium convallium. » Ac si aperte dicatur: Ego decus mundi et gloria humilium. Quanquam et infernus ex superfluo possit intelligi, et ubi Christus ad eripiendos patriarchas descendere dignatus est, sicut quidam intelligunt. Sed nos, his praelibatis ne quorumdam sensum ignorasse causaremur, dicamus uberius beati Ambrosii dicta ponentes. Ante suam, inquit, incarnationem, Salvator in angeli persona partriarchis et prophetis loquendo, solus cui nullus similis inter angelos, thronos, dominationesque 587Aomnibus admirandus, singularis flos in toto coelorum campo coruscans probatur. Quam partem odoris notitiae suae prout poterant sustinere patriarchae vel prophetae, et mundus ipse mortales semper admonuit docendo. Omnis scientia, per prophetas, per signorum virtutes interficiendo impios per aquas diluvii, vel Sodomitas per ignem, per quod insipientes intelligerent sapientiam, et stulti superbi aliquando saperent super se commorari excelsiorem judicem, justam exsolventem vindictam. Ubi vero per incarnationis sacramentum huc in convallem lacrymarum, inter spinas et consortia peccatorum descendit, lilium effectum se esse testatur. In qua convalle quid aliud nisi spurcissima religio idololatriae, invidiae, furtorum, homicidiorumque, 587Baruspicinae auguriorum, vel fornicationum, vel magicarum artium grassabantur spinarum silvae? In quarum medio doctrinae flagrantia, et exempli candore Christus ostensus, credentium turbam quotidie liberat. Tria igitur in se delectabilia et nimis oculis grata, lilii natura continere probatur, id est candorem, odorem, quaeque adusta ignibus medicinam. Quae tria admiranda simili ratione in hanc convallem mundi Dominus apportasse cognoscitur, hoc est abolitionem peccati, abstersionem mendacii, refrigerii temperamentum ignium genitalium, de quo nascitur amor conservandae virginitatis. Et quicunque a talibus se temperant in utroque sexu gloriosi assumpti hominis et beatae Mariae virginis accenduntur exemplis. Et sic flos campi et lilium convallium 587Cper immaculati corporis assumptionem dicitur. Quod sacramentum ad liberandam plebem suam, quo nos a diversis languoribus diversa ejus membra sanaret, et quae ignitis spiculis fornicationum a daemonibus inflicta fuerant vulnera, ejus sanarentur doctrina, et quae acumine nequitiae amicam in prophetis et doctoribus vulnerabant, in liliorum jucunditate converterentur, de quibus Ecclesia renata refulget, et amarissimis stimulis pungitur, sicut ipse in sequentibus ait:

« Sicut lilium inter spinas, sic amica mea inter filias. » Istae igitur amicae inter quas Ecclesia ut lilium inter spinas consistit, licet non cum distinctione dicatur cujus sint filiae, tamen ex eo quod spinis comparantur, advertere possumus non Dei, sed 587Ddiaboli per nequissimam doctrinam genitas nuncupari. Inter tot enim venenosas spinas Ecclesia incolit, mundum videlicet gentilium vel haereticorum, qui ipsam impugnant, et ne otio torpescat, contra hostes armari impugnarique permittitur, ut post pugnam ejus fortitudo appareat. De talibus Dominus ait: « Cavete a fermento Pharisaeorum (Matth. XVI), » quod est doctrina haereticorum. Et apostolus (I Cor. VI): « Quae, inquit, societas luci ad tenebras? » Aut qui est sensus templo Dei cum idolis? Aut quae communicatio Christi cum Belial? Et prope similiter Isaias ait: « Exite, inquit, de medio eorum, et qui portatis vasa Domini, mundamini (Isa. LII). » Quod autem dicit: « Sicut lilium inter spinas, sic amica 588Amea inter filias, » non vituperationem justorum, qui sunt Ecclesiae membra, sed laudem demonstrat, ut nos doceret nimium esse laudabile, pie inter impios vivere, et perversorum in nullo spinosa conversatione attrahi, inter quos justus quasi lux in tenebris resplendere probatur. Ac si Ecclesiae dicatur: Tu requiem quaeris, sed laudem lectuli recordare, quod candidior tribulationum aculeis efficieris, et majores fructus praedicationis quam quietis acquires. Et bene ait: « Sicut lilium inter spinas, sic amica mea inter filias, » id est sicut lilium inter spinas proprium odorem atque candorem tenet, sic electorum Ecclesia inter pravos Christianos atque haereticos, si eis non cesserit, virtutibus flagrat. Sequitur:

588B« Sicut malus inter ligna silvarum, sic dilectus meus inter filios. Sub umbra illius quem desiderabam sedi, et fructus ejus dulcis gutturi meo. » Sicut igitur ante incarnationis mysterium « multifariam, multisque modis, » secundum Apostolum, « Dei Filius locutus est patribus nostris (Hebr. I), » vel ostensus est in prophetis, sic post praedictum mysterium celebravit, multa bona diversis modis praestando humanae naturae, diversis rebus diversisque comparatur personis, pro tempore et loco, vel causa. Nam secundum David prophetam (Psal. LXVII) mons Dei, pro eo quod plenitudo divinitatis habitatura erat in eo. Est et vitulus novellus cornua producens, per duo Testamenta. Et ungulas per octo beatitudines, a quatuor evangelistarum pedibus productas. 588CEt agnus tollens peccatum mundi per mansuetudinis impertionem Spiritus sancti, ita ut alios vestiendo ipse semper abundet. Est et fasciculus dilectus myrrhae, et botrus cypri in vineis Engaddi, ita et pro loco arborem malum vel granatum eum appellat Ecclesia, pro eo quod arbor vitae est cum diverso sapore diversis se personis praebendo. Est procul dubio arbor malus granata, quae inedia famis affectas animas, sole sub ardente nequitiae fatigatas, sub umbra defensionis suae protegit ac defendit, et fructu labiorum doctrinae, esurientes et sitientes per suam dulcedinem reficit. A paradisi expulsione usque ad Virginis partum multis laboribus et intolerabilibus daemonum impugnationibus affecta est humana progenies, quae hic sub Ecclesiae persona 588Damica est appellata, in ejus adventu qui dixit: « Venite ad me, omnes qui laboratis et onerati estis, et ego vos requiescere faciam (Matth. XI), » sub tegmine defensionis ejus, credendo unum Deum, requiescens laeta dicit: « Sub umbra illius quem desiderabam sedi. » Quam dulcedinem regni coelorum quanquam praesens protectio possit intelligi, et beatorum requiem nominat, sive corpus ejus, et sanguinem delibando, vitam aeternam conferentem, exsultans ait: « Et fructus ejus dulcis gutturi meo. » Licet ut dulcedo divinarum Scripturarum intelligatur in praedicatoribus sanctis, qui guttura Domini appellantur, prolata sit. De qua dulcedine ait Propheta: « Gustate, inquit, et videte, quoniam suavis est Dominus. » 589A« Beatus vir, etc. (Psal. XXXIII). » Et illud: « Quam dulcia faucibus meis eloquia tua! Domine, super mel ori meo (Psal.
