Now the cold of unbelief infidelitatiis and the rain of iniquity have departed, which covered the whole globe until the time of the Lord's incarnation and resurrection. « The flowers have appeared in our land, » that is, the beginnings of faith and of justice flowered in the world as the Church grew. « The time of pruning is come, » that is, when the useless shoots of vain religion have been cut away, the hearts of men are prepared for the faith to come; for the summer time is come, 597Dwhen out of one stock of the body the good are separated from the wicked, lest by the nearness of the wicked the good perish, lest by the treachery of unbelievers believers be endangered. This, surely, was the time of pruning announced as coming, of which the Lord says in the Gospel: « For I came to separate the son from the father, and the daughter-in-law from her mother-in-law, and the daughter from her mother (Matth. X), » and the rest. [Vulg. Matt. 10:35 reads hominem adversus patrem suum, "a man against his father," filiam adversus matrem suam, nurum adversus socrum suam — "against," not "from," and "a man," not "the son"] Or, winter is past, because the tribulation of the present life has withdrawn. The rain too has gone, because when we are led to contemplate almighty God in his own substance, the drops of words will no longer be necessary, so that the rain of preaching should have to be poured out. For what a man can here hear less of, there he shall see more fully: « Because now we see through a glass in a dark manner, but then face to face, 598Aand we shall see him as he is (I Cor. XIII). » [Vulg. 1 Cor. 13:12 stops at "face to face"; the final clause is drawn from 1 John 3:2] Then the flowers appear in the land, because when the soul has begun to taste beforehand certain first sweetnesses of the life of eternal blessedness, it is as though already going out it smells among the flowers what, after it has gone forth, it shall have more abundantly in the fruit; whence there also is subjoined: « The time of pruning is come. » For in pruning the barren shoots are cut back, so that those which prevail may bear fruit more abundantly. And so the time of our pruning shall come when we forsake the unfruitful and hurtful flesh of corruption. Which fruit shall be for us most abundant: the vision of the one God. As is the time of pruning, so is the calling out of the world of the soul of the heavenly ones. There follows: « The voice of the turtledove is heard in our land. » Let us state the opinions of various persons, lest perhaps we seem to plead ignorance. The voice 598Bof the turtledove, on account of the comeliness of chastity, they would have understood as the blessed Mary, whose voice was first heard in our land when she said to the angel: « How shall this be done, because I know not man? » (Luc. I.) And: « Behold the handmaid of the Lord, be it done to me according to thy word (Ibid.) » . And: « From henceforth all generations shall call me blessed (Ibid.) » . To whom the angel answered: « The Holy Spirit shall come upon thee, and the power of the Most High shall overshadow thee. » And: « That which shall be born of thee, holy, shall be called the Son of God. » And she is worthy indeed, whom alone all generations call blessed, whom, glorious among all women, not only the nations of divers peoples but even the powers of the heavens praise in wonder; for through her the entrance of life, death put to flight, the world reconciled, are our joy. But some assert the blessed John 598Cthe Baptist, whose voice was heard saying: « I am the voice of one crying in the wilderness, prepare ye the way of the Lord (Matth. V). » And: « Bring forth fruits worthy of penance (Luc. III). » For so long as the winter of transgression, a sorrowful time and bound fast from every good shoot, had covered the face of the world, and the devil held the earth under his dominion, this most sweet voice of the turtledove, whether through the blessed Mary or through John, was not heard in the land. But when that great sun of justice, who rose for us bringing by his indulgence the richness of summer, then by the desire of preserving chastity the voice of the turtledove began to be heard in our land. For with just reason virginity is compared to the turtledove, that most chaste bird — virginity which in John or in Mary holds the first place. 598DFor she, once joined to the Word of God or to the Holy Spirit, never thinks of another mate, nor is there any other joining of the desires of her love save to him to whom she is proved to have been once cemented — like the turtledove, which loves the dwelling-places of the beloved wilderness, and calling its mate resounds with a sweet voice, and when he is lost seeks not another, but always sends forth to the other the voice of its promise and of its mind, to whom it promised to keep the affection of love. When he is dead, it is never said to be joined to another; but all the days of its life it seeks with longing him to whom it was once joined. But it must be known that, although the voice of the turtledove which was heard in our land be such, it is that of the Lord and Saviour, who says in the Gospel: « Do penance, for the kingdom of heaven will draw near (Matth. III). » [Vulg. appropinquavit, "hath drawn near," perfect] Or, by the turtledove most rightly 599Ais the holy Church understood, adorned as with feathers by the variety of the virtues.

« The fig tree hath put forth her green figs. » Since therefore the harvest of human redemption and the coming of the glorious Redeemer were drawing near, the fig tree, that is, the Synagogue, bound fast by the winter and the frost of impiety, is understood to have had in itself nothing but the most bitter juice of a teaching which it falsely gives out as food of souls. But at the Lord's coming, after the voice of the turtledove was heard saying, « Do penance, » and the rest, the fig tree put forth her green figs, that is, the precepts of the law fell, and she herself through the apostles put forth her green figs, who, begotten of her, were made the most sweet food of teaching for souls. But because she would not believe them, she withered with an everlasting barrenness, and, stripped of all comeliness, was kept as nothing in herself but fuel for the eternal fire. 599BThe people, freed from the shadow of her perverse oppression, began — the evil teachers, the Pharisees, being removed — to flower by believing in Christ, and by their own example to summon the nations of other peoples to the flower of belief. Of whom it is said in the following verse: « The flowering vines have given their odour. » For by believing in one almighty God the vines flowered, that is, the evangelical people. Or, the flowering vines, holy ways of life, gave their odour far and wide throughout the world. For as in the winter season the vines are without leaves and flowers and without comeliness, so it is most certainly proved that the nations throughout the world, which he has now called vines, were without the comeliness of justice and the ornament of holiness. There follows:

« Arise, make haste, my love, my fair one, and 599Ccome, my dove, in the holes of the rock, in the hollow of the wall. » The Church, then, called from very far off, was lying in a place lowly enough and hidden, troubled and oppressed by the storm of the demons — she who now is proved to reign in the whole world. But when the sacraments of Christ's passions were celebrated through the teaching of the apostles, in whom Christ speaks, she is summoned to arise and to come to the bridegroom walking among the vines, to rejoice with him, and to put herself forth unto the yield of the fruit of justice of the aforesaid nations. As if it were said to her: O bride and love, to whom I have offered such great goods and for whom I have borne such great things, arise and come, gird thyself for the contest, whence thou mayest receive eternal rest « in the holes of the rock, in the hollow of the wall. » What the rock is, the Apostle sets forth: « The rock, he says, was Christ (I Cor. 599DX). » Whoever therefore desires to be snatched from the enemies of the power of the air, and to escape unharmed, let him always enter, by keeping faith, into the holes of this rock — that is, let him consider the wounds of the nails and of the lance which the bridegroom took upon himself for the safety of the bride — and into the hollow of the wall, that is, the keeping of the heavenly virtues. For this rock has many holes, that is, approaches, through which entrance is made to the Father who prepares the kingdom for believers: namely, meekness, humility, patience, contempt of riches, the accepting of no persons, passing the night in prayer, the mastery of mercy, unmoved chastity, the form of kindness, a singular mirror, the acknowledgment of the undivided Trinity, in which is our redemption. These are beyond doubt the 600Aholes in which the soul is taught to come to the joys of the kingdom of heaven. The hole of the rock may be understood as the protection of that same Lord Jesus Christ, to which the Church takes refuge, as a dove to the hole of the rock. Hence Isaias says: « Enter into the rock, hide thyself in the trench in the earth (Isa. II). » The hollow of the wall may be taken to signify the defence of the Holy Spirit, or the congregation of the faithful — although the holes of the rock may be understood as the affirmations of the four Gospels, which bear witness that the body of that same rock was pierced through by the blows aforesaid. But the hollow of the wall [is] the teachings of the apostles, out of which God is proved to have built for the people of believers, as for a vineyard, a most firm wall against the assault of the malignant spirits. For as they [came to know] the divine mysteries through Christ, so we too, coming to know them 600Bthrough the apostles, are held within the sight of that faith which they themselves confessed. And because « faith without works is idle (Jac. II), » it is rightly subjoined: « Show me thy face. Let thy voice sound in my ears. For thy voice is sweet, and thy face comely. » As if it were openly said: Show me thy face, I pray; go forth into the public view of action; do not rest under the torpor of repose, and show thy faith by works. And let thy voice sound — that is, the voice of preaching, of praise and of confession — in my ears. For his voice is sweet to God who knows how sweetly and pleasantly, out of the sweetness of the kingdom, to declare the words of the Lord to his neighbours, and to resound praises to the Lord himself. And that faith is comely which, adorned with works, does not fear to suffer adversities. There follows:

« Catch us the little foxes that destroy 600Cthe vines; for our vineyard hath flowered. » The storm of the demons being now over, which above he called winter, the bride, already joined to the king's body through the sacraments of baptism, is invited into the vineyard, that is, into the people of believers gathered to her from the Gentiles, and she finds it putting forth flowers through the good of nature, made ready to trust in the God of heaven. But [it being] made ready for pulling down and devouring by the craft and the ambushes of the foxes that await the ripeness of the fruit — which are the most biting dogmas of the heresy of perverse souls — Jesus says to his teachers: « Catch us the little foxes that destroy demo liuntur the vines, » that is, convict the heretics and schismatics, depraved in faith and deceitful in word, who are wont to tear with the tooth of depraved teaching the untrained minds of the faithful. For our vineyard hath flowered, that is, the holy souls of the elect 600Dbring forth the fruit of faith and of good works far and wide. But what he says, "our," is understood in a twofold way, that is, whether he has called it his own and the Father's, or whether he calls the Church joined to himself a sharer. And when he had seen both the Church from the Jews and, in greatest measure, that from the Gentiles put forth flowers, he prepares hunters or guards to catch the foxes before the flowers bring forth ripe fruits. Of whose number is one who said: "Reproving every man, we bring into captivity every knowledge that exalteth itself against the knowledge of God." [Vulg. 2 Cor. 10:5 brings into captivity "every understanding unto the obedience of Christ" and speaks of "every height," not "every man" and "every knowledge"] For whether the foxes be understood as demons, or as heretics and perverse teachers through whom the demons speak blasphemies, it is manifest that both agree — men through whom the minds 601Aof men, in which the right faith of the Church dwells, are broken apart. Whose destruction, or whose hidden ambushes, we ought carefully to guard against, and to love God with our whole heart that we may deserve to be loved by him; and therefore the Church most rightly subjoins:

« My beloved to me, and I to him. » The Church therefore being joined to Christ, or the soul to the Word of God, so that he may heap upon her a more abundant joy in all things — both those in which he himself is fed with gladness, and what it is that delights him in her, he shows; and what she ought to answer for such great benefits is taught. The bridegroom, that is, desires in her a comely face, as he intimated above: one stained with no blackness of sin, not hidden by base words and by lying, nor dreadful of voice with the hoarseness of blasphemies, but she renders in turn a sweetness resounding 601Bin her praises; and that same Church says: « My beloved to me, and I to him. » Christ and she, head and body, are as one man; and as the members [follow] the head, so by loving she follows him, and for that reason she speaks in this manner. For there is no beloved and helper of the Church save Christ, and no beloved of Christ save the Church: because no other rightly loves Christ save the one only Church, and no other is loved by him save she. « Who feedeth among the lilies, till the day break, and the shadows be bowed down. » That is, who is fed with the cleanness of faith and the whiteness of the virtues, until the true day of that judgment of the world to come arrives, and light arises, and the shadows — that is, the errors of the present world, which hinder us — pass away before the sun of justice, and are bowed down together with their lovers into the chasm of hell.

601C« Return: be like, my beloved, to a roe and to a young hart upon the mountains of Bethel. » [Vulg. Bether] This is the voice of the aforesaid Church to Christ: I beseech thee, my beloved, that thou wouldst return to me more often by the coming of thy visitation, because the flesh which thou tookest from the stock of the patriarchs, rising from the dead, thou hast borne up above every one of the heavenly mountains, that thou mayest lighten the labour of my pilgrimage by eternal beholding. The roe in this place, as we have tasted beforehand above, they say is the flesh of Christ, on account of the likeness of the flesh of sin. He himself is called the young hart, because begotten of the stock of the patriarchs, who after his resurrection returning to the heavens was exalted above the mountains of Bethel, which is called the house of God, that is, the heavenly fatherland of the angels. Whence the Psalmist, speaking to him, 601Dsays: « O Lord, our Lord, how admirable is thy name in the whole earth! How exalted is thy magnificence above the heavens! » (Psal. VIII.) [Vulg. reads Quoniam, "For," where Migne prints "How"] Nor indeed is the moral sense to be admitted. For every one of those who believe in God has made himself either a mountain of God by holy conversation, or a mountain of the devil by living perversely. For Bethel is interpreted the house of God, as we have said before. Whoever therefore has so displayed his own life that the Holy Spirit delights to dwell in him, is made a mountain of God, which is the Church of God. Above these things therefore, if at any time, being offended by the sins of the people, he has withdrawn afar, he returns; he is made like in course or in habit to the roe and to the young hart, whose nature it is, when a voice sent forth behind their back resounds before them in the mountains, while they suppose it to be running to meet them, 602Ato return with swifter course to the place from which they had departed. And this is the reason why, being provoked by enemies, it will not forsake its accustomed seat. So too Christ our Lord, however provoked, however exasperated by the sins of men, being called back by fastings and prayers, returns to the accustomed seat of his mercy. And if at any time he seems to be provoked, so that zeal in seeking him may be deservedly increased, no one ought to lose trust nor to cease from seeking. Whence there follows:

CAPUT III.

« In lectulo meo per noctem quaesivi quem diligit anima mea. Quaesivi illum et non inveni. » « Veni, sicut in quibusdam codicibus additur, et vocavi 602Beum, et non respondit mihi. » Haec ergo quamvis praenuntiata sint et consummata in Domini sepultura videantur, dum mulieres ad sepulcrum Domini pervigilant, cum apostoli intolerabiliter lugent, sed amplius ad superiora respiciunt quae subnectuntur; ac si Ecclesia vel unaquaeque sancta anima apertius dicat: Jamdudum multo studio quaesivi illum, sed quia adhuc illecebris carnis meae subdita fui, et tenebris profundae ignorantiae obcaecata, non inveni lumen veritatis, id est Dominum. Aliter in lectulo suo dilectum suum Ecclesia vel anima sancta quaerit, quando ab omni perturbatione saeculi tranquilla in quiete cordis Dominum suum cogitat. Quem quaerens ob hoc minime invenit, ut amore illius accensa ardentius quaerens. Per noctem 602Cquaerimus, quia et si jam mens in illo vigilet, tamen adhuc caligat oculus. « Vocavi eum et non respondit mihi. » Quicunque solum hominem credit Christum, non inveniet eum. Et quicunque deitatem non credit in Christo, non inveniet eum. Et quicunque deitatem non credit in Christo, vocat quidem in noctibus perversorum sensuum suorum Dominum Dei Filium, sed non respondet ei sicut non respondit regi Sauli a quo recesserat Deus. Sine causa igitur laborem impendit quaerendo, qui aequalem hominibus absque peccato et coaeternam Patri, non fuerit utraque natura confessus Deum, qui redemit, et hominem per quem redemit perditos Deus, de quo Psalmista dicit: « Frater non redemit, redimet homo (Psal. XLVIII). »

602D« Surgam, et circuibo civitatem, per vicos et plateas quaeram quem diligit anima mea. Quaesivi illum, et non inveni. » Proposui in animo meo de lectulo carnalium voluptatum surgere, et terras ac maria circuire, et philosophorum adire mysteria, sed nec sic inveni illum. Potest et aliter intelligi. Civitas hoc in loco sancta electorum Ecclesia intelligitur, seu divina Scriptura. Vicos vero atque plateas, libros legis et prophatarum vocat, in quibus Ecclesia seu anima electa dilectum suum, id est Christum dum ardenter quaerit invenit, quem in libris philosophorum non reperit.

« Invenerunt me vigiles qui custodiunt civitatem. Num quem diligit anima mea vidistis? » (Psal. CXXVI.) Vigiles qui custodiunt civitatem, id est Ecclesiam, 603Aapostoli vel caeteri doctores sunt qui sollicitam gentilitatem cum veritatis indagine invenerunt. De quibus dicitur: « Nisi Dominus custodierit civitatem, in vanum vigilant qui custodiunt eam (Psal. CXXVI). »

« Paululum cum pertransissem, inveni quem diligit anima mea. » Id est dum me illorum magisterio tradidi mox lumen veritatis quod quaesivi, inveni. Aliter: Quaerentes autem nos vigiles inveniunt qui custodiunt civitatem quia sancti Patres, qui Ecclesiae statum custodiunt, bonis nostris studiis occurrunt, ut suo vel verbo vel scripto doceant. Quos cum paululum pertransimus, invenimus quem diligimus, quia Redemptor noster, et si humilitate homo inter homines, divinitate tamen super homines 603Bfuit. Quia nisi qui fidei passibus et pura mente omnes sanctos transierit, et Christum excellentiorem omnibus sanctis crediderit, invenire nequaquam poterit. « Tenui manum ejus nec dimittam, donec introducam illum in domum patris mei, et in cubiculum genitricis meae. » Teneo illum firma fide, donec in fine saeculi per officium praedicationis introducam illum in domum et in cubiculum Synagogae, quae me genuit in Domino, « et fiet unum ovile et unus pastor (Joan. X). » Sequitur :

« Adjuro vos, filiae Hierusalem, per capreas cervosque camporum, ne suscitetis, neque evigilare faciatis dilectam, donec ipsa velit. » Ubicunque igitur in hoc cantico similia verba reperiuntur credentium diversis temporibus, diversorum 603Cpopulorum personae introducuntur. Hic namque plebis illius persona videtur induci, quae post Christi ascensionem fatigata desiderio, quaerendo quem diligebat anima ejus, Ecclesia videlicet ex gentibus, quae, paululum munita sibi tranquillitatis pace, in securitatis somno requiescere cupiebat, et ideo repetit hunc versum sponsus, ne minorem eum Ecclesiae ex gentibus congregatae, quam Judaeis habere sollicitudinem putares, sed ut sit de una utriusque una sponsa, et illa dilectissima, quem versiculum jam superius exposuimus. Filiae Hierusalem animae sunt electorum, quae supernae illius Hierusalem visionem scilicet pacis speculantur. Capreae vel cervi Patres Novi ac Veteris Testamenti intelliguntur, quorum in altitudine contemplationis pascua sunt.

603D« Quae est ista quae ascendit per desertum? » Hanc opinor gentem per desertum ascendentem, quam doctor gentium Paulus ab Hierusalem usque ad Illyricum congregatam, et unguentis doctrinae delibutam, mysteriorumque sacramentorum aromatibus aspersam, usque ad pacifici regis lectulum perducit, de quo in sequentibus legitur. Unde miratur Synagoga quomodo gentium populus nullo circumcisionis mysterio emundatus, nulla prophetarum admonitione eruditus, subito ab infimis voluptatibus per desertum gentilitatis et idololatriae ad alta virtutum culmina et sponsi amplexus ascendisset. Vere desertus locus erat, ubi nomen Christi non fuerat nominatum. « Sicut virgula fumi, ex aromatibus 604Amyrrhae et thuris, et universi pulveris pigmentarii. » Id est igni amoris succensa, vel cum omni nisu virtutum ad coelestia tendens. Per myrrham mortificatio carnalium voluptatum, per thus paritas orationum, et in diversis pulveribus pigmentarii omnium virtutum odor. Moraliter vero sancta electorum Ecclesia cum ab hoc mundo in sanctis precibus ardenti amore se erigit, per desertum quod deseruit ascendit. Fumus de incenso nascitur. Et per Psalmistam dicitur: « Dirigatur oratio mea sicut incensum in conspectu tuo, Domine (Ps. CXL). » Fumus excutere lacrymas solet. Itaque fumus ex aromatibus est compunctio orationis concepta ex virtutibus amoris. Quae tamen oratio fumi virgula dicitur, quia dum sola coelestia postulat, sic recta 604Bprogreditur ut ad terrena atque temporalia appetenda minime reflectatur. Et notandum quod non virga, sed virgula nuncupatur, quia interdum in compunctionis ardore, tantae subtilitatis est vis amoris, ut hanc nec ipse animus comprehendere qui illuminatus meruit habere. Bene hoc dicitur myrrhae et thuris. Thus enim ex lege Domini in sacrificio incenditur. Per myrrham vero corpora mortua condiuntur, ne a vermibus corrumpantur. Myrrhae ergo et thuris sacrificium offerunt, qui et carnem afficiunt, ne eis corruptionis vitia dominentur, et redolentem in conspectu Domini amoris sui hostiam incendunt, seque ipsos Deo in sanctis virtutibus exhibent. Unde et illic subditur: « Et universi pulveris pigmentarii. » Pulvis pigmentarii 604Cvirtus bonae operationis. Et notandum quod virtutes bene operantium non pigmenta, sed pulveres dicuntur. Cum enim quaelibet bona agimus, pigmenta offerimus. Cum vero bona ipsa etiam quae agimus retractamus, et ne quid in his sinistrum sit judicio retractionis attendimus, quasi ex pigmentis pulverem facimus, ut orationem nostram Domino per discretionem et amorem subtilius incendamus.

« En lectulum Salomonis sexaginta ambiunt ex fortissimis Israel. » Salomon qui interpretatur pacificus, Dominum Jesum Christum designat. Lectulus Salomonis quieta est mens sanctorum, in quibus ipse velut in lectulo requiescit. Sexaginta fortes praedicatores sunt sancti, et fortissimi bellatores, qui mundo corde digni sunt Deum videre. 604DSenarius enim numerus propter sex dies quibus Deus mundum fecit et sex mundi aetates perfectus est. Denarius vero numerus ad decalogium legis pertinet. Qui per senarium multiplicatus fuerit, sexaginta complent. Sexies deni, sexaginta sunt.

« Omnes tenentes gladios, et ad bella doctissimi, uniuscujusque ensis super femur suum propter timores nocturnos. » Tenent enim gladium spiritus, quod est verbum Dei, qui quod ore docent operibus complent. Ensis autem est custodia vigilans, cordis concupiscentias premens, ne verbum praedicationis immunditia vitae maculet. Nocturnae vero timores sunt insidiae diaboli et tentationes occultae. Neque enim credendum est Salomonem tantae 605Amagnitudinis regem, qui sic immensis divitiis affluebat, ut pondus auri ejus aestimari non posset, et argentum in diebus illius pretium non haberet, quod de lignis Libani ferculum sibi fecerit, ut sequitur:

« Ferculum sibi fecit rex Salomon de lignis Libani. » Ferculum Salomonis est sancta Ecclesia, quae credentes ad aeternae beatitudinis epulas levat, quae de fortibus animo quasi lignis imputribilibus constructa est.

« Columnas ejus fecit argenteas. » Columnae argenteae sunt doctores eloquii luce fulgentes: « Reclinatorium aureum. » Reclinatorium aureum est spes perpetuae quietis fidelibus promissa, sive fulgor claritatis intimae, in qua electorum animae reclinantur in requiem. Est autem columnis argenteis reclinatorium 605Baureum, quia per hoc quod a sanctis praedicatoribus lucere dicitur, mentes audientium fulgorem claritatis in qua reclinentur inveniunt. Per hoc enim quod luculenter et aperte audiunt, in illo quod clarescit in corde requiescunt. Columnae ergo argenteae et reclinatorium aureum factum est, quia per lucem sermonis invenitur apud animum claritas quietis. Ille jam quippe fulget qui interius mentem irradiat, ut per intentionem ibi requiescat, ubi praedicationis gratia non quaeratur. Sed adhuc quod clarum ostenditur intus, qualis sit ascensus adjungit, cum de eodem ferculo protinus subdit: « Ascensum purpureum. » Quid est ascensus purpureus, nisi martyrum sanguis, et passio Redemptoris nostri? Quia non ascenditur ad epulas vitae, nisi per mysterium 605Cpassionis Christi. Et bene ascensus purpureus subditur. Vera quippe purpura, quia de sanguine tingitur, non immerito in colore sanguinis videtur. Et quia maxima multitudo fidelium in exordio nascentis Ecclesiae per martyrii sanguinem pervenit ad regnum. Rex noster ascensum purpureum fecit in ferculo, quia ad clarum quod intus aspicitur per tribulationem sanguinis pervenitur. Quid ergo nos miseri atque ab omni fortitudine destituti, quid acturi sumus? Ecce in hoc ferculo columnae esse non possumus, quia in nobis nec fortitudo operis, nec lumen emicat praedicationis. Reclinatorium non habemus, quia necdum sicut oportet per intellectum spiritualem requiem aeternae charitatis aspicimus. Ascensus purpureus non sumus, quia pro Redemptore 605Dnostro fundere sanguinem non valemus. Quid ergo de nobis agendum est? quae spes erit, si nullus ad regnum pervenit, nisi qui summis praeditus virtutibus fuerit? Sed adest quoque nostra consolatio: Amemus inquantum possumus Deum, diligamus et proximum, et simul quoque ad Dei ferculum pertinemus: quia sicut scriptum est, media charitate constravit. Habet quippe charitatem, et ibi sine dubio praevenis, ubi et columna argentea erigitur, et ascensus purpureus tenetur: nam quia hoc propter nostram infirmitatem dicitur, aperte monstratur cum illic subditur: « Media charitate constravit propter filias Hierusalem. » Filias dicunt, non filios, id est infirmos: per filios Hierusalem infirmantium 606Amentes significat, quibus inter sanctos praedicatores et martyres, per praecepta charitatis in coelestia locum constituit, quia et qui in virtutibus infirmantur, si ipsa bona quae possunt facere cum charitate non negligunt, a Dei aedificatione alieni non sunt. Omnis enim qui charitatem habet Dei et proximi, ad hanc requiem et ad has epulas laetus pervenit: haec omnia ornamenta Ecclesiae praestitit Christus ob nimiam charitatem qua dilexit nos, et tradidit semetipsum pro nobis.

« Egredimini et videte, filiae Hierusalem, regem Salomonem. » Ad hanc itaque laetitiam, et ad hujus mysterii contemplanda secreta, non infirmae, sed perfectae animae convocantur, quae in rege Salomone dominium intelligunt, quibus dicitur: Egredimini 606Bmente et actu de turbulenta mundi conversatione, ut regem valeatis videre. « In diademate, quo coronavit eum mater sua. » Videte Dominum Christum in humanitate, quam de virgine matre susceptam in majestatis paternae dextera collocavit. « In die desponsationis illius. » Id est in tempore incarnationis illius, quo ad copulandam sibi Ecclesiam sponsam ex virginali utero processit. « Et in die laetitiae cordis ejus. » Id est redemptionis humani generis, quae fuit dies laetitiae Christo. Aliter: Admonet ut semper ante oculos cordis habeant sanctae animae regem Salomonem, hoc est pacificum nostrum, qui est Christus. In diademate, id est in illa spinea corona, qua coronavit eum mater sua, Synagoga scilicet Judaeorum, ex qua secundum carnem 606Cgenitus est in die desponsationis ejus, quo sibi desponsavit Ecclesiam. Quae major laetitia esse potuit Christo, quam cum per ejus passionem redemptio facta est humani generis?

CHAPTER III.

« In my little bed by night I sought him whom my soul loveth. I sought him, and found him not. » « I came, as is added in certain codices, and called 602Bhim, and he answered me not. » These things therefore, although they were foretold and seem to have been fulfilled in the Lord's burial — while the women keep vigil at the Lord's sepulchre, when the apostles mourn unbearably — yet the things which are subjoined look further to higher matters; as if the Church, or each holy soul, were to say more openly: Long since with much zeal I sought him, but because I was still subject to the enticements of my flesh, and blinded by the darkness of a deep ignorance, I found not the light of truth, that is, the Lord. In another way the Church or the holy soul seeks her beloved in her little bed, when, undisturbed by every commotion of the world, in the quiet of her heart she thinks upon her Lord. Whom, seeking, she therefore finds not at all, in order that, kindled with love of him, seeking more ardently. By night 602Cwe seek, because even if the mind already keeps watch in him, yet the eye is still dim. « I called him, and he answered me not. » Whoever believes Christ to be man only, will not find him. And whoever does not believe deity in Christ, will not find him. And whoever does not believe deity in Christ, calls indeed upon the Lord, the Son of God, in the nights of his perverse senses, but he does not answer him, as he did not answer king Saul, from whom God had withdrawn. Without cause therefore does he spend labour in seeking, who has not confessed in both natures — equal to men without sin, and coeternal with the Father — God who redeemed, and the man through whom God redeemed those who were lost; of whom the Psalmist says: « A brother hath not redeemed, a man shall redeem (Psal. XLVIII). »

602D« I will rise, and will go about the city: through the streets and the broad ways I will seek him whom my soul loveth. I sought him, and I found him not. » I purposed in my mind to rise from the little bed of carnal pleasures, and to go about lands and seas, and to approach the mysteries of the philosophers, but not even so did I find him. It can also be understood otherwise. The city in this place is understood as the holy Church of the elect, or Holy Scripture. But the streets and the broad ways he calls the books of the law and of the prophatarum, in which the Church or the elect soul, while she ardently seeks her beloved, that is, Christ, finds him — whom she did not discover in the books of the philosophers.

« The watchmen who keep the city found me. Have you seen him whom my soul loveth? » (Psal. CXXVI.) The watchmen who keep the city, that is, the Church, 603Aare the apostles or the other teachers, who found the anxious heathen world by the searching out of truth. Of whom it is said: « Unless the Lord keep the city, they watch in vain that keep it (Psal. CXXVI). » [Vulg. Ps. 126:1 is singular — "in vain hath he watched that keepeth it" — and reads "in vain" with a different word]

« When I had a little passed by, I found him whom my soul loveth. » That is, when I gave myself over to their teaching, at once I found the light of truth which I sought. Otherwise: as we seek, the watchmen who keep the city find us, because the holy Fathers, who keep the state of the Church, meet our good endeavours, that they may teach us either by their word or by their writing. And when we have a little passed them by, we find him whom we love, because our Redeemer, even if in humility he was a man among men, yet in divinity he was above men. 603BFor unless a man has passed by all the saints with the steps of faith and with a pure mind, and has believed Christ more excellent than all the saints, he will by no means be able to find him. « I held his hand, nor will I let him go, till I bring him into my father's house, and into the chamber of her that bore me. » [Vulg. Song 3:4 reads "I held him" and "into the house of my mother"] I hold him with firm faith, until at the end of the world, by the office of preaching, I bring him into the house and into the chamber of the Synagogue, which bore me in the Lord, « and there shall be one fold and one shepherd (Joan. X). » There follows:

« I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, by the roes and the harts of the fields, that you stir not up, nor awake my beloved, till she please. » Wherever therefore in this song like words of believers at divers times are found, the persons of divers 603Cpeoples are brought in. For here the person of that people seems to be introduced which after Christ's ascension, wearied with longing, sought him whom her soul loved — the Church, namely, from the Gentiles, which, being a little fortified with the peace of tranquillity, desired to rest in the sleep of security; and therefore the bridegroom repeats this verse, lest you should think he had less solicitude for the Church gathered from the Gentiles than for the Jews, but that of the two there might be one bride, and she most beloved — which little verse we have already expounded above. The daughters of Jerusalem are the souls of the elect, which behold the vision, that is, the peace of that heavenly Jerusalem. The roes or the harts are understood as the Fathers of the New and of the Old Testament, whose pastures are in the height of contemplation.

603D« Who is she that goeth up by the desert? » This, I think, is the nation going up by the desert, which Paul the teacher of the Gentiles, gathered from Jerusalem as far as Illyricum, and anointed with the ointments of doctrine, and sprinkled with the spices of the mysteries of the sacraments, leads all the way to the bed of the peaceable king, of which we read in what follows. Whence the Synagogue wonders how the people of the Gentiles, cleansed by no mystery of circumcision, instructed by no admonition of the prophets, had suddenly gone up from the lowest pleasures, through the desert of heathenism and of idolatry, to the high summits of the virtues and to the embraces of the bridegroom. Truly it was a desert place where the name of Christ had not been named. « As a little rod of smoke, of the spices 604Aof myrrh and frankincense, and of all the powders of the perfumer. » That is, kindled with the fire of love, or straining toward heavenly things with every effort of the virtues. By myrrh, the mortification of carnal pleasures; by frankincense, the equality of prayers; and in the divers powders of the perfumer, the odour of all the virtues. But morally, when the holy Church of the elect raises herself from this world in holy prayers with burning love, she goes up through the desert which she has deserted. Smoke is born of incense. And by the Psalmist it is said: « Let my prayer be directed as incense in thy sight, O Lord (Ps. CXL). » Smoke is wont to shake out tears. And so the smoke from the spices is the compunction of prayer conceived out of the virtues of love. Which prayer, however, is called a little rod of smoke, because while it asks for heavenly things alone, it goes forth so straight 604Bthat it is not in the least bent back to the seeking of earthly and temporal things. And it is to be noted that it is called not a rod but a little rod, because sometimes, in the ardour of compunction, the force of love is of such subtlety that not even the mind itself, which enlightened has deserved to have it, [can] comprehend it. Well is this said, "of myrrh and frankincense." For frankincense by the law of the Lord is burnt in sacrifice. But by myrrh dead bodies are embalmed, lest they be corrupted by worms. They therefore offer the sacrifice of myrrh and frankincense who both afflict the flesh, lest the vices of corruption have dominion over them, and burn the fragrant victim of their love in the sight of the Lord, and show forth themselves to God in holy virtues. Whence there also is subjoined: « And of all the powders of the perfumer. » The powder of the perfumer 604Cis the virtue of good working. And it is to be noted that the virtues of those who work well are called not perfumes but powders. For when we do any good things, we offer perfumes. But when we go back over those very good things which we do, and take heed by the judgment of review lest there be anything sinister in them, we make, as it were, powder out of perfumes, so that we may burn our prayer to the Lord more subtly by discretion and by love.

« Behold threescore surround the bed of Solomon, of the most valiant of Israel. » [Vulg. Song 3:7 reads "threescore valiant ones surround it"; Migne's lemma omits the word "valiant," which the gloss below expounds] Solomon, who is interpreted the peaceable, signifies the Lord Jesus Christ. The bed of Solomon is the quiet mind of the saints, in whom he himself rests as in a bed. The threescore valiant ones are the holy preachers, and most valiant warriors, who with a clean heart are worthy to see God. 604DFor the number six is perfect, on account of the six days in which God made the world and the six ages of the world. But the number ten pertains to the decalogue of the law. Which, when it has been multiplied by six, they make up sixty. Six times ten are sixty.

« All holding swords, and most expert in war: every man's sword upon his thigh, because of fears in the night. » For they hold the sword of the spirit, which is the word of God, who fulfil in works what they teach with the mouth. But the sword is a watchful guard, pressing down the concupiscences of the heart, lest the uncleanness of life stain the word of preaching. But the fears of the night are the ambushes of the devil and hidden temptations. For neither is it to be believed of Solomon, a king of such 605Agreatness, who so overflowed with immense riches that the weight of his gold could not be reckoned, and silver in his days had no price, that he made himself a litter of the woods of Libanus, as there follows:

« King Solomon made him a litter of the woods of Libanus. » The litter of Solomon is the holy Church, which lifts believers to the banquets of eternal blessedness, which is built of those strong in soul as of woods that cannot rot.

« The pillars thereof he made of silver. » The silver pillars are the teachers shining with the light of eloquence. « The seat of gold. » The golden seat is the hope of perpetual rest promised to the faithful, or the splendour of that inmost brightness in which the souls of the elect recline unto rest. And the golden seat rests upon silver pillars, 605Bbecause through this, that he is said to shine from the holy preachers, the minds of the hearers find the splendour of the brightness in which they may recline. For through this, that they hear clearly and openly, they rest in that which grows bright in the heart. Silver pillars therefore and a golden seat have been made, because through the light of speech there is found in the mind the brightness of rest. For he already shines who inwardly irradiates the mind, so that by intention it may rest there where the grace of preaching is not sought. But further, as to what is shown bright within, he adds of what sort the ascent is, when he immediately subjoins concerning the same litter: « The going up of purple. » What is the purple going up, save the blood of the martyrs and the passion of our Redeemer? For there is no going up to the banquets of life save through the mystery 605Cof Christ's passion. And well is the purple going up subjoined. For true purple, because it is dyed from blood, is not undeservedly seen in the colour of blood. And because the greatest multitude of the faithful, in the beginning of the Church's birth, came to the kingdom through the blood of martyrdom. Our King made a purple going up in the litter, because to that bright thing which is beheld within, one comes through the tribulation of blood. What then shall we do, wretched and destitute of all fortitude? Behold, we cannot be pillars in this litter, because in us neither the fortitude of work nor the light of preaching shines forth. We have no seat, because we do not yet, as we ought, behold through spiritual understanding the rest of eternal charity. We are not the purple going up, because we are not strong enough to pour out our blood for our 605DRedeemer. What then is to be done concerning us? what hope will there be, if none comes to the kingdom save he who has been endowed with the highest virtues? But our consolation too is at hand: let us love God as much as we can, let us love our neighbour also, and we too belong at the same time to God's litter, because, as it is written, he covered it with charity in the midst. For he has charity, and there without doubt thou dost come beforehand, where both the silver pillar is raised and the purple going up is held: for that this is said on account of our weakness is openly shown when it is subjoined there: « He covered it with charity in the midst, because of the daughters of Jerusalem. » They say daughters, not sons, that is, the weak: by the sons of Jerusalem he signifies the minds 606Aof those who are weak, for whom, among the holy preachers and martyrs, he has appointed a place in the heavenly things through the precepts of charity; because those also who are weak in the virtues, if they do not neglect to do with charity the very good things which they can, are not strangers to the building of God. For everyone who has the charity of God and of his neighbour comes with gladness to this rest and to these banquets. All these ornaments Christ bestowed upon the Church, out of the exceeding charity wherewith he loved us, and delivered himself up for us.

« Go forth and see, ye daughters of Jerusalem, king Solomon. » [Vulg. Song 3:11 reads "daughters of Sion"] To this gladness therefore, and to the contemplation of the secrets of this mystery, not the weak but the perfect souls are called together, they who understand dominion in king Solomon, to whom it is said: Go forth 606Bin mind and in deed from the turbulent converse of the world, that you may be able to see the king. « In the diadem wherewith his mother crowned him. » See the Lord Christ in the humanity which, taken from his virgin mother, he set at the right hand of the Father's majesty. « In the day of his espousals. » That is, in the time of his incarnation, in which he came forth from the virginal womb to join to himself the Church as bride. « And in the day of the joy of his heart. » That is, of the redemption of the human race, which was a day of joy to Christ. Otherwise: he admonishes that holy souls should always have before the eyes of the heart king Solomon, that is, our peaceable one, who is Christ. In the diadem, that is, in that crown of thorns wherewith his mother crowned him, namely the Synagogue of the Jews, of which according to the flesh 606Che was begotten, in the day of his espousals, in which he espoused to himself the Church. What greater gladness could there be to Christ, than when through his passion the redemption of the human race was accomplished?

CAPUT IV.

« Quam pulchra es, amica mea, quam pulchra es! » Abrasa scilicet omni vitiorum consuetudine carnis, et a multorum deorum turpium cultu, ad unum verum Deum conversae Ecclesiae geminam ejus pulchritudinem laudat. Pulchram dixit Ecclesiam, et pulchram repetit, quia hanc et praedicatione et actione vidit esse laudabilem. « Oculi tui columbarum. » Sensus tui, inquit, spiritualium sunt rerum contemplatione excellentes ac verecundi. Seu oculi, sancti 606Ddoctores decus populi intelliguntur. « Absque eo quod intrinsecus latet. » Magna est quippe gloria aperti operis, sed longe inoptabilior aeternae retributionis, quae necdum videri potest. « Capilli tui sicut greges caprarum, quae ascenderunt de monte Galaad. » Possunt in capillis fideles populi accipi, qui maximum decus sua numerositate praebent Ecclesiae. Galaad acervus testimonii interpretatur, qui bene convenit adunatae multitudini sanctorum. Possunt per capillos specialiter ipsi qui ex circumcisione crediderunt designari hi ascenderunt de monte Galaad, quia per testimonia sacrarum Scripturarum venerunt ad fidem.

« Dentes tui sicut greges tonsarum, quae ascenderunt 607Ade lavacro. » In capillis fragiliores, videntibus vero perfectiores quique sanctarumque Scripturarum expositores, et ad regendam Ecclesiam apti designantur, qui lacte doctrinae non indigent, solidum cibum mandendo aliis impertiunt. Qui ascenderunt de lavacro, id est fonte sacri baptismatis, qui et tonsi et loti sunt, hoc est, nudati rebus renuntiantes saeculo, et vitae lavacro mundati. « Omnes gemellis fetibus. » Id est gemina charitate, vel fide et opere fecundi. « Et sterilis non est inter eas: » non est qui fetus boni operis non agat.

« Sicut vitta coccinea labia tua, et eloquium tuum dulce. » Coccum color est sanguinis vitta coccinea (myrrha dicitur) cruorem designat beatorum martyrum. Vel vitta coccinea doctrina veritatis 607Bintelligitur. Labia sponsae, id est Ecclesiae, cocco assimilantur, quia Dominici sanguinis pretium quo redempta est praedicare non cessat, et in praedicatione ab ardore sanctae charitatis flammescit, et auditores suos in dilectione Dei ardentes efficit. « Sicut fragmen mali punici, ita genae tuae, absque eo quod intrinsecus latet. » In genis verecundia, in malo punico passio Christi exprimitur. Habet ergo ruborem in genis sponsa mali punici, cum sacramentum Dominicae crucis verbis fatetur, et factis probat. Aliter: Illi enim faciem Ecclesiae decoram faciunt, qui, comitante verecundia, castitatem servant. Vel certe genae Ecclesiae sunt sancti martyres, qui imitantes Christi passionem, roseo sanguinis sui velut fragmen mali punici decore perfusi sunt.

607C« Sicut turris David collum tuum, quae aedificata est cum propugnaculis suis. » Turris David sancta Ecclesia est, sive perfectio vel doctrina sanctorum, quae non a se, sed a Christo est. Collum ipsi sunt praedicatores, quorum et constantia eadem civitas firma est, et undique inexpugnabilis. Propugnacula autem ejusdem civitatis Scripturarum sacrarum munimina sunt, vel patrum precedentium exempla. « Mille clypei pendent ex ea. » Quia quot in divinis libris praecepta sunt, tot sunt nostri pectoris munimina, quibus contra insidias omnes defendimur. « Omnis armatura fortium. » Omnis instructio est, vel operationis, vel doctrinae coelestis, per quam non solum evadimus, sed etiam superamus. Dicamus prout papa Gregorius more suo mirabiliter intonat. 607DIn collo etenim guttur in gutture vox est. Quid per collum sanctae Ecclesiae, nisi sacra eloquio designantur? In qua mille clypei dependere memorantur: per hunc perfectum numerum numerus universus ostenditur, quia universa nostra munitio in sacro eloquio continetur. Ibi quippe sunt praecepta Dei, ibi exempla justorum. Si enim torpet animus a Conditoris suo desiderio, audiat quod dicitur: « Diliges Dominum Deum tuum (Exod. XX), » et reliqua. In odio fortasse labitur proximi, audiat quod dicitur: « Diliges proximum tuum sicut teipsum (Matth. XXII). » Res alienas concupiscit, audiat quod illic scriptum est: « Non concupisces rem proximi tui (Exod. XX). » De injuria quae a proximi ore vel facto 608Aillata est ad iram mens accenditur, audiat quod dicitur: « Non quaeras ultionem, nec memor eris injuriae civium tuorum (Levit. XIX). » In carnis concupiscentia male sauciata mens accenditur, ne sequatur oculus mentem, audiat quod dictum est: « Qui viderit mulierem ad concupiscendum eam, jam moechatus est eam in corde suo (Matth. V). » Contra inimicum forsitan quisque animum suum relaxare disponit in odio, audiat quod illic scriptum est: « Diligite inimicos vestros (Ibid.), » et reliqua. Sed is qui aliena jam non rapit, adhuc forsitan inordinate retinet, audiat quod illic dicitur: « Vendite quae possidetis, et date eleemosynam (Luc. XII). » Infirmantis animus qui perfrui desiderat Deo simul et saeculo, audiat quod illic scriptum est: « Nemo 608Bpotest duobus dominis servire (Matth. VI). » Alius non ad necessitatem stipendii, sed ad voluntatem desiderii possessa retinet, audiat quod illic dicitur: « Qui non renuntiaverit omnibus quae possidet, non potest esse meus discipulus (Luc. XIV). » In collo Ecclesiae, id est in sacri eloquii praedicatione, quae pro sui munitione et altitudine turri David similis dicitur, mille clypei dependent, quia quot illic praecepta sunt, tot etiam pectoris nostri munimina, sicut dictum est: Ad servandam itaque innocentiam etiam laesi a proximo perdurare in humilitate festinamus. Abel ante oculos veniat, qui et occisus dicitur a fratre, et non legitur reluctatus. Sic de caeteris sanctorum exemplis sentiendum. Quia ergo in voce sacri eloquii cujuslibet si quaerimus munimina 608Cvirtutis invenimus. Mille clypei dependent ex ea, omnis armatura fortium. Ecce enim contra aereas potestates, si festinamus fortes existere in hac turri armaturam nostrae mentis invenimus, ut inde praecepta Conditoris, inde sumamus exempla praecedentium patrum, per quam contra adversarios nostros inexpugnabiliter armemur. Et notandum quia aedificata cum propugnaculis suis dicitur. Hic quippe agunt propugnacula quod clypei, quia utraque pugnantem muniunt. Sed hoc inter utraque distat, quia clypeos pro nostro munimine ubicunque volumus movemus, propugnaculo autem defendi possumus, sed hoc movere non possumus. Clypeus in manu est: nam propugnaculum non tenetur. Quid ergo inter propugnacula et clypeos distat, nisi quod in sacro 608Deloquio patrum praecedentium et miracula legimus, quae operari non valemus, et virtutes bonorum operum audimus, quibus operando muniri possumus?

« Duo ubera tua sicut duo hinnuli capreae gemelli, qui pascuntur in liliis donec aspiret dies et inclinentur umbrae. » Duo ubera Ecclesiae, duo Testamenta. Duo hinnuli, duo populi sunt ex circumcisione venientes et gentilitate, quia inde nutriuntur. Quique per humilitatem parvulos se intelligunt et peccatores, et cum charitate currentes omnia mundi transeunt obstacula. Qui pascuntur in liliis, hoc est candidissimis sanctorum exemplis vel doctrinis. De quibus superius tanquam ex armatura fortium disputatum legimus. Donec aspiret dies aeternus, et transeant 609Aumbrae vitae praesentis. Et hinc jam superius disputavimus. « Vadam ad montem myrrhae et ad collem thuris. » Per myrrham mortificatio carnis, per thus devotio orationis exprimitur. Quasi diceret sponsus: Frequentabo eos, et pia propitius illustratione glorificabo quos per passiones sive orationes in virtutibus sublimes esse invenio. Aliter: Vox est imperfectorum, qui cum ad montem myrrhae et collem thuris ascendunt, quando illorum qui per mortificationem carnis et purissimas orationes placuerunt, imitatores esse desiderant.

« Tota pulchra es, amica mea, et macula non est in te. » Hoc est quod ait Apostolus (Eph. V): « Ut exhiberet sibi gloriosam Ecclesiam non habentem maculam » malae operationis, « nec rugam » induplicatae 609Bfidei.

« Veni de Libano, sponsa mea, veni de Libano, veni. » Libanus candor interpretatur, id est anima bonis actibus candidata, quam tertio hortatur sponsus ut veniat. Primo adveniens in carne per bona opera, secundo absoluta carne ad percipiendam vitam aeternam, tertio recepto corpore ad perfruenda post resurrectionem gaudia perfecta. « Coronaberis, de capite Amana, de vertice Sanir et Hermon, de cubilibus leonum, de montibus pardorum. » Leones propter superbiam, pardi propter crudelitatem maligni spiritus, montium vero nomine superba infidelium corda, ubi immundi spiritus sedem habent, designantur. Dum sancti praedicatores tales ad vitam salutis convertunt, coronantur de capite et vertice 609Cmontium, id est principibus iniquorum: quia de labore certaminis crescit corona gloriae. Aliter: Libanus, ut dictum est, candidatio interpretatur. Nisi enim quis per candorem sacri baptismatis remissionem peccatorum acceperit, ad Christum venire non potest. Sanir interpretatur enim lucerna: Amana, iniquitas: Hermon vero consecratio. Quae omnia referuntur ad Christum, qui quos post iniquitatem illuminat, consecrat, ipsosque coronat. De cubilibus leonum veniunt, qui de regum principumque hujus saeculi domibus ad fidem currunt. De montibus, inquit, pardorum, qui relicta elatione superbiae vel diversorum philosophorum traditione, simplices et humiles pergunt ad Christum.

« Vulnerasti cor meum, soror mea, sponsa, vulnerasti 609Dcor meum. » In vulneratione cordis magnitudo amoris Christi in Ecclesia intelligitur. « In uno oculorum tuorum, et in uno crine colli tui. » Id est in unitate sancta doctorum, et in uno crine colli tui, id est in unitate pia subjectae plebis, id est crinium. Item: In illo itaque oculo Ecclesiae Christo complacuit, quo non temporalia, sed aeterna conspexit. Unde Psalmista: « Unam petii a Domino, hanc requiram: ut inhabitem in domo Domini omnibus diebus vitae meae » (Psal. XXVI). « Et in uno crine colli, » qui non ornari visibilibus studuit, sed aeternis. Quae Ecclesia, soror Christi pariter et sponsa est. Sponsa scilicet per fidem, soror per dilectionem.

610A« Quam pulchrae sunt mammae tuae, soror mea, sponsa. » In mammarum nomine, sancti doctores propter consolationem infirmorum et parvulorum sustentationem exprimuntur. Suavitas gratiae pulchrior est austeritate legis. « Et odor unguentorum tuorum super omnia aromata. » Fama suavissima diffusa per orbem totum fidei latior est, quam veteris legis, et patrum in ea quae in sola Judaea coangustantur. Quid per aromata, nisi Veteris Testamenti dona, quae odori Ecclesiae, id est gratiae Spiritus sancti, seu virtutibus comparantur pulcherrimis. Perseverat semper sponsus vices reddendo Ecclesiae, sicut eum Ecclesia laudaverat superius. Ideo ait: « Vulnerasti cor meum, soror mea, » et reliqua.

« Favus distillans labia tua, sponsa. » Quid per 610Bceram, nisi litteram? et per mel, nisi sensus spiritualis? Mel in cera est spiritualis divinorum eloquiorum sensus in littera. Mel ceram distillat, quia multiplices sensus pene singulae pariunt sententiae. Labia sponsae sunt doctores, qui multifarios sensus sacris litteris inesse pandunt. « Mel et lac sub lingua tua. » In lacte eruditio parvulorum, in melle fortior doctrina perfectorum signatur. Sub lingua, id est in meditatione cordis. Non est necesse subnectere, quid inde Paulus apostolus loquatur: « Et odor vestimentorum tuorum sicut odor thuris. » Vestimenta Ecclesiae bona sunt ejus opera, quae odori thuris comparantur: quia cuncta quae sancta pro Domino agit Ecclesia, orationum pro ea vicem reddunt. Intelliguntur etenim vestimenta Ecclesiae ipsius praedicatores, 610Cpuras ad Dominum mundasque mittentes orationes.

« Hortus conclusus, soror mea, sponsa, hortus conclusus, fons signatus. » Superius in praefatione succinctim disputare decrevimus. Hortus conclusus Ecclesia est: conclusus, quia Domini protectione munita, in sancta consistit fide; hortus, quia multifaria spiritualium operum germina gignit, sive propter filios per baptismum spirituales dicitur. Fons est, quia doctrina salutari redundat. Signatus dicitur sermone fidei.

« Emissiones tuae paradisus malorum punicorum cum pomorum fructibus. » Per irrigationem sacri baptismatis, sancta Ecclesia paradisum ex se mittit, quia homines quos per sanctum generat baptismum, 610Dad coelestem paradisum dirigere cupit. Mala punica sancti dicuntur martyres; pomorum fructus bonorum operum suavitates intelliguntur. Quia vero non solum imperium, sed adventus instat, festinantius jam hinc sententias libri decerpere disposuimus, quam superius protelare coepissemus, ne modum libelli excedere videremur, sicuti fassi sumus.

« Cyprus cum nardo, nardus cum croco, fistula et cinnamomum. » Cyprus arbor aromatica est, significans coelestis gratiae benedictionem. Nardus Dominicae passionis typum tenet. Crocus charitatis fervorem exprimit. Conjungitur cyprus nardo, cum divina gratia coufortat nos Christo compati. Item nardus cum croco conjungitur, cum per charitatem 611AChristi mortem libenter suscipimus. Fistula, quae et cassa dicitur, arbor aromatica est, sed modica, et ideo humiles spiritu designat. Item cinnamomum, qui seipsos decipiunt, signat, et ipsa est arbor brevis, sed odorifera et dulcis. Sic humilitas magnam habet apud eum laudem et dulcedinem. « Cum universis lignis Libani. » Sicut fistula et cinnamomum humiles sanctorum cogitationes, sic et ligna Libani sublimes eorum actiones designant: « quia non potest arbor bona fructus malos facere (Matth. VII). » « Myrrha et aloe cum omnibus primis unguentis. » Myrrha et aloe arbores sunt aromaticae, quae mortificationes vitiorum et poenitentiam carnis exprimunt. Cum omnibus primis unguentis, id est charismatibus virtutum excellentioribus. Et 611Bpulchra est conjunctio harum arborum cum unguentis, quia dum carnem a lascivia refrenamus, consequens est ut majora Spiritus dona percipiamus.

« Fons hortorum, puteus aquarum viventium, quae fluunt impetu de Libano. » Utrumque est Ecclesia, et fons est hortorum, quia spirituales gignit fructus, et puteus aquarum viventium, propter occulta mysteria, quae sanctis per revelationem sancti Spiritus solis panduntur. Aquarum viventium, propter eloquia divina, quae de invisibilibus divinae gratiae procedunt thesauris. Quae impetu fluunt de Libano, id est de ipsa Ecclesia, quae et candida est per munditiam fidei, et alta per virtutum gloriam. Aliter: Per fontem hortorum Christus intelligitur, irrigans Ecclesiam suam. Puteus aquarum Spiritus 611Cintelligitur sanctus, a quo dicuntur diversa dona procedere: quae viventes vocantur, quia quos replent viventes faciunt. Quae fluunt de Libano, quia Spiritus sanctus a Filio procedit. Potest per fontem, qui aperte currit, Christus intelligi secundum humanitatem, per puteum qui occultam habet aquam atque profundissimam, idem ipsum secundum divinitatem. De quo impetu aquae fluunt, ut ipse in Evangelio testatur: « Qui credit in me, flumina de ventre ejus fluent aquae vivae (Joan. VII). »

« Surge, Aquilo, et veni, Auster, perfla hortum meum, et fluent aromata illius. » Vox est ista sponsi: In aquilone enim adversa mundi, in austro blandimenta designantur, quia gemina expugnatione probatur Ecclesia. Surge, permittentis est vox, 611Dnon imperantis. In aromatibus, virtutes conscientiae miro odore dispositae intelliguntur. Aliter: Per aquilonem, qui fertur esse frigidus et terram congelare, ne fructus pariat, diabolus intelligitur. Cui ab horto, id est ab Ecclesia praecipitur recedere. Per austrum vero, qui ventus est calidus, et terram a frigoribus absolvere dicitur, sanctus intimatur Spiritus. Qui flando, id est suis sanctam illustrando Ecclesiam donis, facit illam id diversis germinare virtutibus. Bene ait: « Surge, Aquilo, et veni, Auster, » et reliqua. Quia nimirum dum sanctam Ecclesiam donorum suorum virtutibus Spiritus veritatis impleverit, ab ea longe lateque odores boni operis spargit.

CHAPTER IV.

« How beautiful thou art, my love, how beautiful thou art! » He praises, that is, the twofold beauty of the Church, scraped clean of every habit of the vices of the flesh, and turned from the worship of many foul gods to the one true God. He called the Church beautiful, and he repeats "beautiful," because he saw her to be praiseworthy both in preaching and in action. « Thy eyes are of doves. » Thy senses, he says, are excellent in the contemplation of spiritual things, and modest. Or by the eyes the holy 606Dteachers are understood, the glory of the people. « Besides that which lieth hid within. » Great indeed is the glory of open work, but far more undesirable is that of the eternal recompense, which cannot yet be seen. « Thy hair is as flocks of goats, which have come up from mount Galaad. » By the hairs the faithful peoples may be understood, who by their multitude furnish the greatest adornment to the Church. Galaad is interpreted a heap of testimony, which well suits the united multitude of the saints. By the hairs there may be designated in particular those very men who believed from the circumcision; these came up from mount Galaad, because through the testimonies of the Holy Scriptures they came to the faith.

« Thy teeth are as flocks of the shorn, which have come up 607Afrom the washing. » In the hairs the more frail are designated, but to those seeing, all the more perfect, and the expounders of the holy Scriptures, and those fit for governing the Church, who do not need the milk of doctrine, and who by chewing impart solid food to others. Which have come up from the washing, that is, from the font of holy baptism — who are both shorn and washed, that is, stripped of their goods, renouncing the world, and cleansed by the washing of life. « All with twin offspring. » That is, fruitful with a twofold charity, or with faith and work. « And there is none barren among them: » there is none who does not bring forth the offspring of good work.

« Thy lips are as a scarlet fillet, and thy speech is sweet. » Scarlet is the color of blood; the scarlet fillet (it is called myrrh) designates the gore of the blessed martyrs. Or the scarlet fillet is understood as the doctrine of truth. 607BThe lips of the bride, that is, of the Church, are likened to scarlet, because she does not cease to preach the price of the Lord's blood by which she was redeemed, and in preaching she blazes with the ardor of holy charity, and makes her hearers burning in the love of God. « As a piece of a pomegranate, so are thy cheeks, besides that which lieth hid within. » In the cheeks modesty is expressed, in the pomegranate the passion of Christ. The bride therefore has in her cheeks the redness of a pomegranate, when she confesses in words the mystery of the Lord's cross, and proves it by deeds. Otherwise: For those make the face of the Church comely who, with modesty accompanying, keep chastity. Or assuredly the cheeks of the Church are the holy martyrs, who, imitating the passion of Christ, have been suffused with the rosy comeliness of their own blood, like a piece of a pomegranate.

607C« Thy neck is as the tower of David, which is built with its own bulwarks. » The tower of David is holy Church, or the perfection or doctrine of the saints, which is not from itself, but from Christ. The neck is the preachers themselves, by whose constancy that same city is both firm and on every side unconquerable. But the bulwarks of that same city are the defenses of the holy Scriptures, or the examples of the preceding fathers. « A thousand bucklers hang upon it. » Because as many as are the precepts in the divine books, so many are the defenses of our breast, by which we are defended against all snares. « All the armor of valiant men. » It is every equipping, whether of work or of heavenly doctrine, through which we not only escape but even overcome. Let us speak as Pope Gregory, after his manner, wonderfully thunders. 607DFor in the neck is the throat, in the throat the voice. What is designated by the neck of holy Church, if not the sacred utterances? On which a thousand bucklers are recorded to hang: by this perfect number the universal number is shown, because our whole fortification is contained in sacred utterance. For there are the precepts of God, there the examples of the just. For if the soul grows sluggish from the desire of its Creator, let it hear what is said: « Thou shalt love the Lord thy God (Exod. XX), » and the rest. Perhaps it slips into hatred of its neighbor: let it hear what is said: « Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself (Matth. XXII). » It covets the goods of others: let it hear what is written there: « Thou shalt not covet the property of thy neighbor (Exod. XX). » [Vulg. domum proximi tui, "the house of thy neighbor"] The mind is kindled to anger over an injury inflicted by a neighbor's mouth or deed: 608Alet it hear what is said: « Seek not revenge, nor shalt thou be mindful of the injury of thy fellow citizens (Levit. XIX). » The mind, ill-wounded, is kindled in the concupiscence of the flesh; lest the eye follow the mind, let it hear what was said: « He that shall look on a woman to lust after her, hath already committed adultery with her in his heart (Matth. V). » Perhaps someone purposes to let his soul loose in hatred against an enemy: let him hear what is written there: « Love your enemies (Ibid.), » and the rest. But he who now seizes not the goods of others, yet perhaps still holds them in a disordered way: let him hear what is said there: « Sell what you possess, and give alms (Luc. XII). » The soul of one growing weak, who desires to enjoy God and the world at once: let it hear what is written there: « No man 608Bcan serve two masters (Matth. VI). » Another keeps his possessions not for the necessity of a livelihood, but for the will of desire: let him hear what is said there: « He that shall not renounce all that he possesses, cannot be my disciple (Luc. XIV). » On the neck of the Church, that is, in the preaching of sacred utterance — which for its fortification and its height is called like the tower of David — a thousand bucklers hang, because as many as are the precepts there, so many also are the defenses of our breast, as has been said: And so, for the keeping of innocence, even when wronged by a neighbor we hasten to persevere in humility. Let Abel come before our eyes, who is both said to have been slain by his brother, and is not read to have resisted. So is it to be judged of the other examples of the saints. Because therefore in the voice of sacred utterance, if we seek the defenses 608Cof any virtue whatever, we find them. A thousand bucklers hang upon it, all the armor of valiant men. For behold, if we hasten to stand strong against the powers of the air, in this tower we find the armor of our mind, so that from it we may take the precepts of the Creator, from it the examples of the preceding fathers, by which we may be armed unconquerably against our adversaries. And it is to be noted that it is said to be built with its own bulwarks. For here the bulwarks do what the bucklers do, since both fortify the fighter. But this differs between the two, that we move bucklers wherever we wish for our defense, whereas by a bulwark we can be defended, but we cannot move it. The buckler is in the hand: for the bulwark is not held. What then differs between bulwarks and bucklers, save that in sacred 608Dutterance we both read the miracles of the preceding fathers, which we are not able to work, and hear the virtues of good works, by which in working we can be fortified?

« Thy two breasts are like two twin fawns of a roe, that feed among the lilies, till the day break and the shadows incline. » The two breasts of the Church are the two Testaments. The two fawns are the two peoples, coming from the circumcision and from heathendom, because from thence they are nourished. And these through humility understand themselves to be little ones and sinners, and running with charity they pass over all the obstacles of the world. Which feed among the lilies, that is, among the most white examples or doctrines of the saints. Of which we read that it was discussed above, as it were from the armor of valiant men. Till the eternal day break, and the shadows of the present life pass away. 609AAnd of this we have already discussed above. « I will go to the mountain of myrrh and to the hill of frankincense. » By myrrh the mortification of the flesh is expressed, by frankincense the devotion of prayer. As if the Bridegroom were to say: I will frequent them, and, propitious, I will glorify with a holy illumination those whom I find to be sublime in virtues through sufferings or prayers. Otherwise: It is the voice of the imperfect, who ascend to the mountain of myrrh and the hill of frankincense when they desire to be imitators of those who were pleasing through the mortification of the flesh and most pure prayers.

« Thou art all fair, my love, and there is no spot in thee. » This is what the Apostle says (Eph. V): « That he might present to himself a glorious Church, not having spot » of evil working, « nor wrinkle » of a doubled 609Bfaith.

« Come from Libanus, my bride, come from Libanus, come. » Libanus is interpreted whiteness, that is, the soul made white by good acts, whom the Bridegroom exhorts a third time to come. First, coming in the flesh through good works; second, released from the flesh to receive eternal life; third, the body having been taken back, to enjoy the perfect joys after the resurrection. « Thou shalt be crowned from the head of Amana, from the top of Sanir and Hermon, from the dens of lions, from the mountains of leopards. » Lions on account of pride, leopards on account of cruelty, designate the malign spirits; but by the name of mountains are designated the proud hearts of unbelievers, where unclean spirits have their seat. While holy preachers convert such men to the life of salvation, they are crowned from the head and from the top 609Cof the mountains, that is, from the princes of the wicked: because from the labor of the contest the crown of glory grows. Otherwise: Libanus, as has been said, is interpreted a whitening. For unless a man has received the remission of sins through the whiteness of holy baptism, he cannot come to Christ. For Sanir is interpreted a lamp; Amana, iniquity; but Hermon, consecration. All which are referred to Christ, who consecrates those whom he enlightens after iniquity, and crowns them also. They come from the dens of lions who run to the faith from the houses of the kings and princes of this world. From the mountains, he says, of leopards, they who, having left behind the elation of pride or the tradition of divers philosophers, go simple and humble to Christ.

« Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, my bride, thou hast wounded 609Dmy heart. » In the wounding of the heart the greatness of Christ's love in the Church is understood. « With one of thy eyes, and with one hair of thy neck. » That is, in the holy unity of the teachers; and with one hair of thy neck, that is, in the devout unity of the subject people, that is, of the hairs. Again: With that eye, therefore, the Church was pleasing to Christ, with which she looked not on temporal things, but on eternal. Whence the Psalmist: « One thing I have asked of the Lord, this will I seek after: that I may dwell in the house of the Lord all the days of my life » (Psal. XXVI). « And with one hair of the neck, » which sought to be adorned not with visible things, but with eternal. And this Church is alike the sister of Christ and his bride. Bride, that is, through faith; sister, through love.

610A« How beautiful are thy breasts, my sister, my bride. » By the name of breasts the holy teachers are expressed, on account of the consolation of the weak and the sustaining of little ones. The sweetness of grace is more beautiful than the austerity of the law. « And the odor of thy ointments above all spices. » The most sweet report of the faith, spread through the whole world, is broader than that of the old law and of the fathers in it, which are confined within Judea alone. What is meant by spices, save the gifts of the Old Testament, which are compared to the odor of the Church, that is, to the grace of the Holy Spirit, or to her most beautiful virtues. The Bridegroom continues always to render like for like to the Church, just as the Church had praised him above. Therefore he says: « Thou hast wounded my heart, my sister, » and the rest.

« Thy lips are a dropping honeycomb, O bride. » What is meant by 610Bthe wax, if not the letter? and by the honey, if not the spiritual sense? The honey in the wax is the spiritual sense of the divine utterances within the letter. The honey drips from the wax, because almost every single sentence brings forth manifold senses. The lips of the bride are the teachers, who lay open the manifold senses that lie within the sacred letters. « Honey and milk are under thy tongue. » In the milk the instruction of little ones, in the honey the stronger doctrine of the perfect is signified. Under the tongue, that is, in the meditation of the heart. There is no need to subjoin what Paul the Apostle says thereupon: « And the odor of thy garments is as the odor of frankincense. » The garments of the Church are her good works, which are compared to the odor of frankincense: because all the holy things that the Church does for the Lord render in return the office of prayers on her behalf. For the garments of the Church are understood as her preachers, 610Csending up pure and clean prayers to the Lord.

« My sister, my bride, is a garden enclosed, a garden enclosed, a fountain sealed up. » Above, in the preface, we determined to discuss this succinctly. The garden enclosed is the Church: enclosed, because, fortified by the Lord's protection, she stands firm in the holy faith; a garden, because she brings forth manifold seeds of spiritual works, or she is so called on account of the sons who are spiritual through baptism. She is a fountain, because she overflows with saving doctrine. She is called sealed up by the word of faith.

« Thy shoots are a paradise of pomegranates with the fruits of the orchard. » Through the watering of holy baptism, holy Church sends forth a paradise out of herself, because the men whom she begets through holy baptism 610Dshe desires to direct to the paradise of heaven. The pomegranates are called the holy martyrs; by the fruits of the orchard the sweetnesses of good works are understood. But because not only the command but also the coming is at hand, we have resolved from this point to pluck the sentences of the book more hastily than we had begun to draw them out above, lest we should seem to exceed the measure of the little book, as we have confessed.

« Cypress with spikenard, spikenard with saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon. » [Vulg. nardus et crocus, "spikenard and saffron"] The cypress is an aromatic tree, signifying the blessing of heavenly grace. Spikenard holds the type of the Lord's passion. Saffron expresses the fervor of charity. The cypress is joined to the spikenard when divine grace strengthens coufortat us to suffer with Christ. Again, the spikenard is joined with the saffron when through charity 611Awe willingly undergo the death of Christ. Sweet cane, which is also called cassa, is an aromatic tree, but a small one, and therefore it designates the humble in spirit. Again cinnamon signifies those who deceive themselves, and it too is a low tree, but fragrant and sweet. So humility has great praise and sweetness with him. « With all the trees of Libanus. » As sweet cane and cinnamon designate the humble thoughts of the saints, so also the trees of Libanus designate their lofty actions: « because a good tree cannot bring forth evil fruits (Matth. VII). » « Myrrh and aloes with all the chief ointments. » Myrrh and aloes are aromatic trees, which express the mortifications of the vices and the penance of the flesh. With all the chief ointments, that is, with the more excellent free gifts of the virtues. And 611Bbeautiful is the joining of these trees with the ointments, because while we bridle the flesh from wantonness, it follows that we receive the greater gifts of the Spirit.

« The fountain of gardens, the well of living waters, which flow with force from Libanus. » The Church is both: she is the fountain of gardens, because she begets spiritual fruits, and the well of living waters, on account of the hidden mysteries which are laid open to the saints alone through the revelation of the Holy Spirit. Of living waters, on account of the divine utterances which proceed from the invisible treasures of divine grace. Which flow with force from Libanus, that is, from the Church herself, who is both white through the cleanness of faith, and lofty through the glory of the virtues. Otherwise: By the fountain of gardens Christ is understood, watering his Church. By the well of waters the Holy 611CSpirit is understood, from whom divers gifts are said to proceed: which are called living, because those whom they fill they make to live. Which flow from Libanus, because the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Son. By the fountain, which runs openly, Christ can be understood according to his humanity; by the well, which has hidden and most deep water, the same according to his divinity. From whom the waters flow with force, as he himself bears witness in the Gospel: « He that believeth in me, out of his belly shall flow rivers of living water (Joan. VII). »

« Arise, O north wind, and come, O south wind, blow through my garden, and the aromatical spices thereof shall flow. » This is the voice of the Bridegroom: for in the north wind the adversities of the world are designated, in the south wind its blandishments, because by a twofold assault the Church is proved. Arise: this is the voice of one permitting, 611Dnot of one commanding. In the aromatical spices are understood the virtues of the conscience, set in order with a marvelous odor. Otherwise: By the north wind, which is said to be cold and to freeze the earth, lest it bring forth fruit, the devil is understood. He is bidden to withdraw from the garden, that is, from the Church. But by the south wind, which is a warm wind, and is said to loose the earth from the frosts, the Holy Spirit is intimated. Who by blowing, that is, by enlightening his holy Church with his gifts, makes her to bud it forth in divers virtues. Well does he say: « Arise, O north wind, and come, O south wind, » and the rest. Because assuredly, when the Spirit of truth has filled holy Church with the virtues of his gifts, he scatters from her far and wide the odors of good work.

CAPUT V.

  • 612A

« Veniat dilectus meus in hortum suum, et comedat fructum pomorum suorum. » Id est veniat Christus in Ecclesiam suam, et eam ipse conservet ubique immaculatam, et fidei fructu fecundet, et libenter inspiciat, gratanterque accipiat opera sanctorum suorum. « Veni in hortum meum, soror mea, sponsa. » Haec vox non est imperantis, sed indicantis praeteriti temporis. Ac si diceret: Veni, inquit, saepissime in Ecclesiam meam et venio ut corrigam, adjuvem et confirmem, sicuti poposcisti. « Messui myrrham meam cum aromatibus meis. » Per myrrham passio vel mortificatio, per aromata omnes virtutes exprimuntur.
