« Soror mea parvula est, et ubera non habet. Quid faciemus sorori nostrae, in die quando alloquenda est? » Prima nascentis Ecclesiae de gentibus tempora designat, quoniam adhuc et parva fuit numero, 625Det minus idonea praedicare verbum, ut Apostolus loquitur: « Non potui loqui vobis quasi spiritalibus, sed quasi carnalibus, tanquam parvulis in Christo, lac potum vobis dedi non escam (I Cor. III). » Quasi vox sponsi vel apostolorum, de ipsa ad Synagogam dixisset: « Quid faciemus sorori nostrae, in die quando alloquenda est? » Ac si aperte dicat: Parva quidem est Ecclesia gentium, et nec Dei verbi mysterium sufficit subire. Quid ergo tibi videtur, o Synagoga, de sorore nostra faciendum, quando alloquenda est, id est per verbi mysterium ducenda in fidem? Synagoga tacente, ipse sponsus, sive apostoli quid fieri debeat, respondit.

« Si murus est, aedificemus super eam propugnacula 626Aargentea. » Ac si diceret: Si aliquos habet in se fortes in fide, vel claros ingenio, vel philosophia instructos, addamus illis propugnacula argentea, id est scientiam divinarum Scripturarum, ut eo facilius possint protegere infirmos atque indoctos. « Si ostium est, compingamus illud tabulis cedrinis. » Id est, si sint simplices, tamen docendi studio inhiantes, et velut ostium quod aperitur et clauditur, « tempus tacendi vel loquendi » intelligunt: proponamus illis priorum exempla justorum, quo certius et efficacius docendi officium implere possint. Cedri virtutes sanctorum, et tabulae latitudinem designant charitatis. Et ut cum Apostolo dicere valeant: « Mihi autem absit gloriari, nisi in cruce Domini nostri Jesu Christi per quem mihi 626Bmundus crucifixus est, et ego mundo (Gal. VI). » Ad haec namque ipsa respondit Ecclesia:

« Ego murus, et ubera mea sicut turris, ex quo facta sum coram te quasi pacem reperiens. » Ego de vivis compacta sum lapidibus, et glutino charitatis adunata, et super fundamentum immobile aedificata, et doctores mei fortissimi velut turris, qui et parvulos nutrire sciunt, et omnia maligna expellere; et accidit mihi ex eo tempore quo Christus me reconciliavit, pacem per eum reperiri.

« Vinea fuit pacifico in ea quae habet populus, tradidit eam custodibus. » De quo Apostolus ait: « Ipse est pax nostra, qui fecit utraque unum (Ephes. II). » Et de quo propheta: « Multiplicabitur ejus 626Cimperium, et pacis non erit finis (Isa. IX). » Vinea, id est catholica Ecclesia, fructu abundans fidei fuit pacifico, id est Christo qui omnia pacificavit in coelis et in terris, in ea congregatione qua multos populos possidet ex toto orbe, non in Judaea solum, tradidit eam custodibus, sed et prophetis vel apostolis sive evangelicis dogmatibus, vel angelicis dignitatibus. « Vir affert pro fructu ejus mille argenteos. » Id est Christus appendens regnum coelorum pro sanctis laboribus vel eleemosynis ejus. Potest et aliter intelligi: « Vir affert pro fructu ejus mille argenteos, » id est pro retributione aeterna. Hujus vineae vir affert mille argenteos, id est pro acquisitione coelestis regni, qui est fructus violae, cuncta quae mundis reliquit. Millenarius numerus pro perfectione accipitur illius, qui omnia reliquit. Unde et argentei pro omni 626Dpecunia accipiuntur.

« Vinea mea coram me est. » Vox sponsi, quia post resurrectionem futuram sancti omnes semper cum Domino erunt, sicut ipse ait: « Volo, Pater, ut ubi ego sum et illi sint mecum (Joan. I). » Aliter, ac si diceret sponsus: Verum etsi te aliis commendem custodibus, tamen te semper habeo in mea praesentia, videns et remunerans laboris in omnibus devotionem. « Mille tui pacifici. » « Mille tui, » subauditur argentei. Quasi dixisset: Qui amore meo cuncta sua dimittit, in pace habere servata cognoscat, significans simpliciores in Ecclesia qui omnem substantiam millenario signatam numero pro charitate Dei amittunt: « Et ducenti his custodiunt fructus 627Aejus. » Id est. argentei his qui custodiunt fructus ejus, qui sunt doctores sancti, qui omnia mundi dimittunt et in verbo praedicationis laborare non cessant, hi duplici remuneratione, quae centenario designatur numero, donantur apud me. Potest et aliter intelligi: Duodecim quippe si per centenarium numerum multiplicantur, mille ducentos efficiunt. Qui numerus utique apostolis vel apostolicis viris congruit. Hi pacifici, id est Christi discipuli, merito appellantur, a quibus sancta regitur Ecclesia:

« Quae habitas in hortis, amici auscultant te, fac me audire vocem tuam. » In hortis enim Ecclesia habitat, quae jam viriditate spei et bonorum repleta est operum. Ac si aperte dicat: Quia locutio finienda est, hoc ultimum vale a me auditum semper habita 627Bin portis virtutum, et scito quod amici, id est angelici spiritus, et animae sanctorum separatae considerant, et de tuo gaudent profectu. Item amici auscultant, quia omnes electi, ut ad coelestem patriam reviviscant, verba vitae audire desiderant: « Fac me audire vocem tuam, » id est verbum praedicationis et vox laudis, quantum vales semper audiatur a me. Ad haec sponsa respondit:

« Fuge, dilecte mi, et assimilare capreae, hinnuloque cervorum, super monte aromatum. » Ac si aperte dicat: Quoniam in carne apparens praecepta ac dona vitae mihi deferens, nunc his peractis reverte in sinum Patris: O mi dilecte, « assimilare capreae, hinnuloque cervorum supra montes aromatum, » id est, et hoc mihi sit solatium, quia continua visione 627Cnequeo te cernere, saltem crebra visitatione me consolari memento. Christus similis capreae, propter similitudinem carnis peccati, qui de patriarcharum stirpe velut exterius genitus. Hinnulus propter varietatem virtutum dicitur, qui cum qua carne ad coelos ascendit, cum ipsa versatus est in terris. Montes aromatum, angeli intelliguntur, super quos exaltatus est homo assumptus, sicut Psalmista ait: « Ascendit Deus in jubilatione, et Dominus in voce tubae (Psal. XLVI). » Hinc verum dicit: « Ascendens Christus in altum, captivam duxit captivitatem, dedit dona hominibus (Ephes. IV). » Hinc Lucas ait: « Et Dominus Jesus postquam locutus est eis ascendit in coelum, et sedet a dextris Dei (Marc. XVI). »

Jam enim tandem aliquando Deo favente liber finem 627Dpostulat, quem calcetenus ipso adminiculante recta ut credimus indagine, veraque juxta sensum doctorum, nostrique fragilitatem sensus perduximus, et imperialibus decretis atque Augustalibus, o Caesar Auguste, quanquam pavide parere studuimus. Quem supplex supplicando supplico excellentiae culminis vestri, ut subtili investigatione lustrare nobilitas vestra non pigeat, et summo studio sanctorum doctorum opuscula pariter vestra solertia perlegere studeat. Si enim Theodosius imperator in imperio natus et educatus tantae excellentiae exstitit, ut singularem totius orbis monarchiam obtineret, Prisciani grammatici Romanae eloquentiae decoris librum, 628Adisertitudine mira disertissimum calcetenus teneris digitulis, propriisque articulis describere curaret, atque ex ejus pretio, distractoque commercio, despectis imperialibus dapibus vivere semper consueverat, et tanto studio viguit, ut jugi meditatione et propria descriptione legem Romanam divinitus describeret, et mores Romanorum distortos, mucrone quodammodo legis normam rectitudinis corrigeret, atque a sua duritia coerceret, adeo ut, aliis expletis, angelica salpix nocturna visione, coelitus ei intonuit et per oroma somnii gratulando vocem extulit, dicens: Liber, inquit, legis non recedat de ore tuo, meditare in eo die ac nocte, quanto magis, o mitissime princeps, propriis studiis sanctarum Scripturarum auctoritatem jugi meditatione percurrere, 628Bet altitudinem divinae legis velut quodammodo montem appetere, ut doctrina spirituali instructus, et te sagaciter ad altiora virtutum provehere, et alios ad tramitem rectitudinis provocare valeas. Quod profecto plenius penetrare, et quodammodo montem divinae Scripturae subire, nisi solerti studio et puritate cordis, et pudicitia corporis nequiveris. Scriptura etenim sacra, ut saepe praefatus tractator ait, mons est de quo in nostris cordibus Dominus venit. De quo propheta dicit: « Deus a Libano veniet, et sanctus de monte umbroso et condenso. » Iste mons condensus est per sententias, et umbrosus per allegorias. Sed sciendum quia cum vox in monte sonat, vestimenta lavare praecipimur, et ab omni inquinatione carnis mundare, si ad montem festinemus accedere. 628CScriptum quippe est: « Quia si bestia tetigerit montem, lapidabitur (Hebr. XII). » Bestia tangit montem, quando quis luxuriae aut irrationabilibus motibus deditus, Scripturae sacrae celsitudini appropinquat, et non eam secundum quod debet intelligit, sed irrationabiliter ad suae voluptatis intelligentiam flectit. Sed iste talis absurdus vel sensu piger, si circa hunc montem visus fuerit, atrocissimis sententiis, veluti lapidibus necatur. Ardet enim iste mons, quia sacra Scriptura quem spiritualiter replet, amoris igne succendit. Unde per Moysen dicitur: « In dextera ejus ignea lex (Deut. XXXII). » Quia in electorum mentibus, qui ad dexteram judicis statuendi sunt, flagrant praecepta divina, et charitatis ardore succensa sunt, idcirco Augustale decus, et Francorum 628Dgloria gentis rationabilibus mentibus et castimoniis corporis stude montem istum subire et fervore charitatis succensus legendo penetrare, ut pro merito intelligentiae, et aliorum quos corrigere vestra sagax prudentia studuerit, profecto montes aromatum, id est celsitudines angelorum tandem Domino favente adire, atque eorum suffragiis orationum ad dexteram judicis cum sanctis imperatoribus et consulibus collocari merearis, pariterque a Domino audire: « Euge, serve bone, fidelis, quia super pauca fuisti fidelis, supra multa te constituam, intra in gaudium Domini tui (Matth. XXV). » Amen.

CHAPTER VIII.

« Who giveth me my brother, sucking my breasts? » [Vulg. Quis mihi det te fratrem meum sugentem ubera matris meae, "Who shall give me thee for my brother, sucking the breasts of my mother"] This is the voice of the men of old, desiring the coming of Christ in the flesh, and that he should be born and nourished in the Synagogue, according to the nature of the human condition. « That I may find thee without, and kiss thee. » Within was the beloved, while « in the beginning was the Word »; without, while « the Word was made flesh. » « And kiss thee »: that I may see face to face, and speak mouth to mouth. Of which kiss I think enough has been said at the opening of the book. « And now let no man despise me. » Before the coming of Christ the Church was only within the narrow bounds of Judaea; after 624Athe ascension she was spread abroad in the whole world, and made venerable. Before it had been said, « God is known in Judaea »; but now it is said: « Be thou exalted, O God, above the heavens, and thy glory above all the earth. » It may also be understood otherwise. Christ sucks the breasts of his mother when the Christian people is refreshed with the histories of the ancient law. For they call the Synagogue the mother of the Church, that is of the body of Christ, to whom in the beginning the divine oracles were entrusted; and what in the Old Testament was covered under a figure is found in truth through the Gospel in the New.

« I will take hold of thee, » that is, I will receive thee coming with ready and faithful devotion. « And I will lead thee into my mother's house. » The dispensation of the flesh accomplished, I will lead thee, returning, with joyful eyes into my mother's house, 624Bthat is of the heavenly Jerusalem, which is our mother. « There thou shalt teach me, » that is, thou shalt make me hope for better gifts than I had had in the law. « And I will give thee a cup of spiced wine, » that is, a fervent love made fragrant or adorned with the various pigments of the virtues. « And the new wine of my pomegranates, » that is, the triumph of the glorious holy martyrs, who with most fervent charity do not hesitate to pass over to thee through sword and flames. Otherwise: The Church leads Christ into the house of her mother, the heavenly Jerusalem, when together with all the good things which she has obtained through his grace she also deserves herself to be led thither. And there she is taught, « that she may now see, not in a riddle through mirrors, but face to face, » when martyrdom shall have brought forth its best and sweetest fruits, as it were the new wine of 624Cpomegranates.

« His left hand under my head, and his right hand shall embrace me. » In the left hand are expressed the gifts of Christ's incarnation; in the right, the joys to come of the saints with Christ. Or in the left hand the present life, which the saints place under the head of the mind, because they despise it. In the right hand, the beatitude which the saints, out of exceeding desire, shall embrace. Of this verse there has been fuller and more abundant discussion above.

« Who is this that cometh up from the desert, flowing with delights? » But of this too we have already discoursed; yet because repetition of verses is, as we have often said, a token of steadfastness, let us now touch on it briefly. It is the voice of the Synagogue, or of the prophet, marvelling how the Church had come up so suddenly out of the desert of heathenism into the embraces of the bridegroom: 624Dto whom it is said by the prophet: « For that thou wast forsaken and hated (Isa. LX), » etc. And in the psalm: « They wandered in the wilderness, in a place without water (Psalm. CVI). » Flowing with delights, that is, with the beauty of all gifts. « Leaning upon my beloved. » [Vulg. innixa super dilectum suum, "upon her beloved"] Because all that she has she refers to his grace, being established in him by faith, hope, and charity — whom they believed was to be committed to me alone. « I raised thee up under the apple tree. » The bride answered on behalf of the Synagogue. I, the bridegroom himself, under the tree of the cross freed thee from perpetual death, as I freed the apostles and the rest of the elect out of Judaea. « There was thy mother corrupted, there was she defiled that bore thee. » That is, the greater part of the people, by denying Christ and choosing Barabbas, was reprobated at the cross.

625A« Put me as a seal upon thy heart, and as a seal upon thy arm. » A seal we bind upon the arm or the finger for the remembrance of some matter. By « heart » thought is designated, and by « arm » working. That is: If thou wilt have me as bridegroom, let there be within charity with faith unfeigned, and without devout working; and so shalt thou receive my faith, that thou mayest keep it unspotted. And because through my cross thou hast received a seal, fear not that the treasure of justice which I have conferred upon thee be carried off by any thief, so that both in thought and also in working thou mayest run back to that sign; and as I have so loved thee that I was willing to die for thee, so, on the other hand, let thy saints not refuse death for me. « For love is strong as death, jealousy is hard as hell. » Strong 625Beven unto death is my love toward thee, O Synagogue; but thy jealousy toward me was hard as hell: yet turn thy jealousy into love, and thou shalt be to me bride, and sister, and love. « The lamps thereof are lamps of fire and of flames. » The lamps of love are the hearts of the faithful, in which charity dwells, ever bright and clean with the brightness of charity, and burning with the fire of the Holy Spirit, and shining forth with the orb of the flames of good working.

« Many waters cannot quench charity, neither shall the floods drown it. » By many waters and floods he means the onsets of temptations, which visibly or invisibly assail the souls of the faithful, to which charity does not yield. « If a man should give all the substance of his house for love, they shall despise it as 625Cnothing. » But even as those giving the riches of their whole substance for the greatness of the love of Christ despised it as nothing, that is, the world. But in another manner the sense of the sentence hangs upon what goes before. For when he had said, « many waters » or floods « could not quench charity, » he subjoined: « If he shall give all the substance of his house, » etc., so that there may be understood what the Apostle says: « And if I should distribute all my goods to feed the poor, and if I should deliver my body so that I burn, and have not charity, it profiteth me nothing (I Cor. XI). »

« My sister is very little, and she hath no breasts. What shall we do to our sister in the day when she is to be spoken to? » [Vulg. Soror nostra parva, "Our sister is little"] It designates the first times of the Church newly born out of the nations, since as yet she was both small in number 625Dand less fit to preach the word, as the Apostle says: « I could not speak to you as to spiritual, but as to carnal; as unto little ones in Christ, I gave you milk to drink, not meat (I Cor. III). » As it were the voice of the bridegroom, or of the apostles, had said of her to the Synagogue: « What shall we do to our sister in the day when she is to be spoken to? » As if he plainly said: The Church of the nations is indeed small, nor is she sufficient to undergo the mystery of the word of God. What then seems to thee, O Synagogue, must be done concerning our sister, when she is to be spoken to, that is, to be led into the faith through the mystery of the word? The Synagogue keeping silence, the bridegroom himself, or the apostles, answered what ought to be done.

« If she be a wall, let us build upon her bulwarks 626Aof silver. » As if he said: If she has in her any strong in faith, or notable in talent, or instructed in philosophy, let us add to them bulwarks of silver, that is the knowledge of the divine Scriptures, that they may be able the more easily to protect the weak and the unlearned. « If she be a door, let us join it together with boards of cedar. » That is, if they be simple, yet gaping with zeal for being taught, and, like a door which is opened and shut, understand « a time to keep silence or to speak »: let us set before them the examples of the just of former times, whereby they may the more surely and effectually fulfil the office of teaching. The cedars designate the virtues of the saints, and the boards the breadth of charity. And that they may be able to say with the Apostle: « But God forbid that I should glory, save in the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, by whom 626Bthe world is crucified to me, and I to the world (Gal. VI). » For to these things the Church herself answered:

« I am a wall, and my breasts are as a tower, since I am become in thy presence as one finding peace. » [Vulg. coram eo, "in his presence"] I am compacted of living stones, and made one by the glue of charity, and built upon an immovable foundation; and my doctors are most strong, as a tower, who both know how to nourish little ones and to drive out all evil things; and it has befallen me, from that time in which Christ reconciled me, that peace is found through him.

« The peaceable had a vineyard in that which hath the people; he delivered it to keepers. » [Vulg. quae habet populos, "which hath peoples"] Whereof the Apostle says: « He is our peace, who hath made both one (Ephes. II). » And whereof the prophet: « His empire shall be multiplied, 626Cand of peace there shall be no end (Isa. IX). » The vineyard, that is the Catholic Church, abounding in the fruit of faith, belonged to the peaceable, that is to Christ, who pacified all things in heaven and on earth, in that congregation wherein he possesses many peoples out of the whole world, not in Judaea alone; he delivered it to keepers, but also to prophets or apostles, or to evangelical teachings, or to angelic dignities. « A man bringeth for the fruit thereof a thousand pieces of silver. » That is, Christ weighing out the kingdom of heaven for its holy labors or alms. It may also be understood otherwise: « A man bringeth for the fruit thereof a thousand pieces of silver, » that is, for an eternal recompense. The man of this vineyard brings a thousand pieces of silver, that is, for the acquiring of the heavenly kingdom, which is the fruit of the violet, all the things which he left to the worlds. The number of a thousand is taken for the perfection of him who left all things. Whence also the pieces of silver are taken for all 626Dmoney.

« My vineyard is before me. » It is the voice of the bridegroom, because after the resurrection to come all the saints shall be always with the Lord, as he himself says: « I will, Father, that where I am, they also may be with me (Joan. I). » Otherwise, as if the bridegroom said: Truly, even though I commend thee to other keepers, yet I have thee always in my presence, seeing and rewarding in all things the devotion of thy labor. « A thousand are thine, O peaceable one. » « A thousand are thine » — pieces of silver is to be understood. As though he had said: Let him who for love of me lets go all his own know that he has them kept in peace; signifying the simpler sort in the Church, who lose for the charity of God all their substance, marked with the number of a thousand: « And two hundred to them they keep the fruits 627Athereof. » [Vulg. et ducenti his qui custodiunt fructus ejus, "and two hundred to them that keep the fruits thereof"; the printed lemma lacks qui] That is. the pieces of silver to those who keep the fruits thereof, who are the holy doctors, who let go all the things of the world and cease not to labor in the word of preaching; these are granted with me a twofold reward, which is designated by the number of a hundred. It may also be understood otherwise: For twelve, if they be multiplied by the number of a hundred, make a thousand two hundred. Which number surely befits the apostles or apostolic men. These are rightly called peaceable, that is, the disciples of Christ, by whom holy Church is ruled:

« Thou that dwellest in the gardens, the friends hearken to thee, make me hear thy voice. » For in the gardens dwells the Church, who is already filled with the greenness of hope and of good works. As if he plainly said: Since the discourse must be ended, holding this last farewell heard from me, dwell thou ever 627Bin the gates of the virtues, and know that the friends, that is the angelic spirits and the separated souls of the saints, are considering thee, and rejoice at thy progress. Likewise the friends hearken, because all the elect, that they may come alive again unto the heavenly fatherland, desire to hear the words of life: « Make me hear thy voice, » that is, let the word of preaching and the voice of praise be always heard from thee by me, as far as thou art able. To these things the bride answered:

« Flee away, my beloved, and be like to the roe and the young hart upon the mountain of spices. » [Vulg. super montes aromatum, "upon the mountains of spices" — the plural, which the gloss below itself prints] As if she plainly said: Since, appearing in the flesh and bringing to me the precepts and the gifts of life, now, these being accomplished, return into the bosom of the Father: O my beloved, « be like to the roe and the young hart upon the mountains of spices, » that is: and let this be my solace, that since by continual vision 627CI cannot behold thee, remember at least to console me by frequent visitation. Christ is like the roe, because of the likeness of sinful flesh, who was as it were outwardly begotten of the stock of the patriarchs. He is called a young hart because of the variety of the virtues, who, with the flesh wherewith he ascended into the heavens, with that same dwelt upon earth. By the mountains of spices are understood the angels, above whom the man assumed has been exalted, as the Psalmist says: « God is ascended with jubilation, and the Lord with the sound of the trumpet (Psal. XLVI). » [Vulg. in jubilo] Hence he says truly: « Christ ascending on high led captivity captive, he gave gifts to men (Ephes. IV). » Hence Luke says: « And the Lord Jesus, after he had spoken to them, ascended into heaven, and sitteth on the right hand of God (Marc. XVI). » [Vulg. Mark 16:19 assumptus est in caelum, "was taken up into heaven"]

For now at length, God favoring us, the book demands its 627Dend — the book which, down to the very heel, by his own support, with an investigation upright, as we believe, and true according to the mind of the doctors and to the frailty of our own understanding, we have carried through; and, O Caesar Augustus, we have striven, though fearfully, to obey the imperial and Augustan decrees. And, a suppliant, by supplicating I supplicate it of the summit of your excellency, that it may not irk your nobility to survey it with subtle investigation, and that your skill may with the utmost zeal study to read through likewise the little works of the holy doctors. For if the emperor Theodosius, born and reared in empire, stood forth in such excellence that he obtained the sole monarchy of the whole world, and took care to write out the book of Priscian the grammarian, the ornament of Roman eloquence, 628Amost eloquent with wondrous eloquence, down to the very heel, with tender little fingers and with his own joints, and out of its price and the trafficking of its sale was ever wont to live, despising imperial banquets, and flourished with such zeal that by continual meditation and by his own copying he wrote out the Roman law by divine gift, and, as it were with the sword's point of the law, corrected the distorted morals of the Romans to the rule of rectitude, and restrained them from their hardness — so much so that, when the rest was finished, an angelic trumpet thundered upon him from heaven in a nocturnal vision, and through the vision of a dream, in congratulation, lifted up its voice, saying: Let the book, he says, of the law not depart from thy mouth; meditate therein day and night — how much more, O most gentle prince, [must you] with your own studies run through the authority of the Holy Scriptures with continual meditation, 628Band seek the height of the divine law as it were a mountain, that, instructed in spiritual doctrine, you may be able both sagaciously to advance yourself to the higher things of the virtues, and to summon others to the path of rectitude. Which assuredly you will not be able more fully to penetrate, and in a manner to climb the mountain of Holy Scripture, save by diligent zeal and purity of heart and chastity of body. For Holy Scripture, as the aforesaid expositor often says, is a mountain from which the Lord comes into our hearts. Whereof the prophet says: « God will come from Libanus, and the Holy One from the shady and thick mountain. » [Vet. Lat./LXX at Hab. 3:3; Vulg. Deus ab austro veniet, et Sanctus de monte Pharan, "God will come from the south, and the Holy One from mount Pharan"] That mountain is thick through its sentences, and shady through its allegories. But it must be known that, when the voice sounds upon the mountain, we are commanded to wash our garments, and to cleanse them from every defilement of the flesh, if we would hasten to approach the mountain. 628CFor it is written: « That if a beast touch the mountain, it shall be stoned (Hebr. XII). » A beast touches the mountain when anyone given over to wantonness or to irrational motions draws near to the loftiness of Holy Scripture, and understands it not according to what it ought to be, but irrationally bends it to the understanding of his own pleasure. But such a one, absurd or sluggish of sense, if he be seen about this mountain, is slain by most terrible sentences, as it were by stones. For this mountain burns, because Holy Scripture kindles with the fire of love him whom it spiritually fills. Whence it is said through Moses: « In his right hand a fiery law (Deut. XXXII). » Because in the minds of the elect, who are to be set at the right hand of the judge, the divine precepts burn, and are kindled with the ardor of charity: therefore, O ornament of Augustus and glory 628Dof the Frankish nation, study with reasonable minds and with chastities of body to climb this mountain, and, kindled with the fervor of charity, to penetrate it by reading; that, according to the merit of your understanding, and of the others whom your sagacious prudence shall have striven to correct, you may indeed deserve at length, the Lord favoring you, to approach the mountains of spices, that is the heights of the angels, and by the suffrages of their prayers to be placed at the right hand of the judge with the holy emperors and consuls, and likewise to hear from the Lord: « Well done, good servant, faithful, because thou hast been faithful over a few things, I will set thee over many; enter into the joy of thy Lord (Matth. XXV). » Amen.

On this text

Cruces for this work — where the plate is defective or the reading uncertain →

The Latin is Migne's printing of Enarrationes in Cantica canticorum (PL 115, coll. 551B–628D), by Angelomus of Luxeuil, from the Corpus Corporum transcription. Parenthetical references in small type stand on Migne's page — editorial identifications inherited from the edition he reprinted or supplied by his shop, not necessarily the author's own; the author's own citations run in the prose itself. Every gilt column mark is an address: migne.app/pl/115/551b resolves to the first.

Du bon, à bon marché — carried to its limit: the good, free.
